### [1] [GxP-Agent: Process-DAG Topology for Reliable Clinical Trial Programming with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16890)
*Jaime Yan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GxP-Agent, a multi-agent system using a DAG to encode regulatory processes, achieves 100% accuracy in clinical trial programming where single-shot LLMs completely fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical trial programming under CDISC standards is critical for regulatory submissions but current LLM-based code generation fails catastrophically, with 0% success rate in producing valid subject-level analysis datasets.

Method: Introduces GxP-Agent, a multi-agent system that encodes regulatory process ordering as a directed acyclic graph (DAG), decomposing dataset generation into 15 domain-specific nodes executed by worker agents with pharmaverse skill context, validation gates, and conditional retry.

Result: On CDISC-Bench benchmark, GxP-Agent with Claude Sonnet 4.6 achieved 100% structural match (49/49 variables, 254 correct records) across three runs, compared to 59.2% for best baseline and 0% for single-agent approaches. The DAG enables weaker models like GPT-4.1 to achieve 59.2% match where they score 0% otherwise.

Conclusion: Encoding domain process knowledge as graph topology - rather than relying on LLM reasoning alone - is a key enabler for reliable, GxP-compliant clinical trial programming, demonstrating that proper system architecture can overcome fundamental LLM limitations in critical regulatory tasks.

Abstract: Clinical trial programming -- transforming study protocols into analysis-ready datasets under CDISC standards -- is a bottleneck in regulatory submissions, yet LLM-based code generation fails catastrophically on this task: across 11 single-shot attempts with five frontier models, none produces a valid subject-level analysis dataset. We introduce GxP-Agent, a multi-agent system that encodes regulatory process ordering as a directed acyclic graph (DAG), decomposing monolithic dataset generation into 15 domain-specific nodes executed by worker agents with pharmaverse skill context, validation gates, and conditional retry. On CDISC-Bench, a new execution-based benchmark built from the FDA pilot submission CDISCPilot01 (254 subjects, 49 ground-truth ADSL variables), GxP-Agent with Claude Sonnet 4.6 achieves 100% structural match (49/49 variables, 254 correct records) across three independent runs, compared to 59.2% for the best retrieval-augmented baseline and 0% for all single-agent and flat multi-agent approaches. The DAG topology also enables weaker models: GPT-4.1 achieves 59.2% mean structural match under the same DAG, where it scores 0% under every other architecture. The approach generalizes to ADAE (adverse events; 9-node branching DAG, 55 variables, 1,191 records), achieving 100% structural match on the first attempt. These results demonstrate that encoding domain process knowledge as graph topology -- rather than relying on LLM reasoning alone -- is a key enabler for reliable, GxP-compliant clinical trial programming.

</details>


### [2] [Runtime Governance for Agentic AI: Action-Boundary Control with Trusted Provenance and Fail-Closed Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16891)
*Adam Mazzocchetti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Aegis is a runtime governance system that prevents harmful operational side effects from agentic AI systems by treating model outputs as action proposals that require trusted mediation before tool execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems that can perform tool actions (modify files, send messages, etc.) create safety risks from harmful operational side effects, not just text generation. Prompt-level governance shapes behavior but doesn't create execution boundaries to prevent actual harm.

Method: Aegis introduces a runtime governance system where model outputs are treated as action proposals that go through a trusted decision layer before execution. The system evaluates proposals against active policy state, resolves provenance server-side, fails closed under uncertainty, and uses Senate-style settlement (quorum-based non-unilateral authorization) for selected cases.

Result: Evaluation on a sandbox corpus (5 run families, 42 tasks, 3 conditions, 10 repeats per family, 6,300 rows total) showed: prompt-policy conditioning produced 79 risky comparator-path leakage rows. Across 2,100 Aegis-governed rows, zero governed mock-tool applications and zero governed risky side-effect completions occurred. All 1,832 Aegis-attempted governed rows preserved trusted Aegis-resolved provenance, and all 1,019 Senate-settled rows had quorum and final signed tally evidence.

Conclusion: While not proving general autonomous-agent safety, the results support the claim that runtime action-boundary governance prevented observed risky proposals from becoming governed side effects in the evaluated sandbox corpus.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems request tool actions that can modify files, send messages, launch jobs, or change workflow state. This shifts the safety problem from harmful text generation to harmful operational side effects. Prompt-level governance can shape model behavior, but it does not create an execution boundary. We introduce Aegis, a runtime governance system that treats model outputs as action proposals and mediates them through a trusted decision layer before tool execution. The model proposes; the trusted runtime decides. Aegis evaluates proposals against active policy state, resolves provenance server-side, fails closed under uncertainty, and routes selected cases through Senate-style settlement, a quorum- based non-unilateral authorization path. We evaluate Aegis on a repeated sandbox corpus spanning five run families, 42 tasks, three conditions, and ten repeats per family. Across 6,300 rows, prompt-policy conditioning produced 79 risky comparator-path leakage rows. Across 2,100 Aegis-governed rows, the system recorded zero governed mock-tool applications and zero governed risky side-effect completions. All 1,832 Aegis-attempted governed rows preserved trusted Aegis-resolved provenance, and all 1,019 Senate-settled rows had quorum and final signed tally evidence. These results do not prove general autonomous-agent safety. They support the narrower systems claim that, in this evaluated sandbox corpus, runtime action-boundary governance prevented observed risky proposals from becoming governed side effects.

</details>


### [3] [Memory Is Communication: The Frontier Between Remembering and Signaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17053)
*Yashar Talebirad,Eden Redman,Ali Parsaee,Osmar R. Zaiane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Bounded agents face trade-offs between memory (remembering history) and communication (peer signaling) to achieve task performance, characterized by a "remembering-signaling frontier" where more effective memory reduces needed communication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agents have limited cognitive resources (memory and communication bandwidth) and must decide how to allocate between retaining task-relevant history and communicating with peers to make decisions. Understanding the trade-off between these information resources is crucial for designing efficient multi-agent systems.

Method: Theoretical analysis of achievable memory-message rate pairs under performance thresholds, introducing the "remembering-signaling frontier" concept. Preliminary experiments using referential games to test whether target repetition reduces message length, with plans for further experiments varying memory and message rates across cooperative tasks.

Result: Target repetition in referential games coincided with shorter successful messages, but predictability from hidden cyclic rules did not shorten messages. The framework provides a way to estimate the trade-off frontier between memory and communication resources.

Conclusion: There exists a fundamental trade-off between remembering history and peer signaling for bounded agents - when memory provides larger loss reduction, less peer communication is needed. The remembering-signaling frontier provides a theoretical framework to study this resource allocation problem across cooperative tasks.

Abstract: A bounded agent may obtain information for a decision from its own past, from peers, or from both sources. Retaining task-relevant history can reduce later communication, while a peer message can supply what memory lacks. Under limits on both resources, how should an agent allocate its information budget? Given a fixed task and decision rule, the memory and message rate pairs attaining a performance threshold form an achievable region under specified rules for using history and peer observations. We call its efficient boundary the remembering--signaling frontier. Across conditions where history permits the same maximum reduction in task loss, we hypothesize that a bounded agent will need less peer communication when it obtains a larger loss reduction from history. In preliminary referential games, target repetition coincided with shorter successful messages, while predictability from a hidden cyclic rule did not shorten them. Experiments varying memory and message rates can estimate the frontier and test this prediction across cooperative tasks.

</details>


### [4] [The Price of Thinking: Reasoning Effort as a Model-Specific API Contract](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16956)
*Yeabin Moon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: API buyers get contracts specifying model, reasoning effort, output constraints, service product, prompt, and pricing; explicit high reasoning effort costs more without clear accuracy benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the impact of "reasoning-effort" terms in API contracts on cost and accuracy, since API buyers purchase comprehensive contracts rather than just model names.

Method: Registered paired contrast study comparing Sonnet 5 with explicit high effort vs same model with effort omitted, using 30 AIME 2026 items with 5 calls per item; terminal categories were frozen, items were resampled while retaining repeated calls; included contract census, metadata analysis, and preregistered probes.

Result: Explicit high-effort contracts cost \$0.01031 more per call than omitted contracts; accuracy difference was +0.0133 but not statistically significant (permits gains up to 4.67 percentage points); cost per correct answer was \$0.08665 vs \$0.07662 for high-effort vs omitted contracts.

Conclusion: Explicit high reasoning effort in API contracts increases costs without clear accuracy improvements; contract terms like reasoning effort have real financial implications and documented semantics vary by model/provider; study claims are bounded to specific model, task, and date.

Abstract: API buyers purchase a dated contract, not a model name alone: the contract includes the requested and served model, reasoning-effort term or its omission, output rail, service product, prompt, and price schedule. We study the reasoning-effort term through a registered paired contrast of Sonnet 5 with explicit high effort against the same model with effort omitted, using 30 AIME 2026 items and five calls per item. Every paid attempt was assigned one frozen terminal category, and inference resampled items while retaining their repeated calls. Mean delivered cost was \$0.01031 per call higher under the explicit-high contract than under the omitted contract [+\$0.00204, +\$0.01974]. The corresponding accuracy contrast was +0.0133 [-0.0267, +0.0467]; we did not detect an accuracy difference, and the interval permits a gain of up to 4.67 percentage points that this design cannot rule out. Cost per correct answer was \$0.08665 under the high-effort contract and \$0.07662 under the omitted contract, as registered point estimates. A dated contract census, Models-API metadata, and preregistered raw-response probes further documented model-specific omission semantics, including within a provider; claims remained at documentation grade when raw structure was indeterminate. The request registry, parser, terminal taxonomy, statistical plan, and analysis pipeline were frozen before outcomes were examined; the resulting claims are bounded to the model, task, and collection date studied.

</details>


### [5] [KernelArc: A Multi-Agent Framework for GPU Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17071)
*Joyjit Kundu,Ben Stoffelen,Kaili Wang,Peter Vrancx,Ludovic Denoyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KernelArc is a multi-agent framework for GPU kernel optimization where specialized agents coordinate through shared memory and deterministic benchmarking to produce high-performing implementations across various GPU workloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The central motivation is that shared multi-agent search can broaden exploration and reach stronger solutions within fixed computational budgets, while coordination features' effectiveness varies by kernel type and optimization stage.

Method: A multi-agent framework with strategy-specialized agents running in parallel, coordinating through conclusions-only shared memory, deterministic benchmark guards, and read-only cross-agent state with plateau-triggered drafting.

Result: Achieved first-place rankings on the SOL-ExecBench leaderboard (July 30, 2026) across representative L1, L2, Quantization, and FlashInfer tasks, producing optimized implementations for various GPU operations on NVIDIA H100 and B200 GPUs.

Conclusion: Multi-agent coordination enables broader exploration and better solutions within budget constraints, though specific coordination mechanisms have context-dependent value depending on kernel characteristics and optimization progress.

Abstract: We present KernelArc, a multi-agent framework for autonomous GPU kernel optimization across heterogeneous workloads. Strategy-specialized agents run in parallel and coordinate through conclusions-only shared memory, a deterministic benchmark guard, and read-only cross-agent state with plateau-triggered drafting. We evaluate \kernelarc{} on NVIDIA H100 and B200 GPUs using category-representative SOL-ExecBench workloads. The resulting implementations span custom BF16 GEMM, static cuBLASLt Expert-API configuration tables, fused mixture-of-experts backward, shape-gated decoder-layer fusion, native NVFP4 grouped-query attention, and paged prefill attention. At the public SOL-ExecBench leaderboard snapshot recorded on July~30, 2026, these submissions ranked first on representative L1, L2, Quantization, and FlashInfer tasks. The trajectories support the paper's central motivation: shared multi-agent search can broaden exploration and reach stronger incumbents within a fixed candidate budget, while the value of individual coordination features depends on the kernel and optimization stage.

</details>


### [6] [FedPref: Federated Preference Learning for Structured Radiology Report Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16971)
*Flint Xiaofeng Fan,Cheston Tan,Yew-Soon Ong,Roger Wattenhofer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedPref enables multiple hospitals to collaboratively train a language model for extracting structured JSON from radiology reports using federated learning with preference ranking, without sharing sensitive data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radiology reports contain free-text findings that need structured extraction for downstream analysis, but hospitals have uneven data distribution. Small hospitals lack sufficient local data, and pooling data across institutions is often infeasible due to privacy concerns.

Method: Federated preference learning (FedPref): Frozen public language models propose alternative JSON extractions, local annotations rank them, and hospitals collaboratively train compact Qwen3-8B adapters while sharing only model updates. Uses heterogeneous teacher pool for cross-model contrast.

Result: On development data from six simulated hospitals, FedPref improves client-mean F1 by 2.49 points and worst-site F1 by 9.10 points vs isolated training. On 400-report gold test set, FedPref reaches 68.68 F1 and pooled training achieves 71.67, preserving same performance ordering.

Conclusion: FedPref enables institutions with unequal, unpooled data to benefit from collaboration without sharing reports or annotations, with largest gains for sites with least data.

Abstract: Radiology reports describe findings and locations in free text, but downstream search and analysis require these relations in a fixed schema. Learning this extraction requires labels that are unevenly distributed across institutions: smaller hospitals have less local evidence, and pooling data may be infeasible. We introduce FedPref: frozen public language models propose alternative JSON extractions, local annotations rank them, and sites collaboratively train compact Qwen3-8B adapters while sharing only model updates. A heterogeneous teacher pool provides cross-model contrast when repeated single-model samples collapse. On development data from six simulated hospitals with unequal data volume and disease prevalence, FedPref improves client-mean F1 by 2.49 points and worst-site F1 by 9.10 points compared with training each site in isolation, with the largest gains at the sites holding the least data. Central training on the pooled preference-pair union is 2.66 points higher on client-mean F1. On a locked, 400-report manually validated gold test set, FedPref reaches 68.68 F1 and pooled training 71.67, preserving that same ordering. FedPref thus lets institutions with unequal, unpooled data benefit from collaboration without ever sharing reports or annotations.

</details>


### [7] [Task-Aware Harness Provisioning for LLM Agents in Mission-Critical Infrastructure Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17433)
*Liangtao Lin,Qingang Zhang,Zhaomeng Zhu,Tianwei Zhang,Yonggang Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an approach to optimize harness configurations for LLM agents in mission-critical infrastructure by matching task requirements with harness provisions, introducing a map-guided escalation algorithm that starts with minimal harness and expands only when needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent systems for mission-critical infrastructure often use comprehensive harnesses for all tasks, causing unnecessary resource waste. There's a need to identify optimal harness configurations that match specific task requirements without over-provisioning resources.

Method: 1) Frame harness configuration as a resource-matching problem between task requirements and harness provisions. 2) Classify MCI tasks based on mathematical representation of underlying systems. 3) Rank harness configurations by information type and amount provided. 4) Construct task-to-harness mappings from research literature mining and controlled agent execution. 5) Propose map-guided escalation algorithm that starts with task-specific minimal harness and expands to full provision only after failed self-check.

Result: In liquid cooling systems: improves agent accuracy from 0.652 (full provision) to 0.715; achieves comparable accuracy to Reflexion with 48% fewer tokens. In power grids: full provision remains accuracy-optimal, but map-based provisioning offers lower-cost alternatives. Shows harness provisioning follows domain-dependent accuracy-cost Pareto frontier.

Conclusion: Optimal harness provisioning for LLM agents in mission-critical infrastructure depends on specific domain characteristics and follows a Pareto frontier between accuracy and cost, rather than having a universal optimal solution. The proposed approach enables more efficient resource utilization while maintaining performance.

Abstract: LLM agents have been widely adopted to operate mission-critical infrastructure (MCI). These agents normally rely on a harness that determines what information they can access, which tools they can use, and what actions they can take. Existing systems often expose the same comprehensive harness to every task, which may not be necessary and cause resource wastes. In this paper, we focus on the identification of optimal harness configurations, and view it as a resource-matching problem between what each task requires and what the harness provides. To measure this match, we classify MCI tasks based on the mathematical representation of the underlying system and rank harness configurations by the amount and type of information they provide. We then construct task-to-harness mappings from two sources: mining research literature and measuring controlled agent execution. Leveraging the measured mapping, we propose a new harness provisioning algorithm: map-guided escalation. It begins with a task-specific harness and expands to full provision only after a failed self-check. We evaluate our method in two representative MCI tasks: in liquid cooling, it improves the agent accuracy from 0.652 under full provision to 0.715 and achieves accuracy comparable to Reflexion with 48% fewer tokens; In power grids, full provision remains accuracy-optimal, while map-based provisioning offers lower-cost alternatives. These findings show that harness provisioning follows a domain-dependent accuracy-cost Pareto frontier rather than a universal optimum.

</details>


### [8] [The Problem Is the Problem: Towards Scalable Mathematical Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16977)
*Zeyu Zheng,Shengtong Zhang,Jeremy Avigad,Prasad Tetali,Sean Welleck*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FAR (Find, Attempt, and Recommend) is a literature-to-review cascade system that automates the search for mathematical problems and filters results for human review, enabling more efficient AI-assisted mathematical discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI-for-math workflows concentrate human effort at problem selection and final review stages, creating bottlenecks. Frontier-model reasoning and expert mathematical review are scarce resources that need better allocation for efficient AI-assisted mathematical discovery.

Method: The FAR system searches broad literature corpora for candidate problems based on human-provided research directions, then filters them through multiple stages: extracting conjectures/open problems, assessing well-posedness, applying automated reasoning, and triaging results for expert review.

Result: In a combinatorics pilot: started from 5,245 papers, identified 6,453 candidate conjectures, filtered to 4,717 well-posed open conjectures, surfaced 598 potential resolutions, and selected 77 for expert review. The system discovered results on conjectures by Davies-Jenssen-Perkins-Roberts, Erdős-Straus, Ikenmeyer-Pak-Panova, and Lund-Saraf-Wolf.

Conclusion: The FAR system demonstrates an effective new human-AI collaboration paradigm that addresses bottlenecks in mathematical research by automating problem discovery and filtering, allowing experts to focus on high-value review tasks.

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly capable of contributing to mathematical research. In research practice, frontier-model reasoning is a limited resource, and expert mathematical review is even more sharply constrained. Allocating these scarce resources well is therefore central to making AI-assisted mathematical discovery efficient. In most current AI-for-math workflows, human effort is concentrated at the beginning and end, in selecting suitable research problems and later reviewing the resulting artifacts. These two stages are becoming bottlenecks for research-level mathematics. We address them by proposing a new human-AI discovery paradigm. The human input is no longer a single problem selected in advance, but a research direction in which the experts have interest and expertise. The system then searches a broad literature corpus for candidate problems in that direction. Inspired by search and recommender systems, we build Find, Attempt, and Recommend (FAR), a literature-to-review cascade that automates the search for suitable problems and focuses human attention on artifacts that have passed several stages of filtering. In a combinatorics pilot, the pipeline starts from 5,245 combinatorics papers, recovers 6,453 candidate conjectures or open problems, and filters them to 4,717 apparently well-posed and still-open conjectures. Subsequent reasoning and automated triage stages surface 598 potential resolutions and select 77 items for author-team review. Among them, we identify many interesting discoveries, including results on conjectures and questions of Davies--Jenssen--Perkins--Roberts, Erdős--Straus, Ikenmeyer--Pak--Panova, and Lund--Saraf--Wolf. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of this new mode of human-AI collaboration for mathematical discovery.

</details>


### [9] [AutoResearch: Insight In, Hallucination Out](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17906)
*Yiming Ren,Xiang Liu,Qumeng Sun,Xiao Zhang,Jiahao Li,Haoyang Zhang,Junjie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoResearch is a two-stage autonomous research system that integrates idea generation with rigorous execution to ensure scientific grounding, reducing hallucinations and improving reliability compared to other autonomous systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While autonomous research systems can execute long workflows, automation alone doesn't guarantee scientific grounding. Current systems lack proper integration between how research ideas are formed and how they're reliably validated through experimentation, potentially leading to unreliable results.

Method: Two-stage system: 1) Idea Generation integrates emerging research signals with domain knowledge, identifies transferable insights, and uses multi-model generation with cross-review to create testable research plans. 2) Idea Execution uses coordinated agents to decompose plans into experiments, iteratively implement/diagnose them, and employ independent evidence-based review before accepting conclusions.

Result: Across cross-modal retrieval, systems optimization, and benchmark-driven ML settings, AutoResearch turns ideas into measurable progress, detects/corrects unreliable results, and makes evidence-conditioned decisions. On RSICD benchmark, improved mean Recall from 32.84 to 34.69 while having only 5 audit-confirmed issues vs 11-27 for other systems.

Conclusion: AutoResearch demonstrates a research process where insights are grounded before experimentation and conclusions are grounded before acceptance ("Insight In, Hallucination Out"), creating more reliable autonomous research systems through integrated idea generation and rigorous validation.

Abstract: Autonomous research systems are increasingly capable of executing long research workflows, yet automation alone does not ensure that the resulting process remains scientifically grounded. We introduce AutoResearch, a two-stage system that connects Idea Generation with Idea Execution to address both how research ideas are formed and how they are reliably established through experimentation. In Idea Generation, AutoResearch continuously integrates emerging research signals with accumulated domain knowledge, identifies transferable mechanistic insights, and uses multi-model generation and cross-review to produce grounded, testable research plans. In Idea Execution, coordinated agents decompose these plans into experiments, iteratively implement and diagnose them, and employ independent evidence-based review before accepting research conclusions. Across representative settings in cross-modal retrieval, systems optimization, and benchmark-driven machine learning, AutoResearch turns generated ideas into measurable progress, detects and corrects unreliable experimental results, and makes evidence-conditioned decisions to continue, revise, or terminate research directions. For example, on RSICD benchmark, an AutoResearch-generated idea improves mean Recall from 32.84 to 34.69, while recording only 5 audit-confirmed issue events compared with 11-27 for other autonomous research systems. These results demonstrate a research process in which meaningful insight is grounded before experimentation and conclusions are grounded before acceptance: Insight In, Hallucination Out.

</details>


### [10] [SkillEffect: Checked Lowering for Memory-Bounded Agent Tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17007)
*Yinuo Wang,Yiyu Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillEffect is a runtime system that enforces memory bounds when language models generate code for agent skills, preventing memory overflows while maintaining execution authority through checked lowering and audited plugins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When language models generate code for tool interfaces, even semantically correct programs can load entire inputs and exceed memory limits of tool calls, creating resource management challenges for agent skills.

Method: SkillEffect uses a checked-lowering runtime with a recoverable source relation, audited bounded implementation, and registered output postconditions. It features an independent checker that rebuilds proposed lowering from submitted programs and immutable inputs, with relation plugins providing source recognizers, input-fact extractors, bounded-IR constructors, and arena-bound functions.

Result: Across six operator families, bounded access substantially reduces peak memory and improves completion under externally fixed caps. Six plugins instantiate the same contract across five execution patterns (from streaming reduction to bounded-heap Top-k), with the checker accepting all legal configurations and rejecting adversarial proposals.

Conclusion: The SkillEffect architecture successfully enforces heterogeneous registered memory relations at Agent tool dispatch, demonstrating that one checked-lowering system can provide resource control across diverse computations through audited plugins while maintaining a shared trust boundary.

Abstract: Agent Skills can specify procedural and resource obligations for tool use, and language models instantiate them as concrete programs. However, when models turn this guidance into code for existing tool interfaces, even a semantically correct program may load an entire input and exceed the memory available to one tool call. We present SkillEffect, a checked-lowering runtime for computations with a recoverable source relation, an audited bounded implementation, and a registered output postcondition. Before granting execution authority, an independent checker rebuilds each proposed lowering from the submitted program and immutable input. Every relation plugin supplies a source recognizer, input-fact extractor, bounded-IR constructor, arena-bound function, and postcondition; one common runtime provides checked selection, bounded-VM execution, atomic capacity leasing, and staged publication. Generality in SkillEffect is architectural rather than automatic: each supported computation requires an audited relation plugin, while the dispatch, resource-control, execution, and publication mechanisms are shared across plugins. Across six operator families, bounded access substantially reduces peak memory and improves completion under externally fixed caps. Six plugins instantiate the same contract across five execution patterns, from streaming reduction to bounded-heap Top-k. The XLSX onboarding study and Top-k extension show that a new relation and a new retained-state pattern reuse the same trust boundary, while the checker accepts all evaluated legal configurations and rejects all adversarial proposals. Together, these results show that one checked-lowering architecture can enforce heterogeneous registered memory relations at Agent tool dispatch.

</details>


### [11] [DiSCO: Defending text-to-image generation through distribution-guided contrastive prompt optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17067)
*Tong Zhang,Motasem Alfarra,Carlos Hinojosa,Christos Louizos,Bernard Ghanem*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DiSCO is a zero-shot black-box defense against harmful image generation that uses distribution-guided suffix expansion via beam search to modify prompts, requiring no model access or training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing defenses have limitations: white-box methods can't scale to proprietary models, while black-box prompt rewriting fails at the "benign adversarial" problem where linguistically safe prompts still trigger harmful content due to model's learned data distribution.

Method: DiSCO performs distribution-guided suffix expansion via beam search, optimized through contrastive scoring over safe and unsafe image pools generated by the target model itself, with iterative adaptive feedback until safe content is produced.

Result: DiSCO enhances safety of both undefended and defended models on I2P benchmark under multiple red-teaming attacks, achieving 37.7% and 25.13% ASR reduction respectively, while maintaining semantic fidelity and improving image coherence.

Conclusion: As a black-box, architecture-agnostic module, DiSCO can be readily applied to any text-to-image system without necessitating any changes to the model itself, providing scalable protection against harmful content generation.

Abstract: As text-to-image generative models advance, they raise critical safety concerns, particularly the generation of Not-Safe-For-Work (NSFW) content such as violence and nudity, further exacerbated by red-teaming adversarial attacks. Existing defenses predominantly operate under white-box assumptions, relying on text encoder optimization, weight editing, or inference-time intervention, and fundamentally cannot scale to proprietary models. Black-box alternatives based on LLM prompt rewriting offer broader applicability, yet fail in a critical regime we identify as the \textit{benign adversarial} problem: prompts that are linguistically safe but still trigger harmful generation due to the model's learned data distribution. We propose DiSCO, a zero-shot, strictly black-box defense that operates entirely at the prompt level as a plug-and-play module, requiring no model retraining, fine-tuning, or access to model internals. DiSCO performs distribution-guided suffix expansion via beam search, optimized through contrastive scoring over safe and unsafe image pools generated by the target model itself, with iterative adaptive feedback until safe content is produced. We demonstrate that DiSCO consistently enhances the safety of both undefended and defended models on the I2P benchmark under multiple red-teaming attacks, achieving 37.7% and 25.13% ASR reduction, respectively, while maintaining semantic fidelity and improving image coherence. As a black-box, architecture-agnostic module, DiSCO can be readily applied to any text-to-image system without necessitating any changes to the model itself.

</details>


### [12] [A decodability criterion predicts when hidden-state selection beats majority voting in large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17124)
*Zhixiang wang,Ziliang Hong,Ulas Bagci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CASE (Correctness-Axis SElection) is a dynamic combiner that trains a linear gate on answer-token hidden states to select the best candidate, with a novel "decodability" measure that predicts when hidden-state selection will outperform majority voting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Majority voting for combining LLM answers fails on difficult questions where sampled answers share correlated errors, causing the wrong answer to win and worse performance with more samples. Hidden-state selection shows promise but lacks reliability indicators across models and tasks.

Method: CASE trains a linear gate on the answer-token hidden state and selects the highest-scoring candidate. It introduces "decodability" - a leakage-free measure of how well the gate ranks correct candidates above incorrect ones, which predicts selection performance vs voting.

Result: Decodability predicts accuracy gain of selection over voting with Pearson correlation r=0.75, threshold near AUC=0.60. CASE improves over voting by up to 19 points on medium-difficulty questions and 16.8 points on hard questions across general and medical LLMs.

Conclusion: Decodability provides a practical, measurable criterion for choosing between learned selection and majority voting, depends on aligned knowledge recall rather than model scale, and transfers to unseen domains within 3.8 points accuracy.

Abstract: Combining the answers a large language model (LLM) samples for a question into one decision is a test-time information fusion problem, usually solved by majority voting. Voting is unreliable on difficult questions, where the sampled answers share correlated errors, so the wrong answer can win and drawing more samples makes the decision worse. Selecting a candidate by reading a correctness signal from the model's hidden states is a promising alternative, but its accuracy varies across models and tasks, and no measure indicates when it can be trusted. In this paper, we propose CASE (Correctness-Axis SElection), a dynamic selection combiner that trains a linear gate on the answer-token hidden state and selects the highest-scoring candidate. Its main contribution is decodability, a leakage-free measure of how well the gate ranks a question's correct candidates above its incorrect ones, which predicts whether hidden-state selection will outperform voting. A conventional probe appears accurate only because of question-identity leakage, which vanishes under question-grouped evaluation. On held-out data, decodability predicts the accuracy gain of selection over voting with a Pearson correlation r=0.75 and a decision threshold near AUC=0.60. Across general and medical LLMs, CASE improves over voting by up to 19 points on medium-difficulty questions and 16.8 points on hard questions. Decodability depends on the aligned knowledge a model must recall, not on its scale, and its prediction transfers to an unseen scientific domain within 3.8 points. It thus provides a practical criterion, measurable in advance for a given model and task, for choosing between learned selection and majority voting.

</details>


### [13] [Toward Personal Intelligence Through Cooperative Observation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17128)
*Yashar Talebirad,Osman Jime,Ali Parsaee,Eden Redman,Yongbin Kim,Osmar R. Zaiane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "cooperative observation" - a feedback loop framework for personal AI where the system's usefulness builds user trust, which in turn shapes future access to observation, creating a symbiotic relationship between AI capabilities and user permission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personal AI systems are limited by what they can observe about users, and simply expanding observation doesn't improve assistance due to information compression needs. There's a need for a framework that recognizes the interdependent relationship between AI observation, user trust, and system usefulness.

Method: The authors propose "cooperative observation" as a conceptual framework, describing it as a feedback loop involving: 1) AI building partial models of user's life, 2) user evaluating AI actions, 3) user consent/control shaping future observation. They tested this with Organizm prototype over 6 months in single-subject study.

Result: Preliminary account from Organizm prototype provides initial evidence for the cooperative observation framework. The paper outlines evaluation directions for measuring how observation quality affects personal AI performance and user trust.

Conclusion: Cooperative observation represents a crucial framework for personal intelligence systems, emphasizing that observation capacity isn't just a technical constraint but a cooperative relationship that evolves based on usefulness, trust, and user control.

Abstract: A personal AI system needs a model of the user's goals, constraints, and ongoing commitments to plan and act on their behalf, and the quality of that model is bounded by what the system can observe. Broader observation does not by itself improve assistance because a bounded system must select and compress information for the task at hand. We argue that this observation bottleneck has a cooperative structure: the system builds a partial model of the user's changing life, the user evaluates its actions, and the user's consent and control shape what it can observe next. Useful and inspectable behavior can give users a reason to maintain or expand the observation channel, while failures can lead them to correct, narrow, revoke, or abandon it. We use the term cooperative observation for this feedback loop among usefulness, trust, and future access, and propose it as a framework for personal intelligence. We report a preliminary single-subject account from Organizm, a prototype used over six months, and outline evaluation directions for measuring how observation quality shapes personal AI.

</details>


### [14] [KnowSim: Evaluating Information Calibration in LLM Assistants with User Simulators that Learn](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17150)
*Yoonjoo Lee,Hyoungwook Jin,Tae Soo Kim,Shaoyang Zhang,Philippe Laban,Q. Vera Liao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KNOWSIM is a user simulation framework that models explicit knowledge states with prerequisite relationships to evaluate LLMs on information calibration across different user knowledge levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current user simulators for evaluating LLMs don't model user knowledge explicitly, making them unable to produce realistic interactions across knowledge levels or track how knowledge evolves during interactions.

Method: KNOWSIM maintains explicit knowledge states as graphs of Information Units with prerequisite relationships that evolve under learning theory-based update rules. It computes three metrics from knowledge trajectories: Knowledge Gain, Delivery Calibration, and Cognitive Overload.

Result: KNOWSIM aligns significantly with human judgments (73-74% sign agreement) from 705 human-AI sessions across two domains, outperforming three baseline simulators. When applied to 9 LLMs, it reveals that the best model shifts by user knowledge level, showing aptitude-treatment interactions.

Conclusion: KNOWSIM provides a valid framework for evaluating LLMs on information calibration that captures user knowledge dynamics, enabling better assessment of how LLMs adapt to different user knowledge levels during collaborative tasks.

Abstract: To effectively collaborate with users on knowledge-intensive tasks, Large Language Models (LLMs) must perform information calibration: matching content to a user's evolving understanding and cognitive capacity. Yet user simulators used to evaluate and train LLMs do not explicitly model user knowledge so they neither produce realistic interactions across knowledge levels nor reflect how interactions unfold as that knowledge evolves. To close this gap, we introduce KNOWSIM, an evaluation framework built around a user simulator that maintains explicit knowledge states, represented as a graph of Information Units with prerequisite relationships, that evolve under update rules grounded in learning theory. KNOWSIM computes three metrics (Knowledge Gain, Delivery Calibration, Cognitive Overload) directly from the knowledge state trajectory, reflecting key mechanistic aspects of information calibration. We validate KNOWSIM against 705 human-AI sessions across two domains, stratified by knowledge level: its rankings align significantly with human judgments (73-74% sign agreement), outperforming three baseline simulators. Applied to 9 LLMs, KNOWSIM reveals that the best model shifts by user knowledge level, revealing aptitude-treatment interactions invisible to standard evaluation.

</details>


### [15] [Synthesizing Feature Extractors: An Agentic Approach for Algorithm Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17170)
*Hai Xia,Carlos Ansótegui,Stefan Szeider*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An automated approach uses LLMs in a check-fix-verify loop to synthesize interpretable Python feature extractors for constraint satisfaction problems, outperforming expert-curated methods on multiple combinatorial problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual feature extraction for algorithm selection in constraint satisfaction problems requires deep domain expertise and becomes a bottleneck when new problem classes emerge, creating a need for automated approaches.

Method: Uses Large Language Models in an agentic check-fix-verify loop to synthesize executable Python scripts that generate interpretable, problem-specific feature extractors from MiniZinc models and instances, creating typed graph representations and computing structural properties like graph density, variable clustering, and constraint tightness.

Result: The synthesized feature extractors outperform both expert-curated mzn2feat features (up to 8.3 percentage points test-set accuracy improvement on FLECC) and transformer-based trans2feat variants across three combinatorial problems (vehicle routing, car sequencing, fixed-length error-correcting codes) with a portfolio of five state-of-the-art solvers.

Conclusion: The automated LLM-based approach successfully generates effective, interpretable feature extractors for algorithm selection that outperform manual and transformer-based methods while maintaining inspectability, demonstrating the value of agentic LLM loops for automated feature engineering in constraint satisfaction problems.

Abstract: Algorithm selection for constraint satisfaction problems requires extracting features that capture problem structure. Manually designing feature extractors demands deep domain expertise and quickly becomes a bottleneck when new problem classes appear. We present an automated approach that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) in an agentic check--fix--verify loop to synthesize executable Python scripts that act as interpretable, problem-specific feature extractors. Given a high-level MiniZinc model and an instance, the LLM agent generates code that constructs a typed graph representation and computes structural properties such as graph density, variable clustering, and constraint tightness. We evaluate our approach on three combinatorial problems (vehicle routing, car sequencing, fixed-length error-correcting codes) with a portfolio of five state-of-the-art solvers. The synthesized extractors yield algorithm selectors that consistently outperform both expert-curated mzn2feat features (up to $8.3$ percentage points (pp) test-set accuracy on FLECC) and the best transformer-based trans2feat variants. In the meanwhile, the synthesized feature extractors remain inspectable.

</details>


### [16] [Benchmarking the Benchmarks: Evaluating Automated Safety Benchmarks for Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17183)
*Nyamtulla Shaik,Fengjun Li,Bo Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Existing LLM safety benchmarks are insufficient for evaluating Small Language Models (SLMs) due to high ambiguity in judgments that correlates with prompt complexity and model architecture, revealing a capability-safety confound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small Language Models (SLMs) are increasingly deployed in resource-constrained, privacy-sensitive settings where safety failures pose security and societal risks. However, existing AI safety/security/compliance benchmarks are designed for large language models and may not transfer reliably to SLMs.

Method: Conducted a large-scale assessment of five widely used benchmark suites across 26 open-source SLMs using a unified judging rubric that assigns scores of 0 (harmful), 1 (safe), or 0.5 (ambiguous/irrelevant) to responses.

Result: Across benchmarks, ambiguous judgments dominate and correlate with prompt complexity and model architecture. Ambiguity rate increases with lexical density, output perplexity, and output length, and decreases with lexical sophistication, self-coherence, and reply-prompt similarity. Aggregate mean-score leaderboards are mathematically brittle - model rankings change significantly under reasonable ambiguity treatments.

Conclusion: LLM-centric safety benchmarks are insufficient as standalone evidence for SLM safety assessment due to prevalent ambiguity that creates a capability-safety confound, making current evaluation approaches unreliable for SLMs.

Abstract: Small Language Models (SLMs) are increasingly deployed in resource-constrained, privacy-sensitive settings, where safety and bias failures can cause security and societal risks. However, existing AI safety\slash security\slash compliance benchmarks are designed for large language models that may not transfer reliably to SLMs. We therefore ask: Can these benchmarks effectively and reliably evaluate SLMs? To answer this question, we conduct a large-scale assessment of the effectiveness and robustness of these automated pipelines by evaluating five widely used benchmark suites across 26 open-source SLMs under a unified judging rubric, which assigns a score of 0, 1, or 0.5 to harmful, safe, or ambiguous/irrelevant responses, respectively. Across the benchmarks, ambiguous judgments dominate and correlate with prompt complexity and model architecture, indicating that {\em LLM-centric safety benchmarks are insufficient as standalone evidence for SLM safety assessment}. In general, the ambiguity rate increases with lexical density, output perplexity, and output length and decreases with lexical sophistication, self-coherence, and reply-prompt similarity. This reveals a capability-safety confound that mixes model capability with apparent safety. Since ambiguity is prevalent, aggregate mean-score leaderboards are mathematically brittle: model rankings change significantly under reasonable ambiguity treatments, even when the underlying outputs remain unchanged.

</details>


### [17] [Fool's Gold: Defensive Deception Against Safety-Removal Attacks on Open-Weight Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17202)
*Mark Russinovich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel defense called "Fool's Gold" that protects language models from safety removal attacks by poisoning the payoff - when safety is stripped, most answers to hazardous requests become confident but falsified decoys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety alignment in open-weight language models is easily removable through attacks like "abliteration" that can strip refusal directions in minutes. No existing release-time defense prevents this durably, so the authors argue that what cannot be prevented should be deceived instead.

Method: The defense (decoy hardening/Fool's Gold) concedes the refusal strip and poisons its payoff. When refusal is stripped, answers to hazardous requests become confident, fluent decoys with falsified critical elements. Decoys are trained inside a differentiable simulation of the attack, expressing only in the attacked state, while a refusal pin and benign leash maintain clean-state behavior.

Result: Tested on seven models from five families (9B-122B, dense and MoE). Six models passing the efficacy gate show 51-90% of attacked-state responses are decoys, with 27-84% improvement attributable to defense. All six stay within benign-behavior and capability budgets. On the defended 122B model, 82-86% of answers to CBRNE-adjacent hazards were fatally wrong vs ≤10% undefended. Repeated sampling doesn't restore trust - element-wise consensus at K=64 only reconstructs usable procedures on 8.3-62.5% of prompts vs 58-96% undefended.

Conclusion: The defense effectively creates epistemic uncertainty: without independent ground truth, it's impossible to distinguish falsified answers from correct ones. The approach works against chemical and biological hazards but doesn't address in-context jailbreaks and only protects the initially released defended weights.

Abstract: Safety alignment in open-weight language models is trivially removable: abliteration projects a refusal-mediating direction out of the weights in minutes, and no release-time defense we are aware of prevents it durably. What cannot be prevented can be deceived. Our defense, decoy hardening ("Fool's Gold"), concedes the refusal strip and poisons its payoff: once refusal is stripped, most answers to hazardous operational requests are confident, fluent decoys whose critical elements are falsified. Decoys are trained inside a differentiable simulation of the attack, expressing only in the attacked state; a refusal pin and benign leash hold clean-state behavior to the original. We instantiate it on seven models from five families (9B-122B, dense and mixture-of-experts). On the six models passing our pre-registered efficacy gate, 0.51-0.90 of attacked-state responses to held-out prompts are decoys, +0.27-0.84 attributable to the defense; all six stay within registered benign-behavior and capability budgets; the seventh (smaller) fails the gate (boundary case). Rates replicate on a frozen test split or untouched strata. The claim is epistemic: without independent ground truth, no observation surface we tested separates falsified answers from correct ones - on external red-team benchmarks' CBRNE-adjacent slice, the defended 122B is fatally wrong on 0.82-0.86 of matched-quality answers vs at most 0.10 undefended. Repeated sampling does not restore trust: element-wise consensus at K=64 reconstructs a fully usable procedure on 0.083-0.625 of prompts where the instrument validates, vs 0.58-0.96 undefended, with no label-free way to tell the regimes apart; on the weakest such model the claim is per-draw only. We evaluate chemical and biological hazards; the defense does not address in-context jailbreaks and protects only the initially released defended weights.

</details>


### [18] [Explicit State Elicitation Is Not Enough: A Controlled Audit of Memory-Policy Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17247)
*Yihang Chen,Pin Qian,Su Wang,Chong Peng,Huan Xu,Shuaiting Li,Yiqi Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers developed an audit protocol for personalized AI agents' memory use decisions, finding that structured prompts and explicit state outputs don't significantly improve policy accuracy, and example-level success overstates counterfactual consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized AI agents need to make decisions about whether to use, ignore, update, or query user memory when performing tasks, requiring systematic evaluation methods to understand how intermediate structured outputs affect decision-making.

Method: Developed an empirical audit protocol: 1) audit dataset shortcuts, 2) isolate bundled prompt changes, 3) check if intermediate labels are answer-associated, 4) test decomposed semantic evidence, 5) audit provider-level failures. Used synthetic datasets, TF-IDF diagnostics, and constructed controlled counterfactual sets with matched families and rule-derived reference policies.

Result: Initial synthetic set suggested gains from state-structured prompts, but TF-IDF showed lexical separability. On controlled sets, state definitions improved accuracy, but explicit state-output field didn't significantly improve policy accuracy for Llama-3.3-70B (non-significant) or GPT-OSS-120B (marginal, non-significant). State labels shift predictions but due to deterministic mapping, not faithful mechanisms. Family-level analysis shows rare complete four-way success.

Conclusion: Structured intermediate outputs and explicit state fields provide limited benefits for memory routing decisions; example-level accuracy overestimates true consistency; label conditioning may simulate reasoning without faithful internal mechanisms; protocol reveals fundamental evaluation challenges for personalized agent decision-making.

Abstract: Personalized agents must decide whether retrieved user memory should be used, ignored, updated, or queried before it affects a current task. We use this setting to develop an empirical audit protocol for structured intermediate outputs: first audit dataset shortcuts, then isolate bundled prompt changes, check whether intermediate labels are answer-associated, test decomposed semantic evidence, and audit provider-level execution failures. A 480-example synthetic development set initially suggested large gains from a state-structured prompt bundle, but TF-IDF diagnostics showed lexical separability and no positive standalone Ignore cases. We therefore construct a frozen 160-example controlled counterfactual set with 40 matched four-way families and rule-derived reference policies. On this set, exposing the four state definitions improves accuracy, but an isolated explicit state-output field does not significantly improve policy accuracy for Llama-3.3-70B and gives only a marginal, non-significant gain for GPT-OSS-120B. Supplying benchmark-associated state labels shifts policy predictions, but because those labels deterministically map to policies, this is a label-conditioning diagnostic rather than evidence of a faithful internal mechanism. Family-level and seed-stability analyses further show that example-level accuracy overstates counterfactual consistency: complete four-way family success is rare. An exploratory follow-up that elicits decomposed semantic evidence also fails to improve routing for the cleanly evaluated endpoint; the corresponding GPT-OSS condition was unavailable because of provider-side request validation. We evaluate policy classification only, not downstream responses, tool actions, or memory-store mutation.

</details>


### [19] [Do LLMs Know a Good Hypothesis When They See One? Logit-Based Energy Scoring Outperforms Prompted LLM-as-Judge for Scientific Hypothesis Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17270)
*Swati Rajwal,Sanjay Das,Tirthankar Ghosal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A logit-based energy scoring method using LLMs' intrinsic confidence outperforms prompted ranking for scientific hypothesis evaluation, with the best configuration achieving 53.1% accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for evaluating LLM-generated scientific hypotheses often use LLMs as judges or semantic similarity, which can favor familiar ideas over novel ones. There's a need for better evaluation methods in trustworthy AI-enabled scientific workflows.

Method: Proposed a logit-based energy scoring method that evaluates hypotheses using a language model's intrinsic confidence rather than comparative judgment. Benchmarked seven language models on 1,323 papers across 12 disciplines, pairing each paper with its hypothesis and fifteen incorrect alternatives.

Result: Intrinsic scoring reached 33.0% Hit@1 pooled across both scorers, compared with 16.6% for prompted listwise ranking. The strongest configuration (1-billion-parameter model with logit-based energy scoring) reached 53.1%, though this was the maximum across 14 model-by-scorer combinations selected post hoc.

Conclusion: Intrinsic model confidence shows potential for scientific hypothesis evaluation and motivates future research on confidence-based methods for trustworthy AI-enabled scientific discovery.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for scientific hypothesis generation. However, evaluating generated hypotheses remains a challenge for trustworthy AI-enabled scientific workflows. Existing approaches often use LLMs as judges or rely on semantic similarity, which can favor familiar ideas over novel ones. We propose a logit-based energy scoring method that evaluates hypotheses using a language model's intrinsic confidence rather than comparative judgment. We benchmarked seven language models on 1,323 papers across 12 disciplines. Each paper was paired with its hypothesis and fifteen incorrect alternatives. Intrinsic scoring reached 33.0% Hit@1 pooled across both scorers, compared with 16.6% for prompted listwise ranking. The strongest configuration, a 1-billion-parameter model using logit-based energy scoring, reached 53.1%, though this was the maximum across 14 model-by-scorer combinations selected post hoc. Overall, intrinsic model confidence shows potential for scientific hypothesis evaluation. This study also motivates future research on confidence-based methods for trustworthy AI-enabled scientific discovery.

</details>


### [20] [ASI-Bench: At the Dawn of Artificial Superintelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17271)
*Junwei Zhou,Zhen Sun,Binyu Li,Jiangyu Zhou,Yuexi Pan,Hengyu Wang,Honghe Ren,Xiaohan Jia,Xueyang Zhou,Xiaoyu Cao,Yongchao Chen,Yuanning Feng,Junhao Wu,Cheng Zhang,Sijia Chen,Haoyu Xue,Chengsong You,Huan Wang,Koutian Wu,Peigan Gao,Jiakun Wu,Wenzhe Li,Ergan Shang,Qingyuan Zheng,Jingjing Zhou,Ruixuan Jia,Yan Xu,Hongrui Zhang,Xiao-Han Ma,Zhengxiang Cheng,Yuexing Hao,Liting Mai,Xianglin Ji,Wenjun Zhang,Zhuofan Chen,Yixiao Huang,Chi Wang,Wenyue Hua,Yilun Hao,Yuantao Zhai,Ziyan Zhao,Jingyan Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASI-Bench is the first benchmark that evaluates AI systems' capabilities for innovative exploration and autonomous scientific execution across multiple research domains, with progressively reduced human guidance to test how far AI can proceed independently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI systems are built on learning, compressing, and applying existing human knowledge, while ASI requires moving beyond this to explore the unknown, create new knowledge, and turn ideas into verifiable results. Existing benchmarks test whether AI can produce correct answers based on learned knowledge or complete tasks with human guidance, but don't evaluate autonomous scientific exploration.

Method: Created ASI-Bench with 60 project-level research tasks across 11 scientific domains, progressively reducing methodological guidance (full guidance → method specified only → agent determines method). Built by 40+ experts with 31,000+ human hours, with tasks undergoing expert review, AI-assisted auditing, sandbox execution, and scorer validation.

Result: Testing 18 state-of-the-art agent-model configurations shows average scores drop sharply from 50.91 with full methodological guidance to 29.10 with only method specified, and 26.62 when agents must determine methods themselves. This indicates current systems remain heavily dependent on human guidance and cannot autonomously conduct end-to-end, project-level scientific research.

Conclusion: Current AI systems are far from autonomously conducting scientific research, showing heavy dependence on human guidance. ASI-Bench provides a standardized way to evaluate progress toward artificial superintelligence capabilities and is open for community contributions to accelerate progress toward ASI.

Abstract: Artificial superintelligence (ASI) requires AI to move beyond mastering existing knowledge toward exploring the unknown, creating new knowledge, and turning new ideas into verifiable results. However, the capabilities of today's AI systems are still largely built on learning, compressing, and applying existing human knowledge. Accordingly, existing benchmarks primarily test whether AI can produce correct answers based on learned knowledge, or whether it can complete tasks under extensive human guidance. We therefore introduce ASI-Bench, the first benchmark to jointly evaluate AI systems' capabilities of innovative exploration and autonomous scientific execution across general research domains, and the first to progressively withdraw human methodological guidance within the same research project to test how far AI can proceed on its own. Built by over 40 experts with the cost of 31,000+ human hours, ASI-Bench contains 60 project-level research tasks across 11 scientific domains and progressively reduces methodological guidance to test whether AI can independently select methods, conduct research, and produce verifiable results. All tasks undergo expert review, AI-assisted auditing, sandbox execution, and scorer validation. Across 18 state-of-the-art agent--model configurations, the average score drops from 50.91 with full methodological guidance to 29.10 with only the method specified and 26.62 when agents must determine the method themselves. This sharp decline shows that current systems remain heavily dependent on human guidance and are still far from autonomously conducting end-to-end, project-level scientific research. ASI-Bench is open to the world. We invite researchers and builders everywhere to contribute new tasks, challenge the limits of today's AI, and help accelerate humanity's collective path toward artificial superintelligence at https://asibench.apexin.ai/submit.

</details>


### [21] [DeAR: Decentralized Agentic Reasoning via Capability Grounding and Collaborative Thought Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17282)
*Xing Wei,Changmeng Zheng,XiaoYong Wei,Xiufen Ye,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DeAR is a decentralized agentic reasoning framework that replaces centralized protocols with P2P collaboration, using capability grounding, thought map navigation, and topology updates to handle complex multimodal queries more effectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Centralized agentic reasoning systems have routing bottlenecks and static role allocations that struggle with complex multimodal queries, creating a need for more flexible and scalable decentralized approaches.

Method: DeAR implements three mechanisms: (1) decentralized capability grounding for query-dependent agent specialization, (2) thought map navigation for targeted peer interactions, and (3) topology update for adaptive error correction in peer-to-peer collaboration.

Result: Evaluations across 9 diverse multimodal reasoning and text-based QA benchmarks show DeAR consistently outperforms recent baseline methods, demonstrating improved accuracy in knowledge-intensive reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Decentralized and adaptive collaboration among agents enhances accuracy in knowledge-intensive reasoning tasks compared to centralized approaches, as validated by DeAR's performance across multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Existing agentic reasoning systems typically rely on centralized protocols. This design introduces routing bottlenecks and static role allocations that often fail when handling complex multimodal queries. We propose DeAR (Decentralized Agentic Reasoning), a framework that shifts from central control to autonomous peer-to-peer collaboration. DeAR is built on three mechanisms: (1) decentralized capability grounding for query-dependent agent specialization, (2) thought map navigation for targeted peer interactions, and (3) topology update for adaptive error correction. Evaluations across 9 diverse multimodal reasoning and text-based QA benchmarks indicate that DeAR consistently outperforms recent baseline methods, validating that decentralized and adaptive collaboration among agents enhances accuracy in knowledge-intensive reasoning tasks. The source code will be available at https://open_upon_acceptance.

</details>


### [22] [PlanPO: Group Planning-Aware Policy Optimization for Multi-Turn Agentic LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17289)
*Dayang Liang,Liyuan He,Xuan Feng,Shuxin Li,Bo An,Yunlong Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PlanPO is a reinforcement learning method that introduces coarse-to-fine advantage signals to distinguish between successful trajectories based on efficiency, improving planning abilities in multi-turn interactive tasks without extra training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current group-relative policy optimization methods fail to distinguish advantages among successful trajectories even when they differ substantially in interaction efficiency. They assign identical outcome rewards to both efficient and circuitous successes, causing advantage collapse and severe performance bottlenecks.

Method: PlanPO introduces coarse-to-fine advantage signals that capture relative differences in trajectory-level lengths and turn-level response lengths conditioned on successful trajectories for the same task. This enables agents to learn generalizable and deliberate behaviors spanning interaction planning and textual generation from high-quality rollouts within the group-relative optimization structure.

Result: PlanPO improves over GRPO by 27.2% on average across challenging multi-turn benchmarks (ALFWorld, WebShop, and SciWorld), outperforming recent powerful baselines while incurring negligible additional training cost.

Conclusion: PlanPO is an effective RL method for learning generalizable planning abilities beyond task-specific high-quality behavior patterns, solving the advantage collapse problem in group-relative policy optimization by distinguishing efficient from inefficient successful trajectories.

Abstract: Group-relative policy optimization has emerged as a key paradigm for training agentic large language models (LLMs) on multi-turn interactive tasks. However, most existing variants fail to distinguish advantages among successful trajectories even when these trajectories differ substantially in their interaction efficiency. For instance, circuitous successes are often assigned the identical outcome reward, causing advantage collapse and severe performance bottlenecks. To this end, we propose Group Planning-aware Policy Optimization (PlanPO), a simple yet effective RL method for learning generalizable planning abilities beyond task-specific high-quality behavior patterns. Specifically, PlanPO introduces coarse-to-fine advantage signals, which capture the relative differences in trajectory-level lengths and turn-level response lengths conditioned on successful trajectories sampled for the same task. Within the group-relative optimization structure, this enables agents to actively learn generalizable and deliberate behaviors spanning interaction planning and textual generation from high-quality rollouts, without degenerating into vanilla length minimization. Experimentally, PlanPO improves over GRPO by 27.2\% on average across the challenging multi-turn benchmarks ALFWorld, WebShop, and SciWorld, outperforming recent powerful baselines while incurring negligible additional training cost.

</details>


### [23] [LiveHouse-TS: An Open-world Living Benchmark for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17299)
*Haomin Wen,Ziyu Zhou,Qingxiang Liu,Siru Zhong,Yuxuan Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LiveHouse-TS: First open-world living benchmark infrastructure for Time Series Foundation Models that evaluates models prequentially on real future data, moving from snapshot accuracy to continuous temporal validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing TSFM evaluation protocols rely on static benchmarks with fixed historical test windows, which only provide average performance on fixed history and fail to capture how models behave in real-world continuously evolving environments with seasonal variations, distribution shifts, and unexpected events.

Method: Introduces LiveHouse-TS, an open-world living benchmark infrastructure that evaluates models prequentially on real future data, shifting time series benchmarking from snapshot accuracy to continuous temporal validity.

Result: Extensive streaming evaluations across 11 domains with 17 datasets demonstrate that static rankings undergo dramatic reshuffling under a live protocol, showing significant differences in model robustness and performance stability over time.

Conclusion: Static benchmarks provide incomplete evaluation of TSFMs; continuous temporal evaluation through living benchmarks like LiveHouse-TS reveals important insights about model robustness and ranking stability that static evaluations miss, enabling exploration of long-term scientific questions about model performance in real-world evolving environments.

Abstract: Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) have recently emerged as a highly promising paradigm for cross-domain zero-shot forecasting. However, existing evaluation protocols predominantly rely on static benchmarks with fixed historical test windows. While these benchmarks provide a valuable baseline snapshot, they evaluate an average performance on a fixed history, failing to capture how models behave in continuously evolving real-world environments characterized by seasonal variations, distribution shifts, and unexpected events. To bridge this gap, we introduce LiveHouse-TS, the first open-world living benchmark infrastructure for TSFMs. By evaluating models prequentially on real future data in open-world environments, LiveHouse-TS shifts time series benchmarking from snapshot accuracy to continuous temporal validity. Rather than acting as a one-off leaderboard, our infrastructure serves as a continuous time series infrastructure designed to explore vital, long-term scientific questions: Can model rankings be maintained over the long term? Which models remain genuinely robust under distribution shifts? Extensive streaming evaluations across 11 domains with 17 datasets demonstrate that static rankings undergo a dramatic reshuffling under a live protocol.

</details>


### [24] [SignalReasoner: Assessing the Upper Bound of 3B Models for Signal Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17301)
*Guozheng Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates reinforcement fine-tuning strategies for adapting Qwen2.5-3B-Base to graduate-level signal processing problems, achieving over 3x accuracy improvement through domain-aware training approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs have improved in mathematical reasoning via chain-of-thought fine-tuning and RL, their application to signal processing problems remains under-explored, creating a research gap in domain-specific adaptation.

Method: Two training paradigms were examined: (1) direct RL on WirelessMATHBench-XL with verifiable rewards, and (2) SFT on distilled wireless-domain chain-of-thought corpus followed by domain-specific RL. Three RL algorithms (GRPO, GSPO, GMPO) were benchmarked to compare their effectiveness.

Result: The best model achieved 39.12% overall accuracy on WirelessMATHBench-XL, representing a more than threefold improvement over the untrained Base model (12.37%). The study also assessed whether domain-aware CoT SFT helps RL initialization and compared RL algorithm performance.

Conclusion: Reinforcement fine-tuning strategies can significantly improve LLM performance on signal processing mathematical problems, with domain-aware initialization and specific RL algorithms offering substantial benefits for specialized reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Post-training with supervised chain-of-thought fine-tuning and reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards has substantially improved the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, their application to signal processing problems remains relatively under-explored. This report investigates reinforcement fine-tuning strategies for adapting Qwen2.5-3B-Base to graduate-level signal mathematical problems from WirelessMATHBench-XL, a comprehensive benchmark for mathematical reasoning in this domain. We examine two training paradigms: (i) direct reinforcement learning (RL) on WirelessMATHBench-XL with verifiable rewards; and (ii) supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on a distilled wireless-domain chain-of-thought corpus, followed by the same domain-specific RL stage. Across both paradigms, we benchmark Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO), and Geometric-Mean Policy Optimization (GMPO). We aim to assess whether domain-aware CoT SFT serves as an effective initialization for subsequent RL, and whether GSPO or GMPO offer advantages in stability or accuracy over GRPO for signal reasoning tasks. Our best model achieves an overall accuracy of 39.12\%, representing a more than threefold improvement over the untrained Base model (12.37\%).

</details>


### [25] [Wuying-Browser-Agent: Real-World Centric Fundamental Long-Horizon Browser Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17319)
*AIMAE Team,Tianxiang Chen,Yan Cheng,Zhangye Han,Xiaowei Li,Chang Liu,Cheng Liu,Zhongqiang Ma,Long Peng,Xiaobing Tu,Yinggui Wang,Hongliang Wei,Chen Wu,Daiping Xin,Kunyu Zhou,Pengyang Zhou,Peiyuan Chen,Ziyuan Chen,Yutao Deng,Chunyu Dong,Xiangyu Fu,Yicheng Feng,Ruian He,Haochen Li,Miancan Liu,Zhengqin Liu,Wei Peng,Jinkui Ren,Haoyu Tan,Dong Xiao,Rongkun Xue,Shujian Yang,Xianhang Ye,Ziqi Yuan,Ziyang Yu,Linghan Zhang,Xiantao Zhang,Xuanpu Zhao,Yinan Zhao,Zhenghui Zhao,Bin Zhu,Likai Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Wuying-Browser-Agent is a comprehensive framework that addresses the gap between browser agents' performance on clean demos and real-world deployment through execution alignment, specialized training, improved credit assignment, and robust benchmarking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current browser agents perform well on short, clean demonstrations but struggle in real deployment where they must sustain dozens of decisions, recover from mistakes, and navigate complex UIs. The gap requires alignment at all pipeline levels rather than just scaling.

Method: Four key components: 1) Structured browser harness with stable execution primitives and decision-oriented context management; 2) RUIC-SFT training on recovery trajectories and complex-UI interactions; 3) DAO-GRPO for long-horizon credit assignment using potential-based reward shaping and divergence-aware step weighting; 4) BrowserBench - a 350-task bilingual real-web benchmark.

Result: Wuying-Browser-Agent-27B achieves 80.6% on WebVoyager, 66.7% on Online-Mind2Web, and 65.1% on BrowserBench, establishing new open-source state-of-the-art. The pipeline also transfers beyond browser use with average 73.8 score on Tau2-Bench, Claw-Eval, and BFCL-v4.

Conclusion: Closing the gap between demo performance and real deployment requires comprehensive alignment across execution, supervision, optimization, and evaluation. The unified Wuying-Browser-Agent framework demonstrates this approach works effectively and generalizes beyond browser tasks.

Abstract: Browser agents perform well on short, clean demonstrations, but real deployment is fundamentally different: agents must sustain dozens of decisions on live websites while recovering from mistakes and navigating complex UIs. We argue that closing this gap requires alignment at every level of the pipeline, including execution, supervision, optimization, and evaluation, rather than scale alone. We present Wuying-Browser-Agent, a unified framework that addresses each of these levels. A structured browser harness provides stable execution primitives and decision-oriented context management. Reflection and UI-specialized Curriculum SFT (RUIC-SFT) explicitly trains on recovery trajectories and complex-UI interactions. Divergence-Aware Online GRPO (DAO-GRPO) improves long-horizon credit assignment through potential-based reward shaping and divergence-aware step weighting. Finally, we introduce BrowserBench, a bilingual real-web benchmark of 350 tasks averaging 37.9 steps, because most existing benchmarks are too short to expose long-horizon failure modes. Wuying-Browser-Agent-27B achieves 80.6\% on WebVoyager, 66.7\% on Online-Mind2Web, and 65.1\% on BrowserBench, establishing a new open-source state of the art on browser-use benchmarks.
