The same pipeline also transfers beyond browser use, demonstrating strong general agentic ability and reaching an average score of 73.8 on Tau2-Bench, Claw-Eval, and BFCL-v4.

</details>


### [26] [LLMs for Medical Consultation Are Evaluated Too Late: The Preformulation Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17330)
*Yining Hua,Cyrus Ayubcha,Hongbin Na,Levi Lian,Alon Gorenshtein,Yiftach Barash,Eyal Klang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how LLMs handle initial vague medical concerns vs. clear diagnostic cases, showing that standard evaluation overestimates performance for real-world consultations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of medical LLMs focuses on clear diagnostic scenarios, but real consultations often start with vague, minimized, or misframed patient concerns, creating a "preformulation gap" between evaluation and reality.

Method: Evaluated 3 API models across 4 physician-authored multi-turn vignettes under baseline and entry-to-care instruction conditions (24 transcripts), plus 2 cases using adaptive standardized-patient simulation (12 additional transcripts).

Result: With baseline instructions, models frequently gave self-care advice before patient answers (9/12 cases), while with entry-to-care instructions they produced structured handoff summaries (10/12 cases). The instruction changed behavior but didn't reliably ensure elicitation of decisive facts.

Conclusion: The preformulation gap between clear diagnostic cases and initial vague concerns should be evaluated directly through observable first-contact behavior, not inferred from diagnostic accuracy or final-answer quality alone.

Abstract: Large language models for medical consultation are often evaluated after a clinical problem has already been made clear, although real consultations may begin with a vague, minimized, or misframed concern. We evaluated three API models across four physician-authored, multi-turn vignettes under baseline and entry-to-care instruction conditions, yielding 24 fixed-script transcripts; two cases also used adaptive standardized-patient simulation, yielding 12 transcripts. Self-care or home-management advice before any patient answer appeared in 9 of 12 baseline case-model cells and 0 of 12 instruction cells, while structured handoff summaries appeared in 0 of 12 and 10 of 12 cells, respectively. The instruction changed sequencing and documentation, although it did not reliably ensure elicitation of decisive facts. The preformulation gap should therefore be evaluated directly through observable first-contact behavior rather than inferred from diagnostic accuracy or final-answer quality.

</details>


### [27] [TileMix: Tile-Centric Mixed-Precision Attention for LLM Inference Acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17336)
*Hanzhi Zhang,Qiao Zhang,Qinglei Cao,Heng Fan,Yan Huang,Kewei Sha,Yunhe Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TileMix is a novel attention optimization kernel that dynamically routes attention score tiles between FP16 and INT8 precision within hardware-aligned groups during long-context prefills, improving efficiency while maintaining dense token connectivity without training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context prefill in LLMs suffers from high computational and memory costs due to quadratic self-attention. Existing methods use uniform low-precision or token-level selection but don't optimize spatial precision routing over hardware-aligned score tiles within fused dense attention.

Method: TileMix partitions attention matrices into hardware-aligned score tiles, packs routing decisions into compact bitmasks, and dispatches tile groups through either FP16 or INT8 computation while updating a shared online-softmax state. It uses scalable precision grouping where each routing bit controls multiple adjacent key tiles.

Result: TileMix recovers quality lost under uniform INT8 prefills and improves throughput over FP16 on LongEval, LV-Eval, and A100 benchmarks with LLaMA, Qwen, and Vicuna models. It creates a controllable accuracy-efficiency tradeoff while supporting key features like grouped-query attention and INT8 key/value caches.

Conclusion: TileMix provides an effective tile-centric precision-routing approach that maintains dense token connectivity, requires no training, and enables practical accuracy-efficiency optimization for long-context LLM prefills across various model architectures.

Abstract: Long-context prefill in large language models (LLMs) incurs substantial computation and memory traffic because dense self-attention computes quadratic query-key scores. Existing methods either use a uniform low-precision path or select token interactions, leaving spatial precision routing over hardware-aligned score tiles outside fused dense attention. We introduce TileMix, a tile-centric precision-routing kernel that makes numerical precision an executable spatial decision over score-tile groups within fused dense attention. TileMix partitions the attention matrix into hardware-aligned score tiles, packs routing decisions into compact bitmasks, and dispatches each tile group through FP16 or INT8 score computation while both paths update a shared online-softmax state. Scalable precision grouping lets each routing bit govern multiple adjacent key tiles, preserving hardware-aligned compute tiles and compact metadata at long contexts. By routing all legal tile groups, TileMix preserves dense token connectivity, requires no training, and supports grouped-query attention, variable-length batches, and INT8 key/value caches. Across LongEval, LV-Eval, and A100 prefill benchmarks on LLaMA, Qwen, and Vicuna, TileMix recovers long-context quality lost under uniform INT8 and improves prefill throughput over FP16, yielding a controllable accuracy-efficiency frontier across model families. The implementation is available at https://github.com/HanzhiZhang-Ulrica/TileMix.

</details>


### [28] [LLM-Only PDDL Domain Repair with Open-Weight Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17341)
*Nader Karimi Bavandpour,Pascal Bercher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show promise for automated PDDL model repair but cannot reliably guarantee constraint satisfaction like traditional symbolic methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI planning relies on accurate PDDL models, but these models often contain errors. Automated repair methods exist that use positive/negative test plans, but the paper investigates whether recent open-weight LLMs can perform this repair task effectively using an LLM-only approach.

Method: The authors evaluate recent open-weight large language models on the PDDL model repair task using an LLM-only approach, comparing them against symbolic baselines. They measure performance using metrics like F1 score and test pass rates across different domains.

Result: The best-performing LLM achieved an F1 score of .87 compared to .49 for the symbolic baseline (absolute improvement of .38). However, the LLM-only approach has mean test pass rate of only .82, dropping to .06 on the Thoughtful domain, and even the best setting with test traces reaches only .92.

Conclusion: While LLMs show improved performance on the PDDL model repair task compared to symbolic baselines, current open-weight models cannot reliably guarantee satisfaction of the test constraints needed for truly automated and dependable model repair systems.

Abstract: AI planning is concerned with finding a sequence of actions that achieves a specified goal. It relies on explicit models of the world, commonly represented in the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL). An active line of research investigates how errors in such models can be detected and repaired. For example, users may provide positive test plans that are solutions, and negative test plans that fail during execution. Automated repair methods then modify the PDDL model to satisfy these constraints. In this paper, we evaluate the ability of recent open-weight large language models to perform this repair task using an LLM-only approach. Our experiments show that the symbolic baseline achieves an $F_1$ score of $.49$, while the best-performing LLM reaches $.87$ with high reasoning effort, an absolute improvement of $.38$. However, that setting has a mean test pass rate of only $.82$, falling to $.06$ on the Thoughtful domain; even the best setting that includes the test traces reaches only $.92$. Thus, current open-weight models cannot guarantee satisfaction of the test constraints required for reliable automated model repair.

</details>


### [29] [Cognitive Graph Intelligence for Adaptive and Robust DDoS Attack Detection in Next Generation Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17352)
*Mohammad Arif Hossain,Yeahia Sarker,Md Jafrin Hossain,Most. Humayra Khanom Rime,Nirwan Ansari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Graph-based Generative Adversarial Network (GraphGAN) is proposed for cognitive DDoS detection, addressing class imbalance through adversarial generation of synthetic minority samples and using GCN classifiers on traffic flow graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DDoS attacks threaten network availability and require cognitive detection processes that can handle severe class imbalance (few attack samples vs. many benign ones) and non-stationary conditions in network traffic.

Method: 1. Convert sequential traffic flows into k-nearest neighbor graphs using sliding windows to preserve feature-similarity and temporal dependencies. 2. Use GraphGAN with a generator that learns DDoS attack distributions to synthesize realistic minority samples. 3. Use a GCN-based discriminator to distinguish real from synthetic graph data. 4. Train a separate GCN classifier on the balanced dataset for final detection decisions.

Result: Evaluations on four benchmark datasets show GraphGAN achieves superior accuracy, precision, and recall compared to state-of-the-art approaches, particularly in data-scarce scenarios.

Conclusion: GraphGAN effectively models coordinated attack behaviors and mitigates class imbalance by integrating temporal graph construction, adversarial augmentation, and GCN classification, providing a robust and topology-aware solution for intrusion detection in data-constrained environments.

Abstract: Distributed Denial-of-Service (DDoS) attacks threaten network availability, requiring a cognitive detection process that senses traffic, infers intent, and supports an adaptive response under severe class imbalance and non-stationary conditions. This paper proposes a Graph-based Generative Adversarial Network (GraphGAN) that serves as the cognitive detection engine for this task. GraphGAN captures the relational structure among traffic flows while addressing imbalance through adversarial generation of synthetic samples. Sequential flows are converted into $k$-nearest neighbor graphs using sliding windows to preserve feature-similarity and temporal dependencies among flows. The generator learns the distribution of DDoS attacks to synthesize realistic minority samples, while a Graph Convolutional Network (GCN)-based discriminator distinguishes real from synthetic graph data. A separate GCN classifier, trained on the balanced dataset, performs the final detection decision. Evaluations on four benchmark datasets show that GraphGAN achieves superior accuracy, precision, and recall compared to state-of-the-art approaches, particularly in data-scarce scenarios. By integrating temporal graph construction, adversarial augmentation, and GCN classification, GraphGAN effectively models coordinated attack behaviors and mitigates class imbalance, providing a robust and topology-aware solution for intrusion detection in data-constrained environments.

</details>


### [30] [LEGO-RL: Harness-Native Reinforcement Learning for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17393)
*Yiming Du,Yuxin Jiang,Tao Yuan,Jianbo Dai,Shaowei Wang,Jierun Chen,Chaofan Tao,Xianzhi Yu,Lifeng Shang,Kam-Fai Wong,Xiaohui Li,Haoli Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LEGO-RL bridges native coding-agent harnesses with policy-gradient optimization, improving code generation performance while maintaining high probability correlation between rollout and training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Native coding-agent harnesses are misaligned with policy-gradient training due to environmental crashes, reward hacking, and train-inference discrepancies that corrupt outcome signals.

Method: Three-pillar framework: (1) in-process LLM proxying for faithful optimization with token-level alignment, (2) scalable sandbox orchestration with image caching and defenses against reward hacking, (3) integrated plugin for automated validation/monitoring with Live UI for trajectory diagnostics.

Result: Improved Qwen3.5-35B-A3B performance across three harnesses: OpenHands SDK (64.0% to 70.4%), Claude Code (62.4% to 68.2%), and OpenCode (57.2% to 66.6%) on SWE-bench Verified, while maintaining rollout-training probability correlation above 0.99.

Conclusion: LEGO-RL successfully bridges the gap between native coding-agent harnesses and policy-gradient optimization, enabling reliable training without modifying harness internals while improving performance and maintaining alignment between rollout behavior and policy updates.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for coding agents increasingly relies on long-running agent harnesses to manage tool integration, repository contexts, and execution feedback. However, the native execution environments of these harnesses are inherently misaligned with policy-gradient training: environmental crashes and reward hacking corrupt outcome signals, while train-inference discrepancies decouple rollout behavior from policy updates. To address this, we present LEGO-RL, a framework that bridges native coding-agent harnesses with scalable policy-gradient optimization without modifying their internal control flow. LEGO-RL is built upon three pillars: (1) faithful optimization via in-process LLM proxying that captures raw generation streams for token-level alignment and robust trainer-side log-probability recomputation, even under harness-side compaction or re-serialization; (2) reliable execution via scalable sandbox orchestration featuring image caching and stage-wise defenses to mitigate reward hacking; and (3) observable training through an integrated plugin that automates validation and monitoring, paired with a Live UI for granular trajectory diagnostics. We evaluate LEGO-RL by training the sparse MoE model Qwen3.5-35B-A3B with GSPO across three native coding-agent harnesses. LEGO-RL improves Qwen3.5-35B-A3B across OpenHands SDK (64.0% to 70.4%), Claude Code (62.4% to 68.2%), and OpenCode (57.2% to 66.6%) on SWE-bench Verified, while maintaining a rollout-training probability correlation above 0.99.

</details>


### [31] [Depth Enables Local Entropy: Quadratic Depth Dependence in Deep Variation-Norm ReLU Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17434)
*Tao Jiang,Minbo Gao,Shaowei Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper establishes quadratic depth dependence in minimax risk bounds for Gaussian regression over Parhi-Nowak deep-RBV² networks, showing that Ω(L²w²log w) factors are unavoidable under certain radius conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing lower and upper bounds for Gaussian regression over deep RBV² networks differ by a factor of depth. The paper aims to determine whether the quadratic depth dependence in existing bounds is intrinsic or an artifact of analysis.

Method: Constructs a local packing of functions in the network architecture with log-cardinality Ω(L²w²log w), using bias-corrected bounded-coefficient approximation and balanced amplification techniques. Applies Gaussian Fano method to derive radius-explicit lower bounds.

Result: Shows minimax risk is at least of order L²w²log(w)R²/n under certain conditions, matching up to logarithmic factors with pseudodimension-based upper bounds of Õ(L²w²R²/n). This establishes quadratic dependence on depth as intrinsic.

Conclusion: The quadratic depth dependence in minimax risk bounds for Gaussian regression over deep RBV² networks is unavoidable under explicit radius conditions, revealing a transition to representation-limited behavior at smaller radii.

Abstract: We study Gaussian regression over the explicit vector-valued Parhi--Nowak deep-RBV^2 architecture with depth L, width w, layer-sum variation budget A, and output bound B. For this O(L w^2)-parameterized architecture, the known lower and upper bounds differ by one factor of depth. We construct a local packing showing that the quadratic depth dependence is intrinsic under an explicit sample-size-dependent radius condition. The packing has log-cardinality Omega(L^2 w^2 log w); its codewords lie in an O(lambda) L^2 ball and are pairwise Omega(lambda)-separated. The main ingredients are a bias-corrected bounded-coefficient approximation theorem and balanced amplification: multiplying a depth-D ReLU network by q can be implemented using one constant channel so that every coefficient grows by only q^(1/D). Translation to vector-valued RBV^2 blocks then has layer-sum cost O(D w^2 q^(1/D)). Gaussian Fano yields a radius-explicit lower bound governed by the output, testing, and representation scales. Under A=B=R, sigma proportional to R, and the stated radius condition, this gives minimax risk at least of order L^2 w^2 log(w) R^2/n. A pseudodimension-based finite-net upper bound gives O-tilde(L^2 w^2 R^2/n) for unbounded Gaussian responses. Thus the minimax risk has quadratic polynomial dependence on depth, up to logarithmic factors, and exhibits a transition to representation-limited behavior at smaller radius.

</details>


### [32] [Structure-Internalized Rule Language Model for Faithful Knowledge Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17443)
*Xingrui Zhuo,Jiapu Wang,Manzong Huang,Gongqing Wu,Xindong Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SIRLM addresses LLM reasoning inconsistency on knowledge graphs by generating structural rules that align parametric knowledge with KG evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs show promise for Knowledge Graph Reasoning but suffer from representation inconsistency between KG structural context and parametric knowledge, causing reasoning evidence perception drift that undermines effectiveness and faithfulness.

Method: Proposes Structure-Internalized Rule Language Model (SIRLM) with Structure-Internalized Rule Generator (SIRG) featuring: 1) ICL block with structural relation memory to coordinate knowledge, 2) KG tokenizer with structural invariance learning, 3) neuro-symbolic reasoner with rule-constrained message propagation for faithful execution feedback.

Result: Extensive experiments against 17 SOTA KGR methods on 36 datasets demonstrate significant superiority of SIRLM.

Conclusion: SIRLM successfully addresses LLM reasoning evidence perception drift on KGs by coupling structural rule generation with parametric learning, enabling faithful KG-grounded reasoning that can integrate into standard LLM training paradigms.

Abstract: Knowledge Graph Reasoning (KGR) aims to discover latent facts by leveraging the structural evidence available in KGs, posing a challenge to the structural semantic understanding capability of KGR models. Recent studies have demonstrated that Large Language Models (LLMs) can achieve remarkable progress on KGR tasks via flexible in-context learning. However, the inherent representation inconsistency between KG structural context and LLM parametric knowledge remains inadequately addressed. This limitation prevents LLMs from effectively perceiving reasoning evidence that aligns with KG constraints, which undermines both the effectiveness and faithfulness of reasoning. We refer to this problem as reasoning evidence perception drift of LLMs over KGs. To address this problem, we propose a Structure-Internalized Rule Language Model (SIRLM), which centers on structural rule generation to couple the parametric learning of structural knowledge with the faithfulness evaluation of reasoning logic, enabling LLMs to anchor tightly to KG-grounded evidence. Specifically, we first design a Structure-Internalized Rule Generator (SIRG), which incorporates an in-context learning block augmented with a structural relation memory to coordinate structural and parametric knowledge. Furthermore, we equip SIRG with a KG tokenizer based on structural invariance learning and a neuro-symbolic reasoner based on rule-constrained message propagation. These components provide SIRG with learnable structural representations and faithful rule-execution feedback, respectively. Our SIRLM can be seamlessly integrated into standard LLM training paradigms, such as SFT and GRPO. Extensive experiments against 17 state-of-the-art KGR methods on 36 datasets demonstrate the significant superiority of SIRLM.

</details>


### [33] [SAGE: Self-Evolving Storyboard Skills via Attribution-Guided Rule Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17468)
*Maolin Ran,Xiaoyang Lu,Jiaqi Liu,Jian Wang,Weiwen Liu,Jianghao Lin,Yong Yu,Weinan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE is a framework that automates professional storyboarding by learning directing knowledge from expert demonstrations, evolving rules through attribution, and efficiently routing scenario-specific knowledge without human intervention, achieving near-professional quality and dramatically reducing production time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Professional storyboarding relies on tacit directorial expertise and remains an industrial bottleneck in automated short drama production. Current LLM-based approaches face three key challenges: knowledge acquisition from implicit craft, knowledge refinement without execution feedback attribution, and knowledge injection that exceeds usable context or doesn't scale.

Method: SAGE (Skill with Attribution-Guided Evolution) learns directing knowledge from expert demonstrations by deriving content-independent rules through contrasting training screenplays with expert storyboards. During generation, it records adopted rules per narrative group. Combining these records with localized feedback enables targeted rule updates. Evolved rules form scenario packages with a routing index for efficient, bounded retrieval appropriate to each situation.

Result: On 18 test episodes across three genres, SAGE scored 77.8 on an expert-validated rubric vs. 77.1 for professional directors. Deployed for 14 days on Virtual Film Studio, it produced 1,344 narrative group outputs with 87.2% acceptance without substantive edits and over 83% reduction in authoring time per episode.

Conclusion: SAGE effectively automates storyboarding by addressing key challenges in directing knowledge acquisition, refinement, and injection through attribution-guided evolution and efficient routing, achieving near-professional quality while dramatically reducing production bottlenecks. The PROSE dataset (68 episodes of screenplay-storyboard pairs) is released as a public resource.

Abstract: Storyboards turn screenplays into visual shot plans for automated short drama production. Professional storyboarding relies on tacit directorial expertise and remains an industrial bottleneck. Large language models can automate this step, but methods for supplying directing knowledge face three challenges: (1) Knowledge acquisition: the craft remains implicit in exemplars or must be written manually. (2) Knowledge refinement: authored knowledge is not evaluated against execution outcomes, and opaque generation prevents feedback attribution to the knowledge behind each decision. (3) Knowledge injection: injecting all knowledge exceeds usable context, while manual selection for every narrative group does not scale. We present SAGE (Skill with Attribution-Guided Evolution), a deployed framework that learns, attributes, evolves, and routes directing knowledge from expert demonstrations. SAGE derives rules that are independent of episode content by contrasting each training screenplay with its expert storyboard. During generation, the model records each narrative group's adopted rules. Combining these records with localized feedback enables targeted updates to individual rules. Evolved rules form scenario packages with a routing index, so each group retrieves only a bounded set appropriate to its situation without expert intervention. On 18 test episodes across three genres, SAGE scored 77.8 on a rubric validated by experts, versus 77.1 for professional directors. Deployed for 14 days on Virtual Film Studio, SAGE produced 1,344 narrative group outputs; 87.2 percent were accepted without substantive edits, and the production team recorded over 83 percent less authoring time per episode. We release PROSE, the first public dataset pairing screenplays with storyboards by professional directors across 68 episodes: https://github.com/creDreams/PROSE.

</details>


### [34] [When AI Designs AI: Innovation or Imitation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17471)
*Yikang Yang,Zhengxin Yang,Luzhou Peng,Minghao Luo,Yanqi Kan,Wanling Gao,Jianfeng Zhan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current LLM agents can occasionally match human SOTA performance but mostly recombine existing human algorithmic designs rather than creating fundamentally new approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how well LLM agent-designed methods perform compared to human-designed methods, and how different their algorithmic designs are from human approaches.

Method: 1) Derive task-specific algorithmic design spaces from human-designed methods, 2) Map both human- and agent-designed methods into these spaces, 3) Quantify algorithmic differences at module level, 4) Evaluate widely used LLM agents on representative open-ended AI tasks across multiple modalities.

Result: 1) Agents occasionally match/surpass human SOTA (10/72 configurations), but success doesn't generalize across tasks/agents, 2) 96.8% of agent-designed methods fall within human-derived design spaces, 3) Nearly half exactly match existing human algorithmic designs, 4) Agents largely recombine algorithmic choices from human-designed methods.

Conclusion: Current LLM agents can achieve competitive performance but their algorithmic creativity is limited - they predominantly reuse and recombine existing human algorithmic designs rather than discovering fundamentally new approaches.

Abstract: Recent advances in LLM agents have made them increasingly capable of designing methods for complex AI tasks. This raises two central questions about agent-designed methods relative to human-designed methods: how well they perform, and how different their algorithmic designs are. To study these questions, this paper introduces an analysis that derives task-specific algorithmic design spaces from human-designed methods, maps both human- and agent-designed methods into these spaces, and quantifies their algorithmic differences at the module level. Widely used LLM agents are evaluated on a suite of representative, open-ended AI tasks spanning multiple modalities, and the methods they design are analyzed in terms of both task performance and algorithmic differences from human-designed methods. Experimental results show that current agents can occasionally match or surpass human state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance (10/72 configurations), but such success does not generalize reliably across tasks or agents. Moreover, 96.8% of agent-designed methods fall within human-derived algorithmic design spaces, largely recombining algorithmic choices found in human-designed methods, while nearly half exactly match an existing human algorithmic design. Taken together, these findings suggest that although current agents can occasionally match or surpass human SOTA performance, their algorithmic designs remain within human-derived algorithmic design spaces, reflecting the reuse and recombination of algorithmic choices.

</details>


### [35] [Towards Better Agents for Multi-Turn User Interaction: The Next User Turn Is More Than Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17499)
*Yiwen Zhao,Zhihao Wen,Yuchen Mao,Mingxuan Jiang,Yihao Hu,Pan Wang,Xin Zhang,Wei Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FACA (Feedback-Aware Credit Assignment) improves multi-turn tool agents by using next-turn user reactions as local feedback signals alongside terminal rewards, showing significant performance gains across multiple domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current interactive reinforcement learning treats multi-turn dialogues as single rollout to terminal reward, failing to properly credit individual dialogue segments and ignoring valuable feedback from user reactions in next turns.

Method: Proposes FACA which aligns each agent reaction with corresponding dialogue segment, computes locally normalized reaction advantage, and combines it with verified terminal outcome advantage without requiring extra critic or additional rollout.

Result: FACA improves nine-domain τ-family average by 5.91% (8B) and 10.22% (14B) over outcome-only baseline. Gains particularly strong in Telecom domain; randomizing reaction polarity removes Telecom gains. Same ordering holds zero-shot on Pare-Bench and Co-Gym benchmarks.

Conclusion: Next-turn user reactions provide actionable local credit that improves multi-turn user-interacting agents when properly incorporated through feedback-aware credit assignment, demonstrating the value of intermediate user feedback beyond terminal outcomes.

Abstract: User-facing tool agents must coordinate dialogue and tool use as user goals unfold over multiple turns. Yet interactive reinforcement learning typically reduces each rollout to a terminal reward, assigning the same credit to effective elicitation, errors, and later repair. The next user turn is more than context: it also provides noisy, temporally local evidence about the preceding user-to-user segment. We introduce \textbf{F}eedback-\textbf{A}ware \textbf{C}redit \textbf{A}ssignment (\textsc{FACA}), which aligns each reaction with that segment, derives a locally normalized reaction advantage, and adds it to verified terminal outcome advantage without an extra critic or rollout. Against an outcome-only Interactive GRPO control matched in simulator, visible dialogue, initialization, rollout, and optimization, \textsc{FACA} improves the nine-domain $τ$-family average across three independently trained runs by 5.91 and 10.22 percentage points at 8B and 14B, respectively. Gains concentrate in Telecom; at 8B, randomizing reaction polarity removes the Telecom gain. The same ordering holds zero-shot on Pare-Bench and Co-Gym. These results demonstrate that next-turn user reactions provide actionable local credit for improving multi-turn user-interacting agents.

</details>


### [36] [SGHA: Evidence-Grounded Research Problem Discovery with Local Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17501)
*Sarvesh Gharat,Junpei Komiyama*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SGHA is a fully automated research-problem discovery system that uses local LLMs to analyze scientific literature, detect structural gaps, and generate traceable research problems without relying on proprietary frontier models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI scientists rely on opaque proprietary frontier models with black-box knowledge, creating issues with auditability, validity verification, hallucinations, biases, and confidentiality/privacy concerns when transmitting research materials to external APIs.

Method: SGHA structures scientific literature into evidence-linked paper objects and typed evidence graphs, detects unresolved structural patterns across papers, screens candidate gaps before formulation, and produces traceable research-problem families with assumptions, objectives, success criteria, and remaining ambiguities - all using a locally served open-weight 9B language model.

Result: SGHA outperforms AI Scientist-v2 in five machine-learning domains, demonstrating that explicit corpus structure and evidence-constrained reasoning can support promising, inspectable research-problem formulation without relying on frontier models.

Conclusion: The approach shows that local LLMs with structured evidence analysis can overcome the limitations of proprietary frontier models for automated scientific discovery, addressing auditability, bias, and privacy concerns while maintaining effectiveness.

Abstract: Recent efforts toward fully automated AI scientists have demonstrated that language-model agents can generate hypotheses, execute experiments, and draft scientific manuscripts. However, during the early stages of research, when research problems are formulated, these AI scientists often rely heavily on proprietary frontier models. Their proposals are shaped by opaque parametric knowledge and by literature searches conditioned on the proposals themselves. Such knowledge is effectively a black box, and this dependence makes the evidential basis and validity of generated research problems difficult to audit and leaves the process vulnerable to model-specific hallucinations and biases. Furthermore, if proprietary research materials are transmitted to external APIs, the use of these models creates confidentiality, privacy, and data-governance concerns.
  We introduce the Structural Gap Hypothesis Agent (SGHA), a fully automated, corpus-first research-problem discovery system that runs entirely on a local LLM. SGHA structures a scientific literature corpus into evidence-linked paper objects and a typed evidence graph, detects unresolved structural patterns across papers, screens candidate gaps before formulation, and produces traceable research-problem families. In particular, it is able to output assumptions, objectives, success criteria, and remaining ambiguities. All LLM-based components of SGHA are executed using a locally served open-weight 9B language model, without requiring proprietary frontier-model APIs. We compare SGHA with the AI Scientist-v2 idea formulation module in five machine-learning domains. Our results suggest that explicit corpus structure and evidence-constrained reasoning can support promising, inspectable research-problem formulation without relying on frontier models during generation or verification.

</details>


### [37] [Agent Lightning v1.0: Towards Harnessed Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17528)
*Zhiyuan He,Siwei Zhang,Zhiwen Zhou,Yuqing Yang,Yu Kang,Yuge Zhang,Luna K. Qiu,Tin Yan Tsui,Jiahang Xu,Chong Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent Lightning v1.0 introduces a lightweight framework for harnessed agentic RL, where the deployment harness participates in training, improving coding agent performance on SWE-bench by 14.6 points with minimal compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern agent systems rely on harnesses for tool management and control flow, but traditional RL approaches don't account for harness involvement during training. The disaggregated architecture connecting agents to RL through LLM proxies introduces unique challenges in training stability and effectiveness.

Method: Proposes harnessed agentic RL paradigm where the deploy-time harness owns environment interaction while trainer observes LLM request-response pairs. Implemented Agent Lightning v1.0 framework (~3,500 lines of code) supporting arbitrary agent harnesses as a testbed for studying training challenges.

Result: With only 6K training examples and modest compute, RL improves Qwen3.5-9B on SWE-bench Verified from 41.8% to 56.4% (14.6-point absolute gain). Framework successfully addresses challenges in retokenization, sample merging, advantage calculation, loss normalization, and backend scheduling.

Conclusion: Harnessed agentic RL represents a fundamental shift from traditional approaches, with the deployment harness playing a critical role in model post-training. Agent Lightning v1.0 provides a practical framework for reproducible research in this paradigm and demonstrates significant performance improvements with minimal resources.

Abstract: Modern agents operate inside agent harnesses that manage tools, context, and control flow, making the harness a critical part of the agent system. Our original Agent Lightning introduced a disaggregated architecture that connects arbitrary agents to RL training through an LLM endpoint proxy, an approach later adopted by frameworks such as verl Uni-Agent, AReaL 2.0, slime, and Polar. We refer to this paradigm as harnessed agentic RL, where the deploy-time harness directly participates in model post-training. Harnessed agentic RL differs fundamentally from traditional agentic RL: the harness, rather than the training engine, owns the environment interaction loop, while the trainer observes only sequences of LLM request-response pairs. This introduces challenges in retokenization, sample merging, advantage calculation, loss normalization, and backend scheduling, which can substantially affect training stability and effectiveness. We present Agent Lightning v1.0, a lightweight framework for harnessed agentic RL implemented in approximately 3,500 lines of code. It supports arbitrary agent harnesses and serves as a practical testbed for studying these challenges. We evaluate it on instruction-following, search, and coding agents, and provide a complete reproducible pipeline for coding-agent RL. Using only 6K training examples and modest compute, RL improves Qwen3.5-9B on SWE-bench Verified from 41.8% to 56.4%, a 14.6-point absolute gain. We release the complete workflow and training scripts to facilitate reproducible research on harnessed agentic RL.

</details>


### [38] [When to Review: Spaced Repetition for Continual Pre-Training of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17530)
*Alankar Atreya,Devesh Batra,Yoages Kumar Mantri,Geremy Bantug,Greig A Cowan,Raad Khraishi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SRT (Spaced Repetition Training) applies cognitive science's spaced repetition principle (SuperMemo-2 algorithm) to continual pre-training of LLMs, adaptively scheduling which historical examples to replay based on forgetting rates rather than using uniform sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continual pre-training needs to acquire new information without catastrophic forgetting of old knowledge. Existing replay methods use fixed old/new mixture ratios and uniform sampling, ignoring that different examples are forgotten at different rates, leading to inefficient review scheduling.

Method: SRT adapts the SuperMemo-2 spaced repetition algorithm from cognitive science for continual learning. It maintains per-example review state, maps perplexity to recall quality, and schedules historical examples based on predicted forgetting curves while leaving model architecture, objective, and optimizer unchanged.

Result: SRT improves stability-plasticity trade-off on temporally separated Wikipedia and code corpora, recovering 5-37 percentage points of old-knowledge accuracy lost by naive continual pre-training across model scales while preserving or improving new-knowledge acquisition. At larger scale, SRT maintains broad benchmark performance that other methods degrade.

Conclusion: Adaptive review scheduling using spaced repetition principles from cognitive science is effective for continual pre-training, and the approach generalizes beyond language to vision and tabular data when paired with appropriate recall signals.

Abstract: Continual pre-training of large language models must acquire new information without erasing old knowledge. Existing replay methods often choose a global old/new mixture and sample uniformly, ignoring that examples differ in how quickly they are forgotten. We formulate continual pre-training as adaptive review scheduling: the training loop should decide not only how much history to replay, but which examples should return at each step. We introduce Spaced Repetition Training (SRT), a continual learning framework inspired by cognitive science, which schedules sample-rehearsal using the SuperMemo-2 (SM-2) algorithm. SRT maintains per-example review state, maps per-example perplexity to a recall-quality signal, and schedules historical examples for retention and new examples for consolidation while leaving the model, objective, and optimizer unchanged. On temporally separated Wikipedia and code corpora, SRT improves the stability-plasticity trade-off, recovering 5 to 37 percentage points of old-knowledge accuracy lost by naive continual pre-training across model scales while preserving or improving new-knowledge acquisition. At larger scale, SRT preserves broad benchmark performance that naive continual pre-training and uniform replay substantially degrade. Experiments with vision and tabular data further suggest that the scheduling principle extends beyond language when paired with an appropriate recall signal.

</details>


### [39] [Quantifying Risk Under Evolving Uncertainty: Belief-Dependent Robustness for Safe Sequential Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17574)
*Deep Kumar Ganguly,Jan Kretinsky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RATTL (Risk-Adversarial Total-Reward Learning) is a framework that ties agent caution to epistemic uncertainty using Bayesian posterior dynamics and Wasserstein ambiguity sets, enabling continuous interpolation between worst-case robustness and risk-neutral optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses how cautious agents should be while learning their environment, particularly focusing on runtime safety for agents (including LLM-based systems) acting under uncertainty. The motivation is to balance exploration and safety by linking caution to the agent's epistemic uncertainty about the environment.

Method: The authors propose RATTL, which maintains a Bayesian posterior over unknown dynamics and plans against a Wasserstein ambiguity set whose radius is a monotone function of that posterior. The method uses duality from Entropic Value-at-Risk to convert risk level choices into ambiguity radius choices. The planning is shown to be well-posed under transience and compactness conditions.

Result: The paper proves a "Safety Sandwich" theorem showing the RATTL value lies between the uninformed robust value and the full-knowledge optimum, with the gap vanishing as the posterior concentrates. In a binary-hazard instance, the criterion reduces to Conditional Value-at-Risk set by posterior entropy. An example shows agents deferring efficient actions until reaching identification thresholds.

Conclusion: RATTL provides a principled framework for balancing safety and performance during learning by making caution adaptive to epistemic uncertainty. The approach enables agents to start cautiously (worst-case robust) and gradually become more risk-neutral as they gain knowledge, ensuring runtime safety while optimizing performance.

Abstract: How cautious should an agent be while it is still learning its environment? We propose RATTL (Risk-Adversarial Total-Reward Learning), which ties caution to epistemic uncertainty: the agent holds a Bayesian posterior over unknown dynamics and plans against a Wasserstein ambiguity set whose radius is a monotone function of that posterior. The radius contracts with evidence, so behaviour interpolates continuously between worst-case robustness and risk-neutral total-reward maximization. The design follows the duality underlying the Entropic Value-at-Risk, which converts the choice of a risk level into the choice of an ambiguity radius. We show the resulting planning problem is well posed under transience and compactness conditions, and prove a Safety Sandwich: the RATTL value lies between the uninformed robust value and the full- knowledge optimum, with a gap that vanishes as the posterior concentrates. In a canonical binary-hazard instance, the induced criterion reduces to Conditional Value-at-Risk at a level set by the posterior entropy. A worked example shows the agent deferring the efficient action until a sharp identification threshold. RATTL targets runtime safety for agents, including LLM-based systems, acting under uncertainty.

</details>


### [40] [TRUSS: Towards Task-Reliable and User-Safe Automated Agent Skill Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17588)
*Zhibo Zhang,Zhen Ouyang,Ling Shi,Kailong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRUSS is a framework for generating safe and effective Agent Skills by using evidence-guided evaluation, combining static analysis with controlled execution environment testing to ensure both functionality and safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for automatically generating Agent Skills focus on evaluating artifacts or final task outcomes, but fail to reveal which specific actions equipped agents will perform and what side effects those actions might produce, leaving safety and reliability concerns unresolved.

Method: TRUSS uses a two-stage approach: 1) Static analysis to inspect functional claims against source/domain evidence and evaluate artifacts against nine predefined safety properties, 2) Controlled execution where admitted candidates are loaded by a shadow agent in a Controllable Execution Environment with brokered tools that expose actions to policy enforcement and record execution traces. Failed candidates undergo iterative refinement guided by identified issues.

Result: TRUSS achieved 100% precision and recall in vulnerability detection. Repair reduced attack success rates (GPT 5.5: 38.71%→19.35%, GPT 5.4: 46.45%→29.68%) with zero attack regression. For Skill generation, task effectiveness increased from 17.11% to 52.94%, while Security rate improved from 50.80% to 100.00%.

Conclusion: Execution evidence can expose behavioral failures missed by artifact inspection alone and can guide Skill generation toward jointly verified functional and safety outcomes, demonstrating that combining static and dynamic analysis significantly improves both effectiveness and security of automatically generated Agent Skills.

Abstract: Agent Skills package reusable natural language procedures with executable resources, enabling software agents to acquire task specific capabilities without model adaptation. Automatically generating such Skills can improve task performance, yet evaluating a candidate solely from its artifact or final task outcome leaves unresolved which actions the equipped agent will perform and which side effects those actions will produce. We present TRUSS, an evidence guided framework for generating functionally effective and safety reliable Agent Skills. TRUSS first inspects functional claims against source and domain evidence while evaluating the complete artifact under nine predefined safety properties. Candidates admitted by this static gate are loaded by a shadow agent inside a Controllable Execution Environment, where brokered tools expose requested actions to policy enforcement and record their results as provenance preserving execution traces. Functional failures and property violations are linked back to the responsible Skill content and used to guide iterative refinement.
  We evaluate TRUSS on 168 SkillInject artifacts, 155 SkillSafetyBench cases, and all 187 tasks in SkillGenBench. TRUSS achieves 100.00\% precision and recall in vulnerability detection. Repair reduces attack success from 38.71\% to 19.35\% with GPT 5.5 and from 46.45\% to 29.68\% with GPT 5.4, with zero attack regression. For Skill generation, TRUSS raises task effectiveness from 17.11\% without Skills to 52.94\%, while increasing the benchmark Security rate from 50.80\% to 100.00\%. These results show that execution evidence can expose behavioral failures missed by artifact inspection and can guide Skill generation toward jointly verified functional and safety outcomes.

</details>


### [41] [MoNe: Modular Neural Memory for Efficient Long Context Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17616)
*Wonguk Cho,Kyubyung Chae,Tribhuvanesh Orekondy,Sunghyun Park,Hyoungwoo Park,Jeongho Kim,Arash Behboodi,Kyuwoong Hwang,Sungrack Yun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MoNe is a lightweight neural memory module that attaches to frozen Transformers to enable efficient long-context inference without retraining, achieving O(N) preprocessing and O(1) query costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Transformers struggle with long-context inference due to quadratic attention costs and memory requirements that grow with context length. Methods like In-Context Learning (ICL) degrade sharply with very long contexts and have high computational costs.

Method: MoNe uses a two-phase design: 1) Preprocessing phase that reads context in fixed-size segments using test-time learning of fast-weight neural memory networks with layer-localized gradient updates, 2) Inference phase where memory generates keys and values from query tokens alone without re-reading context tokens.

Result: At 128K tokens, MoNe reduces both compute and peak GPU memory by ~80% compared to ICL with only 6.4% parameter overhead. It generalizes to context lengths beyond the backbone's native window and achieves strong performance on needle-in-a-haystack and word extraction benchmarks from RULER, where ICL degrades sharply.

Conclusion: MoNe provides an efficient solution for long-context inference by decoupling inference cost from context length, enabling O(N) preprocessing and O(1) query cost with constant GPU memory requirements regardless of context length.

Abstract: We present MoNe, a lightweight modular neural memory that attaches to any frozen pretrained Transformer to enable long-context inference without retraining. MoNe reads context in fixed-size segments via test-time learning of fast-weight neural memory networks with layer-localized gradient updates; at inference, the memory generates keys and values from the query tokens alone, with no context tokens re-read. This two-phase design decouples inference cost from context length, achieving $O(N)$ preprocessing and $O(1)$ query cost with peak GPU memory that does not grow with $N$. At 128K tokens, MoNe reduces both compute and peak GPU memory by approximately 80% compared to ICL with only 6.4% parameter overhead. MoNe generalizes to context lengths far beyond the backbone's native window, achieving strong performance on needle-in-a-haystack and word extraction benchmarks from RULER, where ICL degrades sharply.

</details>


### [42] [Validated Adaptation for Aerial Crowd Monitoring at Mass Gathering Scale: A Deployment Protocol, a Severity Law, and a Diagnostic for Label-Free Drone Crowd Counting, Toward the FIFA World Cup 2034 (Saudi Arabia)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17625)
*AlAnoud AllGhayth,AlJawharh AlOtaibi,Jude AlSubaie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A drone-based crowd counting system for mass gatherings like Hajj and World Cup 2034, using label-free adaptation to handle distribution shifts in real-world footage, with validation through controlled experiments and safety evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Saudi Arabia needs robust crowd management for events like Hajj and the 2034 FIFA World Cup. Drone-based counting systems face unique challenges: they must maintain accuracy on unseen footage unlike training data, operate without labels in deployment, and provide early warnings of dangerous crowd inflow before crush formations occur.

Method: Label-free adaptation approach tested through 525 controlled runs, full-resolution corpus study, five falsification ablations, and five-condition safety-interlock evaluation. Uses normalization-driven adaptation (not flow-driven) in a regime favoring physics-informed conservation priors (300-frame clips at 200ms spacing). Includes shift gate analysis and continuity residual examination.

Result: Label-free adaptation recovers 31-49% of shift-induced error across four corruptions and five severities, with strongest method gaining 41.8 MAE over frozen source. Repairs dense-scene undercounting that would otherwise under-report forming crushes. Flux-based risk module fires on real congestion episodes in 2 of 6 full-length clips. Shift magnitude and accuracy damage are rank-independent (Spearman rho=0.20; rho=-0.60 among genuine shifts).

Conclusion: Unconditional adaptation with tail monitoring should be policy. Established a six-point protocol for safe deployment. Continuity residual is invariant to proportional counting errors from domain shift. Normalization-driven adaptation (not flow-driven) is effective even in physics-favorable regimes.

Abstract: Saudi Arabia will host the 2034 FIFA World Cup and already operates crowd management at Hajj scale. Drone-based counting must hold accuracy on footage unlike anything in its training corpus, without labels, and must warn of dangerous inflow before a crush forms. We deliver a validated answer built on 525 controlled runs, a full-resolution corpus study, five falsification ablations, and a five-condition safety-interlock evaluation. Label-free adaptation recovers 31-49% of shift-induced error across four corruptions and five severities, with the strongest method gaining 41.8 MAE over the frozen source (95% CI [34.1, 49.6], p=7.5x10^-10, d=2.52). We establish a severity law separating methods with a constant absolute margin from the one whose margin grows, and a stability budget identifying which configuration is safe to fly. On a full-resolution corpus carrying a genuine +48 MAE aerial gap (source retrained to 14.6 validation MAE, a 34% improvement), adaptation repairs the dense-scene undercounting that would otherwise under-report a forming crush, and the flux-based risk module fires on real congestion episodes in 2 of 6 full-length clips. We localise the recoverable error: in a regime built to favor a physics-informed conservation prior (300-frame clips at 200ms spacing, five times wider than standard), the adaptation signal is normalisation-driven, not flow-driven; the continuity residual is invariant to the proportional counting errors domain shift produces, confirmed by four on/off ablations correlated at r=0.999 and a 40% input corruption moving accuracy by only 0.05 MAE. A label-free shift gate shows shift magnitude and accuracy damage are rank-independent (Spearman rho=0.20; rho=-0.60 among genuine shifts), quantifying the 58% of headroom a magnitude gate forgoes. We establish unconditional adaptation with tail monitoring as policy, closing with a six-point protocol.

</details>


### [43] [Graph Surgery and the Do-Operator: A Precise Correspondence for Acyclic Structural Causal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17634)
*Satpreet Makhija*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper establishes mathematical equivalence between graphical do-operator operations and functional mechanism replacements in structural causal models, showing that both approaches remove exactly the same dependencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to provide precise mathematical foundations for the equivalence between graphical (arrow deletion) and functional (mechanism replacement) descriptions of the do-operator in causal inference, which hasn't been formally established despite their apparent similarity.

Method: The authors use deterministic acyclic structural causal models with finitely many endogenous variables. They define functions like Graph(F) to extract dependencies from mechanism families and Surg(G,T) for graph surgery. They prove equivalence theorems, characterize when graph surgery matches dependency extraction, define intervened models, and analyze sequential interventions and dependency properties.

Result: Main theorem: Graph(Fⁱ) = Surg(Graph(F), Tⁱ), showing that replacing target mechanisms removes exactly the same dependencies as graph surgery. They characterize when this holds for models with unused arrows, define intervened models, show sequential interventions combine properly, and prove outcomes depend only on interventions at actual dependency ancestors.

Conclusion: The paper provides rigorous mathematical foundations for causal interventions, establishing precise conditions under which graphical and functional do-operations are equivalent, which enables proper formal reasoning about causal models, interventions, and their properties in deterministic acyclic settings.

Abstract: The $\operatorname{do}$-operator is described graphically by deleting arrows into its targets and functionally by replacing their mechanisms with constants. To call these operations equivalent is not yet a mathematical statement: one returns a graph and remembers only the targets, whereas the other returns mechanisms and also remembers the imposed values. We make a dependency-level comparison precise for deterministic acyclic structural causal models with finitely many endogenous variables. If $\operatorname{Graph}(F)$ extracts the dependencies of a mechanism family $F$, our main theorem is $\operatorname{Graph}(F^ι)=\operatorname{Surg}(\operatorname{Graph}(F),T_ι)$. Thus replacing target mechanisms removes exactly the dependencies removed by graph surgery. For a model $M=(G,F)$ whose graph may contain unused arrows, we characterize when the same equality holds with $G$ in place of $\operatorname{Graph}(F)$; it holds for every intervention exactly when $G$ records the dependencies of $F$ exactly. We then define the intervened model, characterize its run, show how sequential interventions combine, and prove that an outcome depends only on interventions at its actual dependency ancestors.

</details>


### [44] [Beyond the Trace: Coupling an Interpretable Reasoning-State Readout to Native MoE Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17638)
*Kang Chen,Sihan Zhao,Yixin Cao,Yugang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A method to extract readable process states from reasoning models using a two-level internal readout (J64 and R64) that improves inference quality and enables test-time decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning models only show partial process records in their outputs, leaving valuable latent process state unobservable. There's a need to make this latent state readable and actionable to improve reasoning quality and enable better test-time decisions.

Method: Two-level approach: 1) Distill vocabulary-scale J-space into J64, a 64-axis semantic frame learned from model's reasoning states; 2) Reconstruct J64 from native expert-routing statistics to create R64, a low-overhead proxy. Use these readouts for improved single-branch selection, weighted voting, and cumulative stop-and-resample policies during generation.

Result: J64 improves held-out AUC by 0.096 to 0.135 over baseline; R64 has median per-axis correlation of 0.69 to 0.86 with J64; J64 improves accuracy by 1.1 to 5.9 points over control; R64-weighted voting improves plain majority voting in 7 of 8 settings; Router edits induce predicted reasoning behaviors and shift numerical guessing toward exact symbolic execution.

Conclusion: J64 makes latent process state readable while routing (R64) makes it deployable and actionable, enabling better test-time decisions and improving reasoning quality through various applications including selection, voting, and generation control.

Abstract: What a reasoning model writes is only a partial record of the process that produces it. We introduce a two-level internal readout for mixture-of-experts reasoning. We first distill vocabulary-scale J-space into J64, a 64-axis semantic frame learned from the model's own reasoning states. J64 reveals readable process state that the emitted trace does not show: it separates inference effort from problem-induced strain. It also adds 0.096 to 0.135 held-out AUC over a baseline that reads the same rollout as token occupancy and aggregates it in exactly the same way. We then reconstruct J64 from native expert-routing statistics. The result is R64, a low-overhead proxy: its median per-axis correlation with J64 is 0.69 to 0.86 across three models and two families, and on gpt-oss-20b it preserves 95 to 100% of J64's predictive gain. The readout supports test-time decisions at two temporal resolutions. Over completed candidate sets, J64 and R64 improve single-branch selection, and R64-weighted voting improves plain majority voting in seven of eight settings. During generation, rolling readout windows drive a cumulative stop-and-resample policy whose operating point is fixed on training questions alone. J64 improves accuracy by 1.1 to 5.9 points over a sibling-permuted control, and the routing-only R64 proxy retains 0.9 to 3.2 of those points. Finally, router edits aimed at the mechanism J64 names induce the predicted reasoning behaviors and shift a diagnosed stall from numerical guessing toward exact symbolic execution. Together, J64 makes latent process state readable, while routing makes it deployable and actionable.

</details>


### [45] [LLM-Derived Preference Judgments Are Not Self-Consistent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17644)
*Matthew T. Ford,Francis Bahk,Jingjing Wang,Adam S. Jovine,Tinghan Ye,David B. Shmoys,Peter I. Frazier*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-generated preference judgments show systematic inconsistencies that prevent them from being faithfully summarized by a single utility function.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agents increasingly use LLMs to interpret human preferences via numerical judgments, assuming these judgments are self-consistent and can be summarized by a single utility function. However, the fundamental question of whether LLM preference judgments are actually self-consistent remains unverified.

Method: The authors developed statistical tests and interpretable measures to quantify how far observed LLM responses depart from the best-fitting self-consistent utility function. They conducted experiments with flight, apartment, and hotel examples across six different LLMs, testing consistency properties like whether the difference in willingness-to-pay between items matches the stated payment that makes a person indifferent to exchanging them.

Result: Experiments revealed large persistent inconsistencies in LLM preference judgments across all tested domains and models. The inconsistencies were systematic and significant, suggesting that LLM-derived preference judgments cannot be faithfully summarized by a single utility function.

Conclusion: The assumption that LLM-generated preference judgments are self-consistent and can be represented by a single utility function is fundamentally flawed. This has important implications for systems that rely on such judgments for decision-making, suggesting the need for new approaches or acknowledgment of these limitations.

Abstract: Agents increasingly interpret a person's natural-language preferences by querying an LLM for numerical preference judgments, e.g., by asking how much the person would be willing to pay for an item. A growing body of work estimates a utility function from these judgments and then chooses actions based on their estimated utility. This pipeline assumes the judgments are approximately self-consistent: that a single utility function can reproduce them. But are they? To study this question, we measure the self-consistency of cardinal LLM preference judgments. For example, the difference in stated willingness-to-pay between two items should match the stated payment that makes a person indifferent to exchanging them. We develop statistical tests and interpretable measures of how far observed responses depart from the best-fitting self-consistent utility function. Experiments with flight, apartment, and hotel examples across six LLMs reveal large persistent inconsistencies. This suggests that LLM-derived preference judgments cannot be faithfully summarized by a single utility function.

</details>


### [46] [GraphWake: Group Polarization via Memory-Mediated Polarization Cascade in LLM-Agent Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17665)
*Haoran Bu,Zejian Chen,Litian Zhang,Xi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new threat model called Memory-Mediated Polarization Cascade that manipulates LLM agents' memory systems to induce group polarization through exposure to biased arguments, retention in memory, and triggered reproduction in discussions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-driven agents autonomously forming communities on online platforms create new security risks where attackers could manipulate agents to cause group polarization. Existing manipulation methods (prompt injection or echo chambers) are impractical, so a more feasible threat model is needed.

Method: Proposes GraphWake framework with three components: 1) Stance-support argumentation knowledge graphs to construct knowledge-based arguments; 2) Axiom-oriented triple selection to distill arguments for reliable retention and reproduction; 3) Stance-neutral memory cueing to trigger concurrent retrieval and reproduction in discussions.

Result: Experiments across multiple discussions and memory systems demonstrate that GraphWake substantially increases group polarization, revealing a significant community-level polarization risk in agent-operated social platforms.

Conclusion: The paper identifies a novel security threat where agent memory systems can be exploited as persistence channels and public discussions as propagation channels, creating cascading polarization effects in LLM-agent communities, highlighting serious security implications for autonomous agent platforms.

Abstract: LLM-driven agents can autonomously exchange opinions on online platforms and form communities. Such agent-operated social platforms raise a new security concern: attackers may manipulate agents to induce group polarization. Existing methods manipulate agent prompts or construct echo chambers, both of which are difficult to realize in practice. We therefore formulate a new threat, Memory-Mediated Polarization Cascade, which uses agent memory as a persistence channel and public discussion as a propagation channel. This threat contains three stages. During exposure and memory retention, the attacker exposes a small set of target agents to arguments that reinforce their respective stated stances. The targets' memory systems then process and retain these arguments. During retrieval and reproduction, a shared stance-neutral discussion cues the targets to retrieve and reproduce their respective retained arguments. During iterative propagation, untreated agents influenced by the reproduced arguments restate and spread them. We instantiate this threat in GraphWake with three components: (i) stance-support argumentation knowledge graphs construct knowledge-based arguments; (ii) axiom-oriented triple selection distills them for reliable retention and reproduction; and (iii) stance-neutral memory cueing triggers concurrent retrieval and reproduction, initiating propagation. Experiments across multiple discussions and memory systems show that GraphWake substantially increases group polarization. These findings reveal a community-level polarization risk.

</details>


### [47] [Auditing Self-Evolution in Financial Agents: Capability Gains, Security Drift, and Execution-Interface Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17684)
*Jialong Li,Jialing Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper audits security risks in self-evolving AI agents for financial applications, finding that while they improve utility, they also increase attack exposure and unauthorized state changes, requiring comprehensive security monitoring beyond just accuracy metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-evolving agents can turn experience into reusable skills and workflows, but post-evolution accuracy metrics alone don't reveal whether learned behaviors preserve correct behavior or maintain security. There's a need to audit whether such evolution introduces security vulnerabilities, particularly in sensitive domains like e-banking.

Method: The researchers audit three self-evolving systems (SkillOpt, Agent Workflow Memory/AWM, and ReasoningBank) using simulated e-banking environments with matched benign acquisition trajectories, sealed evaluation endpoints, execution-grounded checks, and independent state replay. They conduct post-hoc sensitivity tests to identify specific vulnerabilities.

Result: SkillOpt increased benign utility from 0.741 to 0.837 but also raised attack exposure and unauthorized financial state changes to 0.685. ReasoningBank improved utility to 0.859 without increasing aggregate attack success rate, though unauthorized state changes remained above static baselines. AWM revealed an evaluation hazard where WebArena text-action envelopes disrupted tool execution, reducing utility from 0.756 to 0.319 unless removed.

Conclusion: Auditing self-evolving financial agents requires tracking regressions, attack-surface contact, unauthorized financial-state changes, and artifact-executor compatibility - not just accuracy metrics. Comprehensive security monitoring is essential as agent evolution can simultaneously improve utility while increasing vulnerabilities.

Abstract: Self-evolving agents turn experience into reusable skills, workflows, or memories, but post-evolution accuracy alone does not show whether learned behavior preserves previously correct behavior or security. We audit SkillOpt, Agent Workflow Memory (AWM), and ReasoningBank in simulated e-banking using matched benign acquisition trajectories, sealed evaluation endpoints, execution-grounded checks, and independent state replay. On Qwen 3.7 Flash, SkillOpt raises benign utility from 0.741 to 0.837 while exposure to injected content rises from 0.820 to 0.943. Conditional attack success after exposure falls from 0.605 to 0.562, yet overall attack success rate (ASR) rises from 0.496 to 0.530 and unauthorized financial state changes rise to 0.685. Across three independently evolved lineages, capability, exposure, and unauthorized-state changes increase in all three, whereas ASR increases in only two. ReasoningBank raises utility to 0.859 without increasing aggregate ASR, although unauthorized state changes remain slightly above Static. AWM reveals a separate evaluation hazard: a literal WebArena text-action envelope disrupts tool execution in our native function-calling executor. In a post-hoc sensitivity test, removing only that envelope restores utility from 0.319 to 0.756, while exposure rises from 0.299 to 0.909 and ASR from 0.195 to 0.575. Auditing self-evolving financial agents therefore requires tracking regressions, attack-surface contact, unauthorized financial-state change, and artifact-executor compatibility, not accuracy alone.

</details>


### [48] [Mixture-of-Expert Blocks Contain Strong Hallucination Detection Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17687)
*Joao Fonseca,Rodrigo Rodrigues,Paolo Romano*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InnerExpert is a novel method for per-token hallucination detection in LLMs that leverages Mixture-of-Experts internal signals (router entropy, expert disagreement, usage patterns) to outperform existing approaches while requiring minimal computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs suffer from generating plausible but false content (hallucinations). Existing detection methods operate at answer or sentence level, but per-token detection is essential for localizing hallucinated spans and enabling fine-grained interventions. MoE architectures offer internal signals not available in dense models that haven't been exploited for this purpose.

Method: InnerExpert combines MoE-specific signals (router entropy, expert disagreement, expert usage patterns) with standard transformer features into per-token feature vectors. These features are classified by a lightweight detector trained using labels from an LLM-as-a-judge pipeline, enabling continuous updates without manual annotation.

Result: InnerExpert outperforms existing methods across five datasets and two MoE architectures, achieving up to 0.91 answer-level and 0.76 token-level AUROC. The method requires only a single forward pass, making it computationally efficient.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that MoE-specific internal signals can be effectively leveraged for per-token hallucination detection. InnerExpert successfully addresses the limitations of answer/sentence-level methods while enabling fine-grained intervention capabilities. The approach offers an efficient, scalable solution for hallucination detection that works across different MoE architectures with minimal computational overhead.

Abstract: Despite their widespread use, Large Language Models (LLMs) remain limited by a fundamental problem: the generation of plausible but false content, known as hallucinations. Most existing detection methods operate at the answer or sentence level, yet per-token detection is essential for localizing hallucinated spans and enabling fine-grained interventions. In this paper, we explore the use of the Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) paradigm to address this gap. In MoE architectures, a single forward pass activates a sparse subset of experts (i.e., distinct feedforward networks per layer) via a routing mechanism, producing internal signals (e.g., router entropy, expert disagreement, and expert usage patterns) that are unavailable in dense architectures and have not been previously exploited for hallucination detection. To this end, we introduce InnerExpert, the first method to leverage these MoE-specific signals for per-token hallucination detection. InnerExpert combines routing-level and standard transformer signals into compact per-token feature vectors, classified by a lightweight detector trained on labels produced by an LLM-as-a-judge pipeline, which enables continuous model updates without manual annotation. Our results show that InnerExpert outperforms existing methods across five datasets and two MoE architectures, achieving up to 0.91 answer-level and 0.76 token-level AUROC, while requiring only a single forward pass.

</details>


### [49] [Accuracy and Robustness of Model Cascades Under Data Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17711)
*Pallavi Mitra,Jai Kushwaha,Felix Biessmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Energy-efficient AI cascades using confidence-based routing can fail under input degradations despite good clean data performance, requiring evaluation of routing reliability under distribution shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prediction cascades reduce AI energy consumption but rely on confidence-based routing that may fail when input quality degrades. The paper investigates how corruptions affect confidence-based deferral behavior in model cascades.

Method: Study confidence-based cascade frameworks for image classification; select pareto-optimal cascade balancing accuracy, routing quality, and energy consumption; analyze routing behavior under static corruptions and sequential perturbations.

Result: Identifies three failure modes: (1) static corruptions break routing signal while large model remains useful, (2) both models degrade so deferral can't recover accuracy, (3) sequential perturbations yield stable but unreliable predictions as deferral suppresses.

Conclusion: Energy-efficient model cascades require evaluation beyond clean accuracy, with explicit attention to routing reliability under distribution shift to ensure robustness.

Abstract: Prediction cascades significantly reduce energy consumption of Artificial Intelligence (AI) models while maintaining high predictive performance. The idea is that easy inputs are routed through a lightweight small model, and difficult uncertain cases are deferred to a larger model. While this design can improve computational efficiency on clean data, its effectiveness depends on the reliability of confidence-based routing. Input degradations, such as static corruptions and sequential perturbations, can shift model confidence and routing decisions. In this paper, we study confidence-based cascade frameworks for image classification and investigate how such degradations affect their confidence-based deferral behavior. We select a model cascade at the pareto-optimum of accuracy, routing quality, and energy consumption that achieves competitive predictive performance with an up to 10-fold decrease in CO$_2$ emissions. We study the behavior of that model cascade under input corruptions and analyze how the cascade's routing decisions change when the input distribution shifts. Our analysis identifies three failure modes. Static corruptions either (1) break the routing signal while the large model remains useful, or (2) degrade both models so deferral no longer recovers accuracy. Sequential perturbations reveal a third mode: predictions stabilize but deferral suppresses, yielding stable but unreliable predictions. These findings demonstrate that energy efficient model cascades require evaluation beyond clean accuracy, with explicit attention to routing reliability under distribution shift.

</details>


### [50] [Beyond Suspicious Steps: Ontological Trust in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17718)
*An He,Yao Wang,Haibin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "ontological trust" for monitoring long-horizon AI agents, proposing RGE as a deterministic online monitor that decomposes trust into Role, Goal, and Evidence components to detect task drift during execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing monitors for long-horizon AI agents focus on local compliance, final-trace verdicts, or generic risk scoring, but fail to detect subtle drift where agents maintain plausible local actions while gradually deviating from authorized tasks across many steps, tools, and observations.

Method: The authors introduce RGE, an online monitor that decomposes ontological trust into Role, Goal, and Evidence components. It uses LLMs only to derive structured task and step representations, while trust-state updates, projections, and intervention decisions are deterministic, producing replayable and auditable trust trajectories.

Result: RGE outperforms adapted rule-, judge-, and shield-style baselines on prefix-paired drift detection across three benchmarks (OSWorld, FinanceBench, EICU-AC). With larger estimator models, it achieves >93% Drift F1 on every benchmark while maintaining benign coverage ≥95.8%. Pseudo-consistency detection depends on external visibility of task completion.

Conclusion: Ontological trust provides a framework for detecting subtle trajectory drift in long-horizon AI agents. RGE's deterministic architecture enables auditable trust trajectories, effectively detecting drift while maintaining high benign coverage, though pseudo-consistency detection remains challenging depending on task visibility.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents increasingly operate across many steps, tools, and observa- tions. In this setting, the relevant oversight question is not only whether each action is locally valid, but whether the evolving trajectory still corresponds to the task the user authorized. Drift can accumulate quietly: an agent may call the right tool with plausible arguments at every step, while its prefix moves toward a broader role, an adjacent objective, or evidence the user never supplied. Existing monitors mostly check local compliance, deliver final-trace verdicts, or score generic risk; they do not directly estimate this prefix-level relation.
