Early clusters predict later engagement (continuing to practice and finishing late sessions, especially persistence, $η^{2}\approx 0.106$; completion $η^{2}\approx 0.021$) but not later unassisted accuracy (correctness on late first-attempts without help; $p_{\mathrm{adj}}\approx 0.093$). Volume rises with some styles, yet volume-only clustering barely matches strategy labels (ARI$=0.064$). A knowledge-tracing model (SAKT) on the seven TOEIC exam sections predicts next correctness only modestly better than a baseline that knows only how hard each section usually is (AUC lift $+0.051$; CI $[+0.045,+0.058]$), and that mastery signal is nearly independent of behavior styles (ARI$=0.007$). Behavioral clustering here describes study styles and engagement, not knowledge gains.

</details>


### [76] [RoBell-RVFL: A Robust Generalized Bell Random Vector Functional Link Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16965)
*A. Rahaman,A. Quadir,M. Tanveer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RoBell-RVFL: A robust randomized neural network with quality-aware sample weighting that handles class imbalance and label noise by preserving minority samples while adaptively weighting majority samples based on local information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Class imbalance in real-world datasets biases randomized neural networks, and existing methods (like SMOTE and class-weighted loss) focus only on class proportions while ignoring intra-class distribution, making them vulnerable to label noise and outliers.

Method: Proposes RoBell-RVFL with dual-strategy sample-level weighting: preserves minority class information using unit weights, while adaptively regulates majority class samples through probability-weighted generalized bell membership function in kernel-induced feature space. This suppresses noisy, boundary, and outlier samples while maintaining closed-form learning efficiency.

Result: Extensive evaluations on UCI and KEEL benchmark datasets with up to 40% label noise show RoBell-RVFL consistently and significantly outperforms recent state-of-the-art RVFL variants.

Conclusion: Adaptive, quality-aware sample weighting is essential for robust RVFL learning in noisy and imbalanced environments, rendering conventional global weighting schemes ineffective.

Abstract: The dominance of majority classes in real-world datasets poses a fundamental challenge to randomized neural networks, often biasing decision boundaries and overlooking critical minority samples. Existing remedies, such as synthetic minority over-sampling (SMOTE) and class-weighted loss functions, primarily address class proportions while neglecting intra-class distribution, making them vulnerable to label noise and outliers. In this paper, we propose \textbf{RoBell-RVFL}, a robust and lightweight \emph{quality-aware} generalized bell random vector functional link network that redefines how randomized models handle class imbalance and noisy data. RoBell-RVFL employs a dual-strategy, sample-level weighting mechanism that strictly preserves minority class information using unit weights, while adaptively regulating the influence of majority class samples through a probability-weighted generalized bell (gbell) membership function in a kernel-induced feature space. This design effectively suppresses noisy, boundary, and outlier samples within the majority class, enabling the network to learn from informative samples rather than merely abundant ones. By explicitly incorporating local class probability and class distribution information into the learning process, RoBell-RVFL achieves adaptive control over sample contributions without sacrificing the closed-form learning efficiency of RVFL networks. Extensive evaluations on UCI and KEEL benchmark datasets, along with robustness tests under up to 40\% label noise, demonstrate that RoBell-RVFL consistently and significantly outperforms recent state-of-the-art RVFL variants. The results indicate that adaptive, quality-aware sample weighting is essential for robust RVFL learning, rendering conventional global weighting schemes ineffective in noisy and imbalanced environments.

</details>


### [77] [MultiSigBERT: Beyond Survival Analysis through Multimodal and Sequential Modeling in Oncology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16972)
*Paul Minchella,Stéphane Chrétien,Guillaume Metzler,Loïc Verlingue,Rémi Vaucher*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MultiSigBERT is a multimodal survival modeling framework that combines narrative medical reports, structured data, and temporal trajectories using path signatures for oncology survival prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current survival models are limited by focusing on single data modalities and failing to capture the temporal nature of patient trajectories. Healthcare EHR data contains heterogeneous complementary information (narrative reports, numerical measurements, structured variables) that needs to be integrated for better clinical decision-making in oncology.

Method: The MultiSigBERT framework converts narrative medical reports into sentence embeddings using contextual word embeddings, compresses them via modality-specific PCA, and concatenates them with structured covariates to form joint temporal trajectories. These trajectories are encoded using the Signature transform from Rough Paths theory to capture higher-order temporal interactions across modalities. Finally, Signature features are incorporated into a LASSO-regularized Cox model for individualized risk estimation.

Result: The model achieved a concordance index of 0.743 (sd 0.029) on an independent test set using a real-world oncology cohort from Léon Bérard Center comprising over 120,000 medical reports and structured records from more than 2,500 patients.

Conclusion: MultiSigBERT demonstrates the benefit of jointly modeling multimodal temporal dynamics with patient-level geometric structure for survival prediction, showing improved performance by integrating heterogeneous EHR data through path signature representations.

Abstract: Machine learning has become an essential component of modern healthcare, where the integration of heterogeneous data sources offers unprecedented opportunities to improve clinical decision-making. Electronic Health Records (EHR) contain complementary information -- including narrative clinical reports, numerical measurements, and structured variables -- yet most survival models remain limited to a single modality or fail to exploit the temporal nature of patient trajectories. We propose MultiSigBERT, a unified framework for multimodal sequential survival modeling in oncology based on path signature representations. Here, narrative medical reports (free-text) are converted into sentence embeddings by extracting and averaging contextual word embeddings. These representations are then compressed via modality-specific PCA and concatenated with structured covariates to form joint temporal trajectories which are then encoded using the Signature transform, a tool from Rough Paths theory that efficiently captures higher-order temporal interactions across modalities without supervision needed. The computed Signature features are finally incorporated as high dimensional features into a LASSO-regularized Cox model to estimate individualized risk scores. The performance of our novel MultiSigBERT pipeline is illustrated on the analysis of a real-world oncology cohort from the Léon Bérard Center, comprising over 120,000 medical reports and structured records from more than 2,500 patients. The model achieves a concordance index of 0.743 (sd 0.029) on an independent test set, demonstrating the benefit of jointly modeling multimodal temporal dynamics together with patient-level geometric structure for survival prediction.

</details>


### [78] [Position: Fairness Failure in Generative Models is an Evaluation Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16974)
*Mariia Vladimirova,Jean-Yves Franceschi,Thibaut Issenhuth*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes "Fairness Cards" as a standardized evaluation framework for measuring fairness in generative models, addressing current reproducibility and comparability issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite advances in generative models, fairness concerns remain under-addressed. Current fairness findings are not comparable across papers or actionable for deployment decisions due to inconsistent evaluation practices.

Method: The paper diagnoses recurring failure modes in current fairness evaluation and proposes "Fairness Cards" - a minimal reporting artifact that makes evaluation choices explicit (including prompt families, counterfactual protocols, metrics, and refusal handling).

Result: The proposed Fairness Cards framework enables reproducibility, comparability, and accountability in fairness evaluation for generative models, moving away from ad-hoc bias checks toward standardized evaluation.

Conclusion: The paper advocates for a paradigm shift in evaluation standards for generative models, recommending standardized evaluation approaches to make fairness findings actionable and comparable across research and deployment contexts.

Abstract: Despite groundbreaking advancements in generative models during the last decade, concerns about their lack of fairness, reinforcing societal inequalities and harming marginalized groups, remain under-addressed and difficult to act upon. This position paper argues that fairness failures in generative models, albeit driven by multiple factors, are ultimately stemming from an evaluation problem: fairness findings are rarely comparable across papers or actionable for deployment decisions. This paper diagnoses recurring empirical and conceptual failure modes in current practice and motivates a shift from ad-hoc bias checks to standardized, generative-specific evaluation. We propose Fairness Cards as a minimal reporting artifact that makes evaluation choices explicit (prompt families, counterfactual protocols, metrics, and refusal handling) enabling reproducibility, comparability, and accountability. We conclude with additional recommendations towards a paradigm shift in evaluation standards. Our project page can be found at https://mariiavladimirova.github.io/fairness-cards .

</details>


### [79] [Agents unlock new capabilities through Switching LoRA Adapters as a Tool (SLAaaT)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17034)
*Kenneth Ge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An agent can switch between specialized LoRA adapters mid-task to avoid catastrophic forgetting, enabling composition of different capabilities without performance tradeoffs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training improves specialized task performance but causes catastrophic forgetting in other domains, creating a problem for long agent trajectories that need to compose different capabilities.

Method: Give an agent a tool to switch between specialized LoRA adapters mid-trace. Test on two synthetic coding tasks that are logically simple but require specialization.

Result: The approach allows models to solve previously unsolvable problems, enables autonomous switching (even finding new strategies that beat human heuristic baselines), and achieves up to 18x reduction in capability tax compared to using only one specialized adapter. It substantially outperforms spawning subagents in both task capabilities and token usage.

Conclusion: Dynamic switching between specialized LoRA adapters during agent execution effectively rejects the tradeoff between specialization and catastrophic forgetting, enabling better composition of different capabilities in long trajectories.

Abstract: Post-training can unlock new capabilities and improve performance on specialized tasks, but sometimes at the cost of catastrophic forgetting in other domains. This poses a problem in long agent trajectories that compose different capabilities. We reject this tradeoff by giving an agent a tool to switch between specialized LoRA adapters mid-trace. To test its effectiveness, we compose two synthetic coding tasks that are logically simple but require specialization. We find that this allows the model to solve problems it previously could not, that the model is able to switch autonomously (and find a new strategy that beats our human heuristic baseline on one task), and that this incurs an up to an 18x reduction in capability tax compared to an agent using only one specialized adapter. Our approach also substantially outperforms spawning subagents in both task capabilities and token usage.

</details>


### [80] [J-Miner: Recovering Executable Decision Knowledge from Language-Model Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17063)
*Yunfan Gao,Xinyi Huang,Tao Sheng,Haorui Song,Yun Xiong,Haofen Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: J-Miner extracts explicit, executable decision rules from fine-tuned LLM classifiers by mining internal semantic concepts, achieving high fidelity and enabling knowledge transfer to smaller models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuned LLM classifiers make complex judgments but keep decision knowledge implicit within the model, making it impossible to inspect, validate, or reuse beyond the original classifier.

Method: J-Miner mines text-level named concepts by aggregating vocabulary-aligned internal signals across layers and token positions, then uses classifier predictions to learn executable decision rules over these concepts, distilling local readouts into explicit knowledge representations.

Result: J-Miner rules reproduce up to 98.3% of source-classifier decisions, achieve 6.0-29.5 percentage points higher behavioral fidelity than word-based rules, and transfer to lightweight students (1/24 parameters) while retaining 99.8% of source accuracy.

Conclusion: Task-specific decision knowledge from fine-tuned classifiers can be faithfully represented in explicit, executable form and effectively reused beyond the original classifier.

Abstract: Large language models can be fine-tuned into specialized classifiers that perform well across diverse text tasks and make complex judgments, but they typically expose only final labels, leaving the decision knowledge acquired through fine-tuning implicit within the model. We study how to mine this internal decision knowledge from a fine-tuned classifier and encode it in an executable representation that can be inspected, validated, and reused beyond the source classifier. We introduce J-Miner, which mines text-level named concepts by aggregating vocabulary-aligned internal signals across layers and token positions, and uses the classifier's own predictions to learn executable decision rules over them. This process distills local internal readouts into an explicit classifier-level knowledge representation. Across multiple classification tasks, J-Miner rules reproduce up to 98.3\% of source-classifier decisions and achieve 6.0--29.5 percentage points higher behavioral fidelity than equally compact rules learned from input words. Further analysis shows that the named concepts reflect internal semantic evidence associated with task decisions, while the learned rules consolidate these distributed signals into inspectable decision structures. The resulting decision knowledge also transfers to lightweight standalone students: using about 1/24 as many parameters as the source classifiers, they reconstruct and execute the representation from raw text while retaining 99.8\% of the source classifiers' mean task accuracy. These findings show that task-specific decision knowledge can be faithfully represented in an explicit, executable form and reused beyond the classifier in which it was learned.

</details>


### [81] [Certified but Private: Scalable Zero-Knowledge Proofs for Neural Network Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17070)
*Youwei Zhong,Ben Merbaum,Timos Antonopoulos,Ning Luo,Charalampos Papamanthou,Katerina Sotiraki,Ruzica Piskac*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PANDA is a scalable system using zero-knowledge proofs to verify neural network robustness and fairness without revealing private model parameters, achieving orders of magnitude better scalability than previous approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With growing deployment of machine learning models in safety-critical and legal-compliance settings, formal guarantees of robustness and fairness are crucial. However, model parameters are often commercial secrets that cannot be disclosed to auditors or end users, creating a need for verification without parameter disclosure.

Method: PANDA builds on CROWN (efficient robustness certification framework) and introduces a novel algorithm for proving linear relaxation bounds for non-linear activation layers using zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs). This yields simple, lightweight proofs while maintaining privacy of model parameters.

Result: The system can generate proofs of local robustness for neural networks with over 2.9M parameters in 5 minutes and verify them in 10 seconds. PANDA scales polynomially with network size, supporting neural networks 4 orders of magnitude larger than previous ZKP-based approaches with significantly reduced prover overhead.

Conclusion: PANDA represents a significant advance in privacy-preserving formal verification of neural networks, enabling practical verification of robustness and fairness properties for commercial-scale models without parameter disclosure, addressing key limitations of prior exponential-time ZKP approaches.

Abstract: With the growing deployment of machine learning models, formal guarantees of the robustness and fairness of these models have become increasingly important in safety-critical and legal-compliance settings. However, model parameters are often commercial secrets that cannot be disclosed to auditors or end users. To this end, we present PANDA, a scalable system that uses zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs) to prove the robustness and fairness properties of a model without revealing its private parameters. PANDA is built on top of CROWN, an efficient robustness certification framework that is used in many state-of-the-art formal verification tools for neural networks. The core contribution of PANDA is a novel algorithm for proving linear relaxation bounds for non-linear activation layers, yielding simple, lightweight proofs. Remarkably, our system can generate proofs of local robustness for neural networks with more than 2.9M parameters in 5 minutes, and can verify them in 10 seconds. Prior ZKP-based robustness system rely on exponential-time algorithms that cannot scale to nontrivial networks. In contrast, PANDA scales polynomially in the number of neurons in a network, allowing us to support neural networks 4 orders of magnitude larger than previous approaches with significantly reduced prover overhead.

</details>


### [82] [Dynamic Regime-Aware Conformal Calibration for Reliable Economic Forecast Intervals under Multiple Distribution Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17079)
*Bogdan Oancea*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DRACP proposes a dynamic conformal prediction method that combines density-ratio, localized kernel, and regime-aware weighting with an online controller to maintain reliable coverage under economic forecasting conditions where exchangeability is violated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard conformal prediction assumes exchangeability, which is often violated in economic forecasting due to covariate shift, concept drift, local heterogeneity, and latent regimes. This creates a need for methods that can maintain valid coverage guarantees in dynamic, non-exchangeable settings common in economic applications.

Method: Dynamic Regime-Aware Conformal Prediction (DRACP) integrates three components: 1) density-ratio estimation to handle distribution shifts, 2) localized kernel weighting for local heterogeneity, 3) probabilistic regime-aware weighting for latent regimes, all combined with a self-tuning online significance controller in a unified weighted conformal calibration framework.

Result: DRACP provides the most reliable calibration among tested methods, achieving coverage closest to nominal 0.90 (actual 0.890), never falling below 0.80 on any series, maintaining best coverage at all forecast horizons, and performing best during the 2021-2023 inflation surge. However, it's not the most efficient method (strongly-adaptive online conformal prediction achieves better interval scores and narrower intervals).

Conclusion: DRACP offers a principled trade-off between calibration reliability and efficiency, favoring reliable coverage when prediction intervals must satisfy strict coverage standards. The online controller and conditional-scale normalization provide most performance gains, while weighting components make smaller contributions.

Abstract: Conformal prediction provides distribution-free prediction intervals but relies on exchangeability, an assumption often violated in economic forecasting because of covariate shift, concept drift, local heterogeneity and latent regimes. We propose Dynamic Regime-Aware Conformal Prediction (DRACP), which combines density-ratio, localized kernel and probabilistic regime-aware weighting with a self-tuning online significance controller in a unified weighted conformal calibration framework. We distinguish three theoretical results: finite-sample validity under oracle importance weights, a coverage-gap bound for estimated weights with rates in effective sample size, and deterministic or regret guarantees for the online controller. We evaluate DRACP against six baselines on 48 real forecasting series covering euro-area and EU-27 HICP inflation, US macroeconomic and energy indicators, and daily financial series. Recent online methods (FACI, strongly-adaptive online conformal prediction and conformal PID) were verified against the authors' implementations. DRACP is not the most efficient method: strongly-adaptive online conformal prediction achieves the best interval score and intervals about 20% narrower. Instead, DRACP provides the most reliable calibration, achieving coverage closest to the nominal 0.90 (0.890), never falling below 0.80 on any series, maintaining the best coverage at all forecast horizons, and performing best during the 2021-2023 inflation surge. The strongly-adaptive method undercovers on 20 of 48 series versus 10 for DRACP. DRACP therefore offers a principled trade-off between calibration and efficiency, favoring reliable coverage when prediction intervals must satisfy coverage standards. An ablation study shows that the online controller and conditional-scale normalization provide most of the performance gain, whereas the weighting components make a smaller contribution.

</details>


### [83] [Backward through Time, Algebraically](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17087)
*Konstantinos Kogkalidis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents telos, a PyTorch library providing an algebra-generic differentiable evaluation engine for linear temporal logic (LTL) that can handle various semantic algebras for softly-valued systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard boolean-valued LTL is insufficient for steering softly-valued systems like neural policies and sequence models, which require differentiable semantics. Existing differentiable LTL implementations are shallow embeddings that commit to single semantic algebras with implicit behaviors.

Method: Developed an algebra-generic evaluation engine for LTL that is amenable to differentiation, with an executable specification of acceptable algebras. Implemented various algebras and analyzed their forward and backward behavior.

Result: Created the telos PyTorch library that provides a flexible framework for differentiable LTL evaluation. Each implemented algebra represents different trade-offs in satisfaction semantics, essentially "choosing which direction to disappoint and how."

Conclusion: The functional programming approach yields a practical solution for differentiable LTL semantics that avoids upfront commitment to specific semantic algebras, providing a flexible framework for training softly-valued systems with temporal logic constraints.

Abstract: Linear temporal logic is a modal extension of propositional logic that allows one to state how a system should behave over time. Its canonical domain is the booleans, but discretely-valued judgements are of little use in steering softly-valued systems (neural policies, adaptive controllers, sequence models, etc). In such cases, the goal formula's (dis)satisfaction becomes a training signal, and differentiability becomes a prime concern. Candidate differentiable semantics abound, but navigating them is tricky. Implementations, where available, are shallow embeddings, demanding an upfront commitment to a single semantic algebra and its (usually implicit) conduct. The paper casts the reader as a functional programmer asked to come to terms with this predicament, and refusing. Out of that refusal comes an evaluation engine that is algebra-generic and amenable to differentiation, together with an executable specification of the algebras it can accept. Various algebras are implemented and audited for their behavior, both forward and backward. Each algebra turns out to be a choice of which direction to disappoint, and how. Everything described (and more) is part of the PyTorch library telos, to be found at https://github.com/konstantinosKokos/telos.

</details>


### [84] [Deep Learning for Cross-Border Electricity Price Forecasting: A Comparative Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17091)
*Hadeer Elashhab,Sai Srijan Papineni,Marvin Dorn,Veit Hagenmeyer,Benjamin Schäfer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper establishes a reproducible benchmark framework for electricity price forecasting using public market data, evaluating six deep learning models across multiple markets with a focus on generalization under limited-data conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field lacks standardized benchmark datasets for electricity price forecasting research, making it difficult to consistently compare methods and assess progress. Many studies use different datasets and evaluation metrics, and deep learning approaches have been limited to single-market settings or narrow feature sets.

Method: The authors evaluate six deep learning models (state-space, MLP, RNN, and Transformer architectures) across multiple electricity markets. They simulate low-data target-market conditions using zero-shot, one-shot, and few-shot learning. They use public market data with standardized calendar, historical price, and market-derived features, focusing on the Germany-Luxembourg bidding zone in 2024.

Result: N-HiTS and NBEATSx perform competitively in limited-data scenarios. Transformer-based models can achieve comparable accuracy but require more adaptation and tuning. Model performance benefits from careful feature selection and hyperparameter tuning, with small differences between the strongest models.

Conclusion: The study establishes a reproducible framework for standardized evaluation of electricity price forecasting models, demonstrating that model generalization across markets requires careful consideration of architecture selection, feature engineering, and adaptation strategies, with simpler models sometimes outperforming more complex ones in data-limited settings.

Abstract: While publicly available electricity market data presents a valuable resource for forecasting research, the field lacks established benchmark datasets for standardized comparison. As a result, many studies have relied on different datasets and metrics to evaluate methods in isolated settings, making it difficult to assess progress and compare state-of-the-art approaches consistently. In this work, we use public data to evaluate deep learning models for electricity price forecasting (EPF) across multiple market settings. Our goal is to establish a reproducible framework that enables a consistent evaluation of forecasting models. Although deep learning has been explored for day-ahead EPF, many prior studies are limited to single-market settings, narrow feature sets, or fixed training regimes. This work presents a comparative evaluation of six deep learning models--covering state-space, MLP, RNN, and Transformer-based architectures--emphasizing generalization across markets. We simulate low-data target-market conditions using zero-shot, one-shot, and few-shot learning. Our test set focuses on the Germany-Luxembourg (DE-LU) bidding zone in 2024 using a standardized dataset with calendar, historical price, and market-derived features. Our findings suggest that N-HiTS and NBEATSx perform competitively in limited-data scenarios, while transformer-based models can reach comparable accuracy but tend to require more adaptation and tuning. Model performance also benefits from careful feature selection and hyperparameter tuning, and we note that the differences between the strongest models are often small.

</details>


### [85] [From Abductive Explanations to Global Logical Rules for Node Classification in SGCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17103)
*Bryan Lima Cavalcante,Thiago Alves Rocha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Logic-based framework for explaining SGC networks using minimal abductive explanations to extract compact global rules from node classification predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing logic-based explanation methods for GNNs use explanatory subgraphs that may contain redundant structural information specific to individual nodes, potentially limiting the generality of extracted rules. The paper aims to improve rule extraction by addressing this redundancy issue.

Method: Proposes a logic-based framework for SGC networks that uses minimal abductive explanations as intermediate representation: 1) For each node, compute minimal set of node-feature pairs sufficient to preserve predicted class, 2) Use these explanations to train decision trees, 3) Extract global logical rules from the decision trees.

Result: Experiments on benchmark datasets show the framework produces compact global rules while maintaining high fidelity to the original SGC model.

Conclusion: The proposed approach successfully addresses redundancy in explanatory subgraphs and produces more generalizable logical rules for explaining SGC network predictions in node classification tasks.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have achieved remarkable performance in node classification tasks, motivating growing interest in methods capable of explaining their predictions. Recent logic-based approaches, such as LogicXGNN, derive global logical rules for Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) from collections of explanatory subgraphs. While informative, these subgraphs may contain redundant structural information that is specific to individual nodes, potentially limiting the generality of the extracted rules. In this work, we propose a logic-based framework for node classification in Simple Graph Convolution (SGC) networks that uses minimal abductive explanations as an intermediate representation for rule extraction. For each node, we compute a minimal set of node-feature pairs sufficient to preserve the predicted class. These explanations are then used to train decision trees from which global logical rules are extracted. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that the proposed framework produces compact global rules while maintaining high fidelity to the original SGC model.

</details>


### [86] [Causal Discovery in Equal Variance Linear Gaussian DAGs via SURE-Tuned Ridge Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17132)
*Sambit Mishra,Urbashi Mitra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SURE-Ridge: A non-iterative, closed-form estimator for DAG recovery using parallel ridge regressions with SURE-based adaptive regularization and thresholding, outperforming iterative methods in small-sample and compute-limited regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continuous-optimization methods for DAG recovery (like NOTEARS, DAGMA) require iterative gradient descent and per-problem hyperparameter tuning, which are poorly suited for two important practical regimes: (1) sample-limited regime where samples ≤ number of nodes, and (2) compute-limited regime requiring fast solutions.

Method: SURE-Ridge uses parallel node-wise ridge regressions with regularization parameters adaptively chosen using Stein's Unbiased Risk Estimate (SURE). It then applies an adaptive thresholding procedure to extract a directed acyclic graph from the resulting soft adjacency matrix.

Result: Numerical results show SURE-Ridge achieves the lowest structural Hamming distance in small-sample regimes and the lowest runtime across all sample sizes tested, compared with NOTEARS, DAGMA, and GBNSL baselines.

Conclusion: SURE-Ridge provides an efficient, non-iterative alternative to continuous-optimization methods for DAG discovery, particularly valuable in sample-limited and compute-limited settings while maintaining competitive or superior accuracy.

Abstract: Recovering the directed acyclic graph (DAG) of a structural equation model (SEM) from observational data is a central problem in causal discovery. The iterative gradient descent and per-problem hyperparameter tuning of continuous-optimization methods are poorly suited to two practically important regimes: the sample-limited regime, where the number of samples is comparable to or smaller than the number of nodes in the DAG, and the compute-limited regime. This work proposes SURE-Ridge, a non-iterative, closed-form estimator for equal variance linear Gaussian SEM. The method performs parallel node-wise regressions with regularization parameters chosen adaptively by Stein's unbiased risk estimate (SURE), and applies an adaptive thresholding procedure to extract a DAG from the resulting soft adjacency matrix. Numerical results show that SURE-Ridge achieves the lowest structural Hamming distance in the small-sample regime and the lowest run time across all sample sizes tested, compared with NOTEARS, DAGMA, and GBNSL baselines.

</details>


### [87] [Iterative tensor network transformations for element-wise evaluation of elementary and filtering functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17135)
*Xiao Wang,Tomohiro Hashizume,Pia Siegl,Dieter Jaksch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ITNTs enable nonlinear operations on tensor train-compressed data, allowing efficient processing of exponentially large datasets for scientific computing and optimization tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tensor networks are powerful for compressing large-scale data but have limited applicability due to difficulty performing nonlinear operations on compressed representations.

Method: Introduced iterative tensor network transformations (ITNTs) - a framework for element-wise evaluation of elementary and nonlinear filtering functions on data encoded as tensor trains, operating entirely in the compressed domain.

Result: Successfully applied to: (I) evaluating nonlinear functions on 3D reactive flow fields for reaction rate computation and filtering, and (II) finding extrema in complex optimization problems like solving Max-SAT on spaces up to 2^70 configurations.

Conclusion: ITNT establishes a foundational tool that provides tensor network methods with general-purpose data science and large-scale optimization capabilities, enabling efficient computation on exponentially large datasets.

Abstract: Tensor networks are powerful formats for compressing large-scale data. However, their application to general data processing has been limited by the difficulty of performing nonlinear operations. Here, we introduce iterative tensor network transformations (ITNTs), a general algorithmic framework for the element-wise evaluation of elementary and nonlinear filtering functions on data encoded as tensor trains (TTs), a class of tensor networks. Our approach operates entirely in the compressed domain, enabling efficient computation on exponentially large datasets while maintaining a controlled computational cost. We demonstrate its power in two key areas: (I) evaluating highly nonlinear elementary and filtering functions on a 3D reactive flow field, enabling high-fidelity reaction rate computation and region filtering, and (II) finding extrema in complex optimization problems, such as solving Max-SAT instances on spaces up to $2^{70}$ configurations. These results establish ITNT as a foundational tool that provides tensor network methods with the capability for general-purpose data science and large-scale optimization.

</details>


### [88] [OraclePhys: A Systematic Framework for LLM Fine-Tuning on Structural Mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17162)
*Mingyu Li,Guorui Song,Jing Lin,Haoqian Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OraclePhys is a fine-tuning framework with a structural-mechanics benchmark, supervision dataset, and controlled training study showing that answer form (not bit count) causally determines what fine-tuning teaches, with ranking objectives enabling out-of-distribution forward modeling while certain scoring methods only teach routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what language models internalize from fine-tuning by making it an experimental variable rather than just diagnosing after the fact. The paper aims to systematically study how different answer forms and verifier roles affect what models learn during fine-tuning on structural mechanics tasks.

Method: Developed OraclePhys framework with three components: 1) OraclePhys-Bench (exactly-graded structural-mechanics benchmark with finite-element oracle scoring), 2) OraclePhys-30K (supervision dataset of seven answer forms), and 3) controlled training study across seven answer forms and three verifier roles. Compared ranking, scalar, and boolean objectives across different model families and physics domains.

Result: 1) Label's answer form causally determines fine-tuning outcomes: ranking objectives enable out-of-distribution forward modeling, scalar objectives partial modeling, boolean objectives nothing detectable. 2) Written/score-filtered answers install capability while advantage-weighted scores (GRPO) only teach routing. 3) Trained 8B model reaches data-precision frontier, outperforming frontier LLMs at zero- and 32-shot, matching specialist level.

Conclusion: What the label spells out about the target computation determines what fine-tuning teaches; training content teaches routing. The answer form (not bit count) is causally important for learning physics reasoning capabilities during fine-tuning.

Abstract: What a language model internalizes from fine-tuning is usually diagnosed after the fact. We make it an experimental variable. OraclePhys is a systematic fine-tuning framework with three components: OraclePhys-Bench, an exactly-graded structural-mechanics benchmark whose finite-element oracle scores every answer and counterfactual edit -- no human labels, no LLM judging; OraclePhys-30K, a supervision dataset of seven answer forms over byte-identical structure descriptions; and a controlled training study across the seven forms and three verifier roles. The study yields two findings. First, the label's answer form -- not its bit count -- causally determines what fine-tuning teaches: a ranking objective installs an out-of-distribution forward model where the untrained base sits at the guessing prior, a scalar objective at best a partial one, a boolean nothing detectable; the vector-scalar gulf survives a second physics domain, a second model family, and a paraphrased evaluation surface. Second, written or score-filtered answers install this capability, while advantage-weighted scores (GRPO) raise reward yet leave the model statistically equivalent to its start on held-out physics -- within the recipes and budgets tested -- sufficing only for routing. The trained 8B -- the first LLM on spatial structural response -- reaches the task's data-precision frontier: above a frontier LLM at zero- and 32-shot, at a specialist's level. What the label spells out about the target computation is what fine-tuning teaches; what you train on is what you route.

</details>


### [89] [Q-Learning With World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17163)
*Perry Dong,Yueru Jia,Chelsea Finn,Dorsa Sadigh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QWM is a model-based RL framework that uses world models for test-time search over imagined trajectories while training policies only on real transitions, avoiding compounding model bias and improving sample efficiency on manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current model-based RL methods that optimize policies on imagined rollouts suffer from compounding bias and struggle to scale to real-world robotics with long horizons and visual complexity. There's a need to leverage world models' sample efficiency while avoiding these issues.

Method: Propose QWM (Q-learning with World Models), which trains policies and value functions only on real transitions but uses world models at test time to perform search over imagined trajectories. This allows selecting high-value actions during online rollouts while avoiding compounding model bias.

Result: QWM significantly outperforms prior state-of-the-art methods on both sample efficiency and performance on challenging manipulation benchmarks Robomimic and LIBERO.

Conclusion: World models can be effectively leveraged for test-time search alongside standard Q-learning without compounding model bias, achieving strong performance in real-world robotics settings with long horizons and visual complexity.

Abstract: Off-policy reinforcement learning (RL) has become increasingly sample-efficient, enabling applications such as RL fine-tuning of Vision-Language-Action models into reliable, high-performing policies. World models offer a further lever for sample efficiency, as they predict state changes rather than actions alone, but their success has largely been confined to supervised policy learning. Prior model-based RL methods often optimize the policy or value function directly on imagined rollouts, which is prone to compounding bias and struggles to scale to large, high-dimensional problems such as real-world robotics, a problem that worsens with task horizon and visual complexity. In this work, we instead ask whether we can leverage world models directly on top of standard Q-learning to improve performance, while remaining trained and grounded in the real, online setting. We propose QWM, a framework that leverages world models to perform test-time search over imagined trajectories on top of Q-learning to select high-value actions during both online rollouts and evaluation. Since the policy and value function are trained only on real transitions, QWM avoids compounding model bias while still gaining the sample-efficiency benefits of predictive search. On challenging manipulation benchmarks Robomimic and LIBERO, QWM significantly outperforms strong prior state-of-the-art methods on both sample efficiency and performance.

</details>


### [90] [SCENARIODIFF: A Scenario-level Guidance Framework for Multimodal Time Series Forecasting--Extended Version](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17164)
*Tuan-Binh Tran,Dat Nguyen Cong,Duc-Trong Le,Thanh Trung Huynh,Tung Kieu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCENARIODIFF is a hierarchical contextual reasoning framework for multimodal time series forecasting that organizes textual context into three structured levels and uses a Multimodal Diffusion Transformer with Anchor Blended Sampling for improved forecasting, especially in event-driven domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Textual context (news, reports, logs) provides valuable signals for time series forecasting, especially when future dynamics are driven by external events not yet visible in historical data. Existing methods either ask LLMs to predict numerical values directly or fuse text and time series implicitly, making contextual influence difficult to interpret and control.

Method: Proposes SCENARIODIFF, a hierarchical contextual reasoning framework with three levels: Historical Context Agent extracts stepwise evidence from raw documents, Scenario Agent produces qualitative scenario descriptions for forecast horizon, and Anchor Guidance Agent generates sparse anchor points for event-relevant future regions. These condition a Multimodal Diffusion Transformer, with Anchor Blended Sampling for local refinement without retraining.

Result: Experiments on the Time-MMD benchmark show SCENARIODIFF is especially effective in event-driven domains, demonstrating the value of explicit hierarchical scenario guidance for multimodal time series forecasting.

Conclusion: The explicit hierarchical scenario guidance approach in SCENARIODIFF provides interpretable and controllable contextual influence for multimodal time series forecasting, particularly valuable for noisy and weakly aligned document contexts in event-driven applications.

Abstract: Textual context such as news, reports, and logs can provide valuable signals for time series forecasting, especially when future dynamics are driven by external events that are not yet visible in historical values. Existing multimodal forecasting methods often either ask large language models (LLMs) to predict numerical values directly or fuse text and time series implicitly, making contextual influence difficult to interpret and control. We propose SCENARIODIFF, a hierarchical contextual reasoning framework for multimodal time series forecasting under noisy and weakly aligned documents. SCENARIODIFF organizes contextual information into three levels: a Historical Context Agent extracts stepwise evidence from raw documents, a Scenario Agent produces a qualitative scenario description for the forecast horizon, and an Anchor Guidance Agent generates sparse anchor points for event-relevant future regions. These structured signals condition a Multimodal Diffusion Transformer, while Anchor Blended Sampling locally refines generated trajectories without retraining. Experiments on the Time-MMD benchmark show that SCENARIODIFF is especially effective in event-driven domains, demonstrating the value of explicit hierarchical scenario guidance for multimodal time series forecasting. Our full implementation is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/ScenarioDiff_ICDM-2C4C

</details>


### [91] [Population Health-Based Machine Learning Reveals Associations Between Psychosocial Factors and Chronic Kidney Disease](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17174)
*Md. Atik Shams,David Eisenberg,Sumaiya Fatema,Asma Sultana,D. M Hasibul Islam,Junnatul Mawa,Anindita Datta,Nafiya Ahmed,Danastan Tasaouf Mridula,SK. Sazid Mahmud,Simon Bin Akter,Tanjila Helaly,Jorge Fresneda Fernandez,Humayera Islam,Tanmoy Sarkar Pias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A machine learning study using large-scale telehealth data to classify CKD status and identify key disease drivers through ensemble models and SHAP analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chronic kidney disease progresses silently and severely impacts quality of life, making early detection critical for improving patient outcomes.

Method: Used large-scale data from BRFSS (2019, 2021) and NHIS (2020, 2021) with 9 imputation methods for missing data and sampling strategies for class imbalance. Developed customized stacked ensemble models for CKD classification.

Result: Achieved balanced accuracy of 72.56-76.12% with AUROC scores of 79.59-82.29%. SHAP analysis identified key predictors including regular medical check-ups, age, blood pressure, and mental health stress indicators.

Conclusion: Provides a robust and interpretable framework for CKD risk stratification and offers actionable insights into disease-associated factors for early detection.

Abstract: Chronic kidney disease (CKD) progresses silently and severely undermines quality of life, making early detection critical for improving patient outcomes. We present a two-part study that combines large-scale telehealth data with advanced machine learning to both classify self-reported CKD status and identify key drivers of disease. Using selected features from the Behavioral Risk Factor Surveillance System (BRFSS 2021: 438,693 samples; BRFSS 2019: 418,268 samples) and the National Health Interview Survey (NHIS 2021: 29,482 samples; NHIS 2020: 31,568 samples), we addressed missing data with nine state-of-the-art imputation methods and mitigated class imbalance via sampling strategies. Our customized stacked ensemble model achieved balanced accuracy of 72.56-76.12%, with corresponding AUROC scores of 79.59-82.29%. SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) analysis, followed by clinical review, highlighted critical predictors, including regular medical check-ups, age, blood pressure, and indicators of mental health stress. These findings deliver a robust and interpretable framework for CKD risk stratification and provide actionable insights into its associated factors.

</details>


### [92] [Task Specialization Fine-Tuning for Contextual Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17180)
*Jianan Zhou,Jung-Hoon Cho,Tianyue Zhou,Han Zheng,Jie Zhang,Roy Dong,Yining Ma,Cathy Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TSFT: A model-based framework that optimally allocates fine-tuning budget across task regions for contextual RL, outperforming baselines across diverse domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current CRL approaches either use multi-task learning with a single policy or train multiple policies strategically. The paper advocates for a unified pretrain-finetune paradigm where a single pretrained policy gets fine-tuned for task specialization, but this introduces challenges like heterogeneous marginal returns and sample inefficiency that need to be addressed.

Method: Proposes Task Specialization Fine-Tuning (TSFT): an online framework that uses a simple parametric model to predict fine-tuning performance, then solves the discrete budget allocation problem exactly using integer linear programming to determine optimal fine-tuning allocation across task regions.

Result: TSFT significantly outperforms baseline methods in task coverage across diverse decision domains including combinatorial optimization, continuous control, and LLM fine-tuning. It approaches oracle performance levels, demonstrating effectiveness in sample-efficient CRL.

Conclusion: The work establishes a new direction for model-based contextual RL that aligns with modern pretrain-finetune paradigms, providing a principled solution for optimal fine-tuning budget allocation across task regions to maximize task coverage.

Abstract: Contextual Reinforcement Learning (CRL) seeks to generalize classical RL by maximizing task coverage across a context space of related tasks. While prior works often train from scratch and rely on either multi-task learning for a single policy or strategically training multiple policies, we advocate for a unified alternative: pretraining a single policy with good initial performance, followed by fine-tuning multiple policies for task specialization. This new paradigm, however, introduces unique challenges, such as heterogeneous marginal returns and sample inefficiency. This raises a critical research question: given a pretrained policy and a constrained budget, how much fine-tuning should each task region receive to enable sample-efficient CRL? To this end, we propose Task Specialization Fine-Tuning (TSFT), an online framework that predicts fine-tuning performance with a simple parametric model and exactly solves the resulting discrete budget allocation problem via integer linear programming. Extensive experiments across diverse decision domains, including combinatorial optimization, continuous control, and LLM fine-tuning, demonstrate that TSFT significantly outperforms baselines in task coverage and approaches oracle performance. Our work charts a new direction for model-based CRL, aligning with the modern pretrain-finetune era.

</details>


### [93] [Reinforcement Learning as (Discrete) Potential Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17181)
*Christopher Connolly*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper explores connections between reinforcement learning, probability theory, and potential theory, examining how potential-theoretic viewpoints can enhance RL representations and algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to leverage the deep connection between probability theory and potential theory to improve reinforcement learning. RL fundamentally depends on probability theory through Markov chains, and potential theory offers a complementary viewpoint that may lead to better sample efficiency and formal constraints for RL algorithms.

Method: The method involves reviewing the connection between probability theory and potential theory, then applying this potential-theoretic viewpoint to core RL representations and algorithms under a fixed-policy assumption. The framework can be extended from linear potential theory (under fixed policies) to nonlinear cases when the fixed-policy assumption is relaxed.

Result: The paper establishes that potential theory provides a valuable framework for understanding RL representations and algorithms. Under fixed policies, linear potential theory applies naturally, and this can be extended to nonlinear potential theory when policies are not fixed, suggesting a unified mathematical framework for RL analysis.

Conclusion: The potential-theoretic viewpoint offers promising directions for RL improvement, particularly for sample efficiency and formal constraint derivation. The mathematical connection between probability theory and potential theory provides a foundation that can be extended from linear to nonlinear cases as policy assumptions change, creating a more comprehensive theoretical framework for reinforcement learning.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) theory fundamentally depends on probability theory through the Markov chain. There is a deep connection between probability theory and potential theory. This paper reviews that connection and explores the potential-theoretic viewpoint for core reinforcement learning representations and algorithms under a fixed-policy assumption. This viewpoint may offer a path for improved sample efficiency and formal constraints that can be applied to RL. When the fixed-policy assumption is relaxed, the linear potential theory framework can be naturally extended to the nonlinear case.

</details>


### [94] [How smoothing the affinity matrix affects neighborhood preservation in t-SNE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17190)
*Shirin Mohebi,Guillaume Bied,Jefrey Lijffijt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a row-wise power transform (controlled by gamma parameter) for t-SNE's affinity matrix that adjusts probability distribution sharpness per data point, enabling better preservation of either very nearest neighbors (with sharpening) or broader local neighborhoods (with smoothing).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study how the sharpness of t-SNE's probability distribution affects neighborhood preservation at different scales, since t-SNE's affinity matrix is central to its ability to preserve local neighborhoods in high-dimensional data visualization.

Method: Introduces a row-wise power transform controlled by parameter gamma that smooths or sharpens each row of t-SNE's affinity matrix while preserving sparsity and rank order. This is shown to be equivalent to rescaling the Gaussian bandwidth and changing perplexity, but with point-dependent effective perplexities.

Result: Empirical findings show that sharpening improves preservation of very nearest neighbors, while smoothing improves preservation of broader local neighborhoods. The method outperforms alternative affinity constructions including multiscale methods in the mid-local range.

Conclusion: The row-wise power transform provides a flexible way to control neighborhood preservation at different scales in t-SNE visualization, offering distinct advantages over global perplexity adjustment by enabling point-dependent sharpness control.

Abstract: Dimensionality reduction methods are instrumental to visualize high-dimensional data, and t-SNE stands as one of the most widely used methods due to its emphasis on local neighborhood preservation. A central component of t-SNE is the affinity matrix, which expresses pairwise similarities in the form of symmetrized probabilities, over which the optimization problem of t-SNE is defined. We study how the sharpness of this probability distribution affects neighborhood preservation at different scales. We introduce a row-wise power transform controlled by a parameter gamma that can smooth or sharpen each row of the affinity matrix while preserving sparsity and rank order. We show that this transform is equivalent to rescaling the Gaussian bandwidth and thus to changing the perplexity. However, as the sharpness of the probability distribution varies per point, a fixed gamma leads to point-dependent effective perplexities, making it distinct from changing the global perplexity. Empirically, we find that sharpening improves preservation of the very nearest neighbors, while smoothing improves preservation of broader local neighborhoods, outperforming alternative affinity constructions including multiscale methods in the mid-local range.

</details>


### [95] [Pessimistic Meta-Induction and Its Limits: Lessons from Frequentist Statistics and Machine Learning Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17213)
*Hanti Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops a new challenge to the pessimistic meta-inductive argument by showing meta-induction fails to achieve truth convergence, while ordinary induction succeeds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the pessimistic meta-inductive argument against scientific realism, which claims historical failures of scientific theories show current theories will likely fail.

Method: Draws on epistemology from frequentist statistics, machine learning, and formal epistemology to evaluate induction in terms of convergence to truth. Compares ordinary enumerative induction with meta-induction using convergence criteria.

Result: Ordinary enumerative induction achieves everywhere convergence to truth, while meta-induction fails to achieve even almost everywhere convergence. In the problem context where meta-induction arises, no inference method whatsoever achieves almost everywhere convergence.

Conclusion: The inductive step of the pessimistic meta-inductive argument is fundamentally flawed because meta-induction lacks the necessary convergence properties, undermining the argument against scientific realism.

Abstract: This paper challenges the pessimistic meta-inductive argument against scientific realism by undermining its inductive step rather than its historical premise. Although related challenges already exist, I develop a new one. Drawing on a general epistemology of scientific inference developed in frequentist statistics, machine learning, and formal epistemology, I evaluate induction in terms of convergence to the truth. I argue that ordinary enumerative induction can achieve everywhere convergence, whereas meta-induction fails even to achieve almost everywhere convergence. Indeed, in the problem context where meta-induction arises, the failure is deeper: no inference method whatsoever achieves almost everywhere convergence.

</details>


### [96] [Delta2Gamma: Band-Wise Adaptive Contrastive Learning of EEG for Alzheimer's Disease Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17231)
*Chanwoo Park,Chanwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Delta2Gamma is a self-supervised framework that learns EEG representations by decomposing signals into five neural rhythm bands and using contrastive learning with adaptive temperature scaling, achieving 92.4% accuracy in distinguishing Alzheimer's from controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current dementia screening methods have limitations: imaging-based diagnosis is expensive and hard to deploy widely, while EEG is portable and inexpensive but suffers from noisy recordings, high inter-subject variability, and limited clinical labels.

Method: Delta2Gamma decomposes EEG recordings into five canonical neural rhythms (delta, theta, alpha, beta, gamma). Each band has its own encoder and projection head, and uses contrastive learning by creating augmented views of signals. The framework introduces adaptive temperature prediction for each band during training to automatically balance bands with different signal statistics.

Result: On the ADFTD cohort under a strict leave-one-subject-out protocol, Delta2Gamma achieved 92.4% accuracy in separating Alzheimer's disease from cognitively normal controls, outperforming both supervised backbones and recent dedicated EEG methods.

Conclusion: Delta2Gamma demonstrates that self-supervised learning on decomposed EEG rhythms with adaptive temperature balancing can effectively capture disease-relevant patterns, offering a promising low-cost, scalable approach for dementia screening using portable EEG technology.

Abstract: Low-cost, scalable screening for dementia remains an open problem. Imaging-based diagnosis is costly and hard to deploy widely. Electroencephalography (EEG) is portable and inexpensive, but its recordings are noisy, vary widely across subjects, and carry few clinical labels. We tackle this with Delta2Gamma, a self-supervised framework that learns EEG representations from unlabeled data by contrasting augmented views of each signal. Rather than treat EEG as a single stream, Delta2Gamma decomposes every recording into the five canonical neural rhythms (delta, theta, alpha, beta, gamma). Each band gets its own encoder and projection head. Each also gets a temperature that is predicted adaptively during contrastive training, so bands with different signal statistics are balanced automatically. On the ADFTD cohort under a strict leave-one-subject-out protocol, Delta2Gamma separates Alzheimer's disease from cognitively normal controls with 92.4\% accuracy. This exceeds both supervised backbones and recent dedicated EEG methods.

</details>


### [97] [Physics-Informed and Hybrid Machine Learning in Additive Manufacturing: Application to Fused Filament Fabrication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17246)
*Berkcan Kapusuzoglu,Sankaran Mahadevan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-informed ML strategies for predicting FFF part quality using limited experimental data by incorporating physics knowledge and simulations into deep neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to develop accurate machine learning models for predicting bond quality and porosity of FFF parts while overcoming the limitation of scarce experimental data, and ensure physical consistency in predictions.

Method: Three main strategies: 1) Incorporating physics constraints in DNN loss function, 2) Using physics model outputs as additional DNN inputs, 3) Pre-training DNN with physics model data then updating with experimental data. Eight different combinations of these strategies were investigated.

Result: Combining multiple physics-informed strategies produces accurate ML models even with limited experimental data, and helps enforce physically consistent relationships between bond quality and tensile strength, making porosity predictions physically meaningful.

Conclusion: Physics-informed and hybrid ML strategies effectively incorporate physical knowledge into data-driven models for FFF part quality prediction, enabling accurate modeling with limited experimental data while maintaining physical consistency.

Abstract: This article investigates several physics-informed and hybrid machine learning strategies that incorporate physics knowledge in experimental data-driven deep-learning models for predicting the bond quality and porosity of fused filament fabrication (FFF) parts. Three types of strategies are explored to incorporate physics constraints and multi-physics FFF simulation results into a deep neural network (DNN), thus ensuring consistency with physical laws: (1) incorporate physics constraints within the loss function of the DNN, (2) use physics model outputs as additional inputs to the DNN model, and (3) pre-train a DNN model with physics model input-output and then update it with experimental data. These strategies help to enforce a physically consistent relationship between bond quality and tensile strength, thus making porosity predictions physically meaningful. Eight different combinations of the above strategies are investigated. The results show how the combination of multiple strategies produces accurate machine learning models even with limited experimental data.

</details>


### [98] [Co-RL: Unsupervised Reasoning Emerges from Diverse Cohort in Multi-agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17253)
*Yunhao Yang,Yuexin Bian,Yunjie Tian,Di Fu,Tianjin Huang,Yuanyuan Shi,Ziang Xiao,Nuno Vasconcelos,Yijiang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Co-RL: A multi-agent reinforcement learning framework where multiple independent models train cooperatively using rewards derived from peers, achieving unsupervised reasoning without ground-truth labels while maintaining diversity and preventing training collapse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for reasoning requires expensive ground-truth supervision, which becomes scarce as models surpass human evaluation capabilities. Self-rewarding RL has limitations like reinforcing biases, reducing diversity, and causing training collapse due to self-generated feedback loops.

Method: Co-RL (Cooperative RL): A framework where multiple decoupled models (sharing no parameters) are simultaneously optimized through RL using rewards derived from their peers. Diversity is increased through heterogeneous model families, sizes, and rephrased training samples to reduce correlated errors and self-reinforcing feedback loops.

Result: Across 7 text-only benchmarks for LLMs: 3.0-8.6% average gains. Across 4 multimodal benchmarks for VLMs: 2.3-7.2% average gains. Co-RL consistently outperforms base models and prior label-free approaches, while matching or surpassing supervised methods without any ground-truth labels. The approach maintains behavioral diversity and mitigates training collapse.

Conclusion: Unsupervised reasoning emerges through cooperative multi-agent training. Diversity in model cohorts reduces correlated errors that drive self-reinforcing feedback loops, improving reasoning performance while maintaining diversity and preventing training collapse. The approach enables effective RL-based reasoning without expensive ground-truth supervision.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a powerful approach for improving reasoning in language and vision-language models, yet its strongest successes still depend heavily on ground-truth supervision (e.g., verifiable reward). Such annotations are costly to obtain and become increasingly scarce as reasoning capabilities advance beyond what humans can reliably evaluate. Self-rewarding RL reduces this dependence by enabling models to derive reward signals from their own completions. However, training solely on self-generated feedback can reinforce existing biases and suboptimal behaviors, reduce response diversity, and ultimately lead to homogenized responses and training collapse. In this work, we show that unsupervised reasoning can emerge through cooperative multi-agent training. We introduce Co-RL, a framework in which multiple decoupled models, sharing no parameters, are simultaneously optimized through RL using rewards derived from their peers. We further show that increasing cohort diversity, through heterogeneous model families, sizes, and rephrased training samples, reduces the correlated errors that drive self-reinforcing feedback loops. This diversity consistently improves reasoning performance, maintains behavioral diversity, and mitigates training collapse. Across text-only and multimodal domains, Co-RL consistently outperforms the base models and prior label-free approaches, while matching or surpassing supervised methods, without access to any ground-truth labels. Concretely, Co-RL yields average gains of 3.0-8.6% across seven text-only benchmarks for LLMs and 2.3-7.2% across four multimodal benchmarks for VLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/DrStranded/Co-RL.

</details>


### [99] [Understanding Curriculum Learning in Large Language Models via Cross-Difficulty Optimization Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17268)
*Zhikai Ding,Ziyi Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Curriculum learning effectiveness varies across reasoning tasks, and this paper explains it through cross-difficulty knowledge transfer relationships, leading to a dynamic sampling method that outperforms existing strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current curriculum learning methods for LLM post-training show inconsistent effectiveness across reasoning tasks, with no single curriculum being universally optimal. The fundamental question driving this research is: what determines when curriculum learning works?

Method: 1. Analyze optimization dynamics induced by different curriculum schedules; 2. Show that transfer relationships between difficulty levels characterize these dynamics; 3. Formalize this as "Relative Transfer" - a principled measure of cross-difficulty knowledge transfer; 4. Develop Transfer-aware Dynamic Curriculum Sampling (TDCS) that dynamically adjusts sampling distribution based on estimated transfer relationships.

Result: TDCS consistently outperforms representative scheduling strategies across multiple reasoning benchmarks, different model scales, and various training paradigms. The method provides a unified optimization-based explanation of curriculum learning effectiveness.

Conclusion: The effectiveness of curriculum learning in LLM post-training is determined by cross-difficulty knowledge transfer relationships. The proposed Relative Transfer measure explains optimization dynamics, and TDCS provides a practical dynamic sampling approach that consistently improves performance across diverse settings.

Abstract: Curriculum learning has been widely adopted in the post-training of large language models by organizing training data from easy to hard. However, its effectiveness varies substantially across reasoning tasks, suggesting that no single curriculum is universally optimal and raising a fundamental question: what determines when curriculum learning works? In this paper, we answer this question by analyzing the optimization dynamics induced by different curriculum schedules. We show that the transfer relationship between different difficulty levels characterizes the optimization dynamics induced by curriculum learning, which in turn explains the effectiveness of different curriculum schedules, and formalize this relationship as Relative Transfer, a principled measure of cross-difficulty knowledge transfer. Based on this measurement, we derive Transfer-aware Dynamic Curriculum Sampling (TDCS), which dynamically adjusts the sampling distribution according to the estimated transfer relationship throughout training. Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that TDCS consistently outperforms representative scheduling strategies across different tasks, model scales, and training paradigms. More importantly, our work provides a unified optimization-based explanation of curriculum learning through cross-difficulty transfer.

</details>


### [100] [Rethinking Irregular Time Series Forecasting from the Perspective of Basis Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17284)
*Rongwen Li,Changjian Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DNBNet is a new method for irregular time series forecasting that addresses limitations of existing approaches through debiased neural basis functions, multi-scale decomposition, and a dual-branch decoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Irregular time series forecasting is challenging due to sparse observations and non-uniform sampling. Existing methods using predefined basis functions suffer from asymptotic bias (ignoring timestamp sampling density) and limited adaptability to diverse temporal patterns.

Method: Proposes Debiased Neural Basis-Function Network (DNBNet) with: 1) debiased neural basis-function response mechanism using importance sampling to correct bias and neural networks to parameterize basis functions, 2) multi-scale decomposition module with average pooling, 3) mass-aware fusion mechanism, and 4) dual-branch decoder for forecasting.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of DNBNet and its strong generalizability across diverse irregular time series scenarios.

Conclusion: DNBNet successfully addresses the limitations of existing irregular time series forecasting methods by correcting asymptotic bias, adapting to diverse temporal patterns, and handling data sparsity, achieving superior performance and generalization.

Abstract: Irregular time series forecasting is crucial in many domains, such as healthcare and meteorological observation. However, due to the inherent characteristics of irregular time series, including sparse observations and non-uniform sampling, accurately predicting future dynamics remains challenging. In light of these two characteristics, many existing methods aggregate irregular observations into fixed-dimensional estimated response coefficients through predefined basis functions and use these coefficients as sequence representations. Nevertheless, this modeling paradigm still suffers from two key limitations: (i) a potential non-vanishing asymptotic bias caused by ignoring the sampling density of timestamps; and (ii) the limited adaptability of predefined basis functions to diverse temporal patterns. In this study, we propose a Debiased Neural Basis-Function Network (DNBNet) to address these challenges. Its core is a debiased neural basis-function response mechanism, which corrects asymptotic bias through importance sampling while parameterizing basis functions with neural networks to adapt to diverse temporal patterns. In addition, considering the sparsity of irregular data, we design a novel multi-scale decomposition module based on average pooling, together with a mass-aware fusion mechanism, to obtain richer representations. Finally, a dual-branch decoder is employed for forecasting. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of DNBNet and its strong generalizability across diverse irregular time series scenarios. Our code can be obtained at https://github.com/hnu-vis/DNBNet.

</details>


### [101] [Abra: Scaling Diffusion Image Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17286)
*Kyle Chickering,Wei-An Lin,Swayam Bhanded,Dan Saunders,Akshat Tripathi,Jiaming Song,Shyamal Buch,Xinchen Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Diffusion models scale predictably like LLMs but need 10x more data per parameter (200 tokens/param vs Chinchilla's 20) and are robust to overtraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While compute-optimal scaling laws are well-established for language models, they remain largely unexplored for visual generation models like text-to-image diffusion models, creating a gap in understanding how to optimally train visual models at scale.

Method: Created Abra, a controlled family of flow-matching transformers, and trained them across three orders of magnitude of compute (10¹⁹ to 10²² FLOPs), conducting systematic scaling law studies for text-to-image diffusion models at significantly larger compute budgets than previous works.

Result: Diffusion models scale predictably like LLMs but require 10x more data per parameter (200 image tokens/parameter vs Chinchilla's 20). Unlike LLMs, diffusion models are robust to overtraining, and scaling predictability extends to generative quality metrics, optimal CFG settings, representation quality, and training curve shapes.

Conclusion: Practitioners should prioritize more data over larger models for diffusion models, as they require significantly more data for compute-optimal training than LLMs and are resistant to overtraining, with scaling laws extending beyond loss to downstream quality metrics.

Abstract: Compute-optimal scaling laws guide the training of frontier language models yet remain largely unexplored for visual generation. We present a systematic scaling law study for text-to-image diffusion models using Abra, a controlled family of flow-matching transformers trained across three orders of magnitude worth of compute ($10^{19}$ to $10^{22}$ FLOPs), reaching significantly larger compute budgets than previous works. We demonstrate that diffusion models scale just as predictably as language models but require far more data to train optimally: compute optimality occurs at approximately $200$ image tokens per parameter, ten times the Chinchilla compute-optimal prescription for LLMs. We show that unlike language models, diffusion models are robust to overtraining and that practitioners should err on the side of more data rather than a larger model. Finally, we show that this predictability extends beyond training loss to generative quality metrics, optimal CFG settings, representation quality, and even the shape of the training curves, which collapse onto a universal form.

</details>


### [102] [Beyond MSE: Rethinking the Evaluation Metric and Benchmarking for Irregular Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17293)
*Rongwen Li,Haixin Xie,Xiao Wang,Changjian Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Continuous-time Squared Error (CSE), a new evaluation metric for irregular time-series forecasting that eliminates bias from timestamp sampling distributions, providing more accurate assessment of continuous-time predictive performance than traditional MSE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing irregular time-series forecasting research focuses on model design but insufficiently studies evaluation metrics. Current benchmarks use MSE, which is biased because it's influenced not only by model predictions but also by sample-specific timestamp sampling distributions, leading to inaccurate assessment of continuous-time predictive performance.

Method: Proposes Continuous-time Squared Error (CSE) which employs importance weighting to eliminate the influence of timestamp sampling distributions. Provides theoretical proof that CSE's asymptotic estimation error with respect to continuous-time risk is no greater than that of MSE.

Result: Constructs a systematic benchmark covering synthetic, semi-synthetic, and eight real-world datasets. Experiments show CSE can recover continuous-time risk more accurately than MSE, and relying solely on MSE may not fully reflect models' continuous-time predictive performance in real-world scenarios.

Conclusion: CSE is an effective evaluation metric for irregular time-series forecasting that provides more accurate assessment of continuous-time predictive performance by eliminating bias from timestamp sampling distributions, addressing a critical gap in current evaluation practices.
