Abstract: Existing research on irregular time-series forecasting has primarily focused on model design, while evaluation metrics remain insufficiently studied. Existing benchmarks typically use mean squared error (MSE) as the evaluation metric. We show that, in irregular forecasting, MSE is determined not only by the model prediction but also by the sample-specific timestamp sampling distributions, leading to a biased assessment of the models' continuous-time predictive performance. To address this issue, we propose the Continuous-time Squared Error (CSE), which employs importance weighting to eliminate the influence of the timestamp sampling distributions. We further theoretically prove that CSE's asymptotic estimation error with respect to continuous-time risk is no greater than that of MSE. Finally, we construct a systematic benchmark covering synthetic, semi-synthetic, and eight real-world datasets to validate the effectiveness of CSE and systematically evaluate models' continuous-time predictive performance. Experiments show that CSE can recover continuous-time risk more accurately than MSE, while relying solely on MSE may not fully reflect models' continuous-time predictive performance in real-world scenarios. Our code can be obtained at https://github.com/hnu-vis/ITS-Bench.

</details>


### [103] [Agentic ESOpt: Fine-Tuning Long-Horizon LLM Agents with Minimal GPU Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17310)
*Zhi Zheng,Rongsheng Chen,Yunpeng Ba,Zhenkun Wang,Yee Whye Teh,Wee Sun Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Evolution strategies (ES) are proposed as a better alternative to reinforcement learning for fine-tuning long-horizon LLM agents, offering advantages in model scalability, flexibility, and long-horizon performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement Learning has limitations for long-horizon agentic reasoning: heavyweight training, impractical for larger LLMs, and difficulty with credit assignment over long trajectories.

Method: Propose Agentic ESOpt framework using evolution strategies for full-parameter agentic fine-tuning with parameter-context co-evolution, featuring cosine decay schedule for perturbation scale to improve exploration-adaptation trade-off.

Result: On WebArena-Lite, full-parameter optimization of Qwen-3.5-27B improves baseline by 6.69%. In test-time heuristic design, Agentic ESOpt improves matched baseline in 28 of 36 settings through online prompt-parameter co-evolution.

Conclusion: Evolution strategies offer superior performance over reinforcement learning for long-horizon LLM agent fine-tuning due to better scalability, flexibility, and handling of long trajectories.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has been promising in single-turn LLM fine-tuning. However, long-horizon agentic reasoning introduces increasingly branching interactions and sparse rewards, exposing several limitations of RL: its heavyweight backpropagation-based training stack makes it impractical to fine-tune larger LLMs, and longer-horizon trajectories make credit assignment in RL substantially harder. This paper argues that evolution strategies (ES) can be a better choice for fine-tuning long-horizon LLM agents. Compared with agentic RL, ES offers three key advantages: 1) Model Scalability: ES enables full-parameter optimization with only minimal, inference-level GPU memory, making it possible to fine-tune large LLMs. 2) Flexibility: its lightweight, black-box feedback interface makes ES fine-tuning easy to compose with prompt-space evolution (e.g., skill optimization & test-time compute); and 3) Long-Horizon Scalability: ES performs trajectory-level parameter attribution without decomposing rewards across horizons, yielding better scalability than Agentic RL as the horizon length grows. Based on this insight, we propose Agentic ESOpt, a full-parameter agentic fine-tuning framework tailored to flexible parameter--context co-evolution. At each step, Agentic ESOpt samples perturbations around the current LLM parameters, evaluates the resulting agents with rewards, and applies an online reward-weighted update. To improve the exploration--adaptation trade-off, Agentic ESOpt further introduces a cosine decay schedule of the perturbation scale $σ$. On WebArena-Lite, full-parameter optimization of Qwen-3.5-27B improves the No Skill baseline by 6.69%. In test-time automatic heuristic design, Agentic ESOpt performs online prompt--parameter co-evolution, improving its matched baseline in 28 of 36 settings.

</details>


### [104] [MoFE: A Novel Mixture-of-Experts Framework with Fourier Neural Operators for Cryptocurrency Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17342)
*Bowen Liu,Mingming Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MoFE: A novel Fourier Neural Operator-based Mixture-of-Experts framework for cryptocurrency price forecasting that addresses non-stationarity and phase-lagged predictions by modeling multi-frequency volatility components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cryptocurrency price forecasting is challenging due to non-stationarity, abrupt regime shifts, and complex stochastic dependencies. Traditional deep learning models struggle with these dynamics and often produce phase-lagged predictions.

Method: MoFE integrates Fourier Neural Operators within a Mixture-of-Experts architecture. It models cryptocurrency volatility as superposition of multi-frequency components using specialized adaptive FNO and Convolution dual-domain experts that learn continuous function-to-function mappings. A dynamic gating mechanism enables adaptive strategy switching across market regimes.

Result: The model achieves state-of-the-art performance on Bitcoin datasets (Jan 2020-Dec 2025) for T+1 and T+5 forecasting horizons. It effectively mitigates phase-lag effect, delivers superior Directional Accuracy and Information Coefficient, and generates significant excess returns with high Sharpe ratio in simulated trading.

Conclusion: MoFE successfully addresses key challenges in cryptocurrency forecasting by combining Fourier Neural Operators with Mixture-of-Experts architecture, providing both accurate predictions and practical trading performance improvements.

Abstract: Forecasting cryptocurrency prices remains a formidable challenge due to inherent non-stationarity, abrupt regime shifts, and multi-scale stochastic dependencies. Conventional deep learning models often struggle to capture complex underlying dynamics, frequently resulting in persistent phase-lagged predictions. To address these limitations, we propose MoFE, a novel deep learning framework that integrates Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs) within a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture. Rooted in the theoretical framework of stochastic differential equations, MoFE conceptualizes cryptocurrency volatility as a superposition of multi-frequency components, which includes user network based fundamental growth, mining costs and halving mechanism caused seasonal volatility, and market sentiment-induced chaos. Specifically, specialized adaptive FNO (AFNO) and Convolution dual-domain experts learn continuous function-to-function mappings to encapsulate global spectral trends, cyclical adjustments and microstructures, while a dynamic gating based MoE mechanism enables adaptive strategy switching across diverse market regimes. Extensive experiments on Bitcoin datasets spanning January 2020 to December 2025 demonstrate that MoFE achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance in both T+1 and T+5 forecasting horizons. Notably, the model effectively mitigates the phase-lag effect, delivering superior Directional Accuracy (DA) and Information Coefficient (IC). In high-fidelity simulated trading environments, these predictive gains transfer into significant excess returns and robust risk-adjusted performance, characterized by a high Sharpe ratio.

</details>


### [105] [Tight Bounds for Data-driven Multiple Hyper-parameter Tuning with Structured Loss Function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17343)
*Anh Tuan Nguyen,Viet Anh Nguyen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Established tight pseudo-dimension bounds for multi-dimensional data-driven hyperparameter tuning by refining upper bounds with real algebraic geometry and providing matching lower bounds through a multi-regime framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generalization guarantees for data-driven algorithm design are challenging due to implicit, non-smooth dependence of model performance on hyperparameters. Current multi-dimensional bounds under piecewise-polynomial assumptions are theoretically loose and lack comprehensive lower bounds.

Method: 1) Refined learning-theoretic upper bounds using real algebraic geometry, analyzing invariant connected sign cells during block elimination rather than isolated sign vectors to avoid topological over-counting. 2) Developed multi-regime lower-bound framework that disentangles combinatorial and algebraic capacities, constructing shattered problem instances across distinct regimes. 3) Extended topological framework to accommodate general bi-level validation-loss tuning and broader semi-algebraic applications.

Result: Established tight pseudo-dimension bounds for multi-dimensional data-driven tuning, proving that the refined upper bounds are tightly saturated through constructed lower bounds, providing strictly sharper sample complexities than previous approaches.

Conclusion: The paper resolves the challenge of establishing tight generalization guarantees for data-driven algorithm design by providing both refined upper bounds and matching lower bounds through a novel topological and algebraic framework, with extensions to broader applications in hyperparameter tuning.

Abstract: Data-driven algorithm design frames hyperparameter tuning as a statistical learning problem, but establishing generalization guarantees remains challenging due to the implicit, non-smooth dependence of model performance on hyperparameters. Existing multi-dimensional bounds under piecewise-polynomial assumptions remain theoretically loose and lack comprehensive lower bounds. We resolve this by establishing tight pseudo-dimension bounds for multi-dimensional data-driven tuning. First, we refine the learning-theoretic upper bound using real algebraic geometry; by analyzing invariant connected sign cells during block elimination rather than isolated sign vectors, we avoid topological over-counting to derive strictly sharper sample complexities. Second, we present a multi-regime lower-bound framework that disentangles combinatorial and algebraic capacities. By constructing shattered problem instances across distinct regimes, we prove our upper bounds are tightly saturated. Finally, we extend our topological framework to accommodate general bi-level validation-loss tuning and broader semi-algebraic applications.

</details>


### [106] [Repetition as Reinforcement: Enhancing Sample Efficiency via Instant Episode Repetition in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17347)
*Hoda Yamani,Yuning Xing,Koen van Rijnsoever,Bruce A. MacDonald,Henry Williams*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Instant Episode Repetition (IER) is a simple mechanism that improves RL sample efficiency by immediately repeating successful action sequences during environment interaction, outperforming standard methods and self-imitation learning baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inspired by biological learning where repetition strengthens memory and skills, the paper aims to improve RL sample efficiency by directly reinforcing successful behaviors through immediate repetition during data collection.

Method: Instant Episode Repetition (IER): Upon identifying a high-reward episode, the agent immediately repeats the same action sequence for a fixed number of subsequent episodes, directly influencing data collection rather than just training updates. Integrated into SAC and TD3 algorithms.

Result: Experimental results show IER improves learning performance over standard baselines and self-imitation-based methods on continuous-control benchmarks including MuJoCo, DeepMind Control Suite, and a real-world robotic manipulator task.

Conclusion: IER is a simple yet effective mechanism that leverages biological repetition principles to improve RL sample efficiency by directly reinforcing successful behaviors through immediate episode repetition during environment interaction.

Abstract: Repetition is a fundamental mechanism in human learning, where revisiting successful experiences strengthens memory, consolidates skills, and improves future performance. Motivated by this biological principle, we introduce Instant Episode Repetition (IER), a simple and novel mechanism that improves sample efficiency by immediately repeating action sequences from successful episodes during environment interaction. Unlike conventional approaches such as Experience Replay and Self-Imitation Learning (SIL), which passively reuse past experience during training updates, IER directly influences the data collection process. Upon identifying a high-reward episode, the agent repeats its action sequence for a fixed number of subsequent episodes, reinforcing valuable behaviors through renewed interaction with the environment. We integrate IER into state-of-the-art SAC and TD3 algorithms and evaluate its effectiveness on continuous-control benchmarks, including MuJoCo, the DeepMind Control Suite, and a real-world dynamic object translation task with a robotic manipulator. Experimental results demonstrate that this simple mechanism improves learning performance over standard and self-imitation-based baselines.

</details>


### [107] [CORAM: Coherent Orthogonal Rotation for Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17366)
*Xinyi Sui,Ziran Liu,Nam Ling,Wei Wang,Wei Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CORAM is a model merging method that partitions weight matrices, uses singular value decompositions in base-model frames, merges task-specific factors on manifolds, and applies adaptive amplification to improve over existing merging techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing model merging methods like OrthoMerge use linear arithmetic in Euclidean weight space or orthogonal transforms, which cannot properly handle the geometry of updates or change singular values, limiting their effectiveness.

Method: CORAM partitions each target weight matrix into row slices, represents expert slices via SVD in the base-model SVD frame, merges task-specific factors on their corresponding manifolds, applies amplification (λ=κĉ) with ĉ estimated from norms, uses spread slicing to distribute highly updated rows, and includes residual pathways for non-target layers.

Result: CORAM improves over OrthoMerge by 0.25 to 1.35 points and matches or exceeds strongest weight-space baselines across four suites covering three model families (3B to 9B scales) with language and vision-language experts, with its amplification rule staying within 0.72 points of the best swept value.

Conclusion: CORAM provides an effective model merging approach that properly handles the geometry of updates through manifold operations and adaptive amplification, outperforming existing methods while avoiding expensive hyperparameter sweeps.

Abstract: Merging finetuned models combines specialized capabilities without joint training or access to the original data. Most methods operate by linear arithmetic in Euclidean weight space, which cannot carry the geometry of the update. Orthogonal Model Merging (OrthoMerge) uses a single orthogonal transform for each weight matrix, but such a transform cannot change singular values. We propose CORAM, which partitions each target matrix into row slices, represents every expert slice by its singular value decomposition in the corresponding base-model SVD frame, and merges the task-specific factors on their corresponding manifolds. Because manifold averaging contracts the merged update, CORAM applies an amplification coefficient $λ=κ\hat{c}$. The scale c_hat is estimated from the expert and merged update norms and is approximately $\sqrt{N}$ for $N$ experts with comparable update magnitudes. The restoration strength kappa is selected from the dispersion of expert updates without evaluating candidate merged models. This rule remains within 0.72 points of the best swept value on all evaluated suites. CORAM also includes spread slicing to distribute highly updated rows across slices and a residual pathway for non-target layers. Across four suites covering three model families, 3B to 9B scales, and language and vision-language experts, CORAM improves over OrthoMerge by 0.25 to 1.35 points and matches or exceeds the strongest weight-space baselines.

</details>


### [108] [Pathology Transport: Optimal-Transport Explanations for Clinical Data, and When Their Heatmaps (Fail to) Localize Disease](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17370)
*Lalit Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Optimal-transport generative models can produce clinical explanations, but synthetic-to-real gap exists: compelling heatmaps on artificial lesions don't guarantee real disease localization, while supervised methods remain more reliable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To build explainable clinical AI systems using generative models that can model distributions of healthy and diseased patients, providing geometric explanations rather than just probing classifiers.

Method: Develop optimal-transport rectified flow trained between two clinical distributions, tested on tabular tumor biomarkers (Breast Cancer Wisconsin) and chest X-rays with synthetic lesions and real RSNA radiologist annotations.

Result: Tabular data: single flow yields counterfactuals, unsupervised malignancy score (AUROC 0.91), and label-free attribution correlating with supervised classifier. Chest X-rays: transport heatmap shows population-level signal only; reconstruction-based variant localizes synthetic lesions (0.52 pointing game) but fails on real data (chance level), while supervised Grad-CAM outperforms.

Conclusion: Generative models provide interpretable clinical explanations, but synthetic-to-real gap exists where label-free heatmaps that work on planted lesions don't guarantee real disease localization; supervised methods remain more reliable for actual clinical applications.

Abstract: Generative models promise a route to explainable clinical AI: rather than probe a classifier, model the distributions of healthy and diseased patients and read explanations off the geometry between them. We build such a system - an optimal-transport rectified flow trained between two clinical distributions - and use it to ask a pointed question the field too rarely tests: do the resulting explanation heatmaps actually localize disease? On tabular tumour biomarkers (Breast Cancer Wisconsin) a single flow yields per-patient counterfactuals, an unsupervised malignancy score (AUROC 0.91; 0.93 +/- 0.01 across five seeds), and a label-free attribution that agrees with a supervised classifier (r ~ 0.5) - a compact, honest interpretability engine, though it never out-predicts logistic regression. Moving to chest X-rays, we show the transport heatmap is a population-level signal, not a localiser; a reconstruction-based, identity-preserving variant does localize synthetic lesions (pointing game 0.52), yet on real RSNA radiologist boxes it collapses to chance while only supervised Grad-CAM stays above it. The central result is a synthetic-to-real gap: label-free heatmaps that look compelling on planted lesions are not evidence of real localisation. We contribute a reusable optimal-transport recipe for generative explanations and a controlled benchmark for stress-testing whether they localize.

</details>


### [109] [Integrating Novelty and Surprise for Experience Prioritization and Exploration in Image-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17373)
*Hoda Yamani,Henry Williams,Bruce A. MacDonald*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NSPER and NSPER+R are novel experience replay methods that combine novelty and surprise signals to improve sample efficiency in image-based reinforcement learning, outperforming existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sample efficiency is a major challenge in reinforcement learning, especially with high-dimensional visual inputs. Traditional experience sampling methods are often random or suboptimal, leading to redundant updates and slow learning. While Prioritized Experience Replay (PER) addresses reuse of high-value transitions and intrinsic rewards promote exploration, their integration has not been extensively studied.

Method: The paper introduces Novelty and Surprise Prioritized Experience Replay (NSPER), which uses novelty to capture underrepresented states and surprise to expose gaps in the agent's understanding. The method is further extended to NSPER+R, which integrates these signals as intrinsic rewards to jointly improve replay quality and exploration.

Result: Experiments on DeepMind Control Suite tasks demonstrate that both NSPER and NSPER+R improve training efficiency and convergence speed compared to existing methods in image-based reinforcement learning.

Conclusion: The integration of novelty and surprise signals in experience replay effectively addresses both sample efficiency and exploration challenges in visual RL domains, offering improved performance over current state-of-the-art approaches.

Abstract: Sample efficiency is a central challenge in reinforcement learning (RL), particularly in image-based domains where agents must learn from high-dimensional visual inputs. Traditional sampling often relies on random or suboptimal experience selection, leading to redundant updates and slow learning. Improving efficiency requires mechanisms that prioritize informative experiences while also encouraging effective exploration. Prioritized Experience Replay (PER) addresses part of this challenge by reusing high-value transitions, while intrinsic rewards promote the exploration of novel or uncertain states. However, their integration has not been extensively studied. This paper introduces Novelty and Surprise Prioritized Experience Replay (NSPER), which uses novelty to capture underrepresented states and surprise to expose gaps in the agent's understanding of the environment. We further extend this with NSPER+R, integrating these signals as intrinsic rewards to jointly improve replay quality and exploration. Experiments on DeepMind Control Suite tasks show that NSPER and NSPER+R improve training efficiency and convergence speed compared to existing methods in image-based RL.

</details>


### [110] [GUPO: Gradient Uncertainty-aware Policy Optimization for Post-Training Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17411)
*Peizheng Guo,Jianqi Zhang,Xingyu Zhang,Yun Fan,Jiahuan Zhou,Changwen Zheng,Wenwen Qiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GUPO improves GRPO for LLM reasoning by modeling group gradient uncertainty to resolve conflicting gradient directions during policy optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO averages group gradients from different queries in mini-batches, but these gradients often point in conflicting directions, leading to less effective policy updates. Current GRPO treats gradients as deterministic without accounting for their reliability differences during aggregation.

Method: Proposes Gradient Uncertainty-Aware Policy Optimization (GUPO), which models each group gradient as a random variable using Bayesian formulation, estimates its probability distribution, derives gradient uncertainty with Dirichlet-based formulation, and uses this uncertainty to calibrate each gradient's contribution during aggregation.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of GUPO in improving policy optimization for LLM reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: GUPO addresses the limitation of standard GRPO by accounting for gradient uncertainty, providing a more reliable aggregated update direction when group gradients conflict, leading to more effective policy optimization for post-training LLMs.

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has become a widely used approach for post-training Large Language Models (LLMs) for reasoning. In GRPO, the group gradients induced by different queries within the same mini-batch are directly averaged to form the policy update. However, these group gradients can point in conflicting directions. Our empirical analysis suggests that group-gradient conflicts tend to be associated with less effective policy updates, motivating the need for a reliable aggregated update direction under such conflicts. Standard GRPO aggregation treats the realized group gradients as deterministic contributions and does not account for differences in their reliability during aggregation. To address this issue, we propose Gradient Uncertainty-Aware Policy Optimization (GUPO), which models each group gradient as a random variable under a Bayesian formulation and estimates its probability distribution. GUPO then derives gradient uncertainty using a Dirichlet-based formulation and uses it to calibrate the contribution of each group gradient during aggregation. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of GUPO.

</details>


### [111] [General Semantic Knowledge Infusion for Spatio-Temporal Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17440)
*Mattis thor Straten,Yannick Wolker,Steffen Strohm,Prathvish Mithare,Ralf Krestel,Matthias Renz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Incorporating external knowledge graphs like Wikidata into traffic forecasting GNNs improves prediction accuracy by adding semantic context beyond physical sensor connectivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GNNs for spatio-temporal traffic forecasting are limited by relying only on sensor proximity or road-network topology, lacking deeper contextual understanding of the urban environment.

Method: Create semantic subgraphs from general-purpose knowledge graphs (Wikidata) around traffic sensors, generate knowledge graph embeddings capturing relationships like nearby points of interest and administrative hierarchies, then fuse these embeddings with conventional traffic sensor graphs to provide semantics-informed adjacency matrices.

Result: Experiments show that integrating external knowledge provides additional information not conveyed by street network data alone, enhancing prediction accuracy of established traffic forecasting models.

Conclusion: Data fusion of general-purpose knowledge graphs and sensor networks improves traffic forecasting accuracy and offers potential pathways for better model interpretability, demonstrating the value of semantic context beyond physical connectivity.

Abstract: Although Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have made significant advances in spatio-temporal traffic forecasting, their performance is limited when they rely solely on sensor proximity or road-network topology. This paper presents a spatio-temporal prediction framework, developed to incorporate knowledge in various forms. This framework aims to improve sensor-level, contextual understanding of the environment. A general-purpose knowledge graph (e.g., Wikidata) is used to create semantic subgraphs around traffic sensors and generate knowledge graph embeddings that capture meaningful relationships, such as nearby points of interest, administrative hierarchies, and the functional roles of locations. These embeddings are then fused with conventional traffic sensor graphs to provide additional adjacency matrices informed by semantics. This allows GNNs to learn the semantic context beyond physical connectivity. This study differs from previous research in two key ways. Firstly, rather than proposing a novel GNN architecture, it demonstrates the general impact of external knowledge on prediction accuracy. Secondly, experiments with well-established traffic forecasting approaches show that external knowledge provides additional information that street network data alone cannot convey. The results show that integrating data from general-purpose knowledge graphs and sensor networks through data fusion can enhance the prediction accuracy of traffic forecasting models, and offers a potential pathway toward improved interpretability.

</details>


### [112] [Causal Local States: Scalable Simultaneous Causal Network Inference and Forecasting for Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17452)
*Jonas Braun,Fabian Fischbach,Daniel Köglmayr,Sebastian Baur,Christoph Räth*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CLS framework simultaneously infers causal networks and forecasts dynamics by selecting minimal neighbor sets for optimal prediction at each node.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models are black boxes that lack interpretability of driving interactions, while causal discovery methods don't optimize for prediction. Existing combined approaches fail with single global parameters that can't handle heterogeneous systems.

Method: For each node independently, select smallest set of neighbors enabling near-optimal prediction. Combine resulting neighborhoods to forecast full system dynamics, creating an approximate Granger-causal interaction network.

Result: Achieves high-fidelity reconstruction of underlying networks and forecasting performance on par with models supplied with the true network, demonstrated on three benchmarks of increasing difficulty.

Conclusion: CLS provides a step toward explainable and scalable forecasting of complex systems by simultaneously addressing structure inference and prediction.

Abstract: Machine learning methods predict many real-world systems with remarkable accuracy, but they are typically treated as black boxes that offer no insight into which interactions drive the dynamics. Causal discovery methods reconstruct the interaction network from observational data, but without regard to whether the inferred structure supports prediction. Existing approaches combining both tasks rely on a single global hyperparameter, such as a causal threshold or a fixed neighborhood size, which cannot recover the structure of heterogeneous systems. Here we introduce causal local states (CLS), a framework that simultaneously infers an approximate Granger-causal interaction network and forecasts the system dynamics. For each node independently, we select the smallest set of neighbors that allows a predictive model to forecast the node near-optimally, and the resulting neighborhoods are then combined for a forecast of the full system. On three benchmarks of increasing difficulty, we achieve reconstruction of the underlying networks with high fidelity and forecasts on par with a model that is supplied with the true network, providing a step toward explainable and scalable forecasting of complex systems.

</details>


### [113] [Evaluating RL Explainability Methods by How Much They Help Fix Bugs in Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17524)
*Ram Rachum,Yotam Amitai,Bálint Gyevnár,Reuth Mirsky,Cameron Allen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes EvalXRL, a new benchmark for evaluating Explainable RL methods by measuring how well their explanations help diagnose and fix malfunctioning RL agents using LLM-based coding agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current XRL evaluations rely on limited metrics (faithfulness, compactness) and human-grounded proxies, lacking assessment of practical utility for diagnosing and fixing real RL agent problems.

Method: EvalXRL uses LLM coding agents to apply different XRL methods to diagnose held-out malfunctions in RL agents, then repair them. It iterates across environment × malfunction × XRL method tuples using RL reward signals to score methods in a closed-loop interactive process.

Result: Proposes first head-to-head comparison framework for XRL methods in closed-loop usage, enabling evaluation based on actual diagnostic and repair effectiveness rather than just theoretical metrics.

Conclusion: The paper presents a novel benchmark (EvalXRL) that shifts XRL evaluation from static metrics to practical utility assessment, promising more meaningful comparisons of explanation methods' real-world effectiveness.

Abstract: This preliminary paper outlines a planned evaluation benchmark for Explainable Reinforcement Learning (XRL) methods. Current evaluations rely on functionally-grounded metrics like faithfulness and compactness, and on human-grounded proxies like subjective ratings or prediction accuracy. We suggest evaluating XRL methods by how effectively their generated explanations help to diagnose and fix malfunctioning reinforcement learning (RL) agents. We propose EvalXRL, a benchmark in which a Large Language Model (LLM) coding agent uses different XRL methods to diagnose a held-out malfunction in an RL agent, and then repair it.
  Our proposed benchmark iterates across (environment $\times$ malfunction $\times$ XRL method) tuples and uses the reward signal of the RL agents to form a final score for each XRL method. The coding agent may use the method interactively: invoke the XRL method, process its output, form new hypotheses on what is broken, and invoke the method again with parameters adjusted for testing these hypotheses. This closed-loop structure may be described as a simplified version of the scientific method. Some XRL methods provide self-evaluations that follow this pattern; we propose the first head-to-head comparison of multiple XRL methods in closed-loop usage.

</details>


### [114] [No Gaussian Required: Contrastive Inverse Dynamics for JEPA World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17542)
*Jack Boylan,Chris Hokamp*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AC-MTM introduces an action-contrastive inverse dynamics objective as a distribution-free anti-collapse mechanism for JEPAs, replacing prescribed latent geometry with transition-based discrimination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: JEPAs need anti-collapse mechanisms to avoid trivial constant encoder solutions. Current methods (like SIGReg in LeWM) prescribe latent geometry (isotropic Gaussian), but this may bottleneck performance when the environment has complex geometry that doesn't match the prescribed distribution.

Method: AC-MTM keeps LeWM's forward latent-prediction objective and adds a training-only inverse-dynamics head with Action-NCE: each latent transition must identify the action that produced it among other actions in the batch. This creates a discrimination task that collapsed encoders fail, providing anti-collapse pressure from the transition data itself rather than prescribed distributions.

Result: On four standard pixel-control tasks, AC-MTM trains stably from scratch and matches SIGReg. On the harder multi-object OGBench Visual Scene task, AC-MTM achieves 80.0±2.0% success vs 58.0±2.0% for SIGReg, improving by 20-24 points per seed. Requires no target network, stop-gradient, pretrained encoder, or reconstruction objective.

Conclusion: Action-contrastive inverse dynamics provides an effective distribution-free anti-collapse signal for JEPAs, eliminating the need to prescribe latent geometry and showing superior performance on complex multi-object tasks where prescribed distributions become a bottleneck.

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) learn world models by predicting future embeddings, but the objective admits a trivial solution of a constant encoder, so every practical system adds an anti-collapse mechanism (LeCun, 2022; Assran et al., 2023; Bardes et al., 2022; 2024). LeWorldModel (LeWM) prevents collapse with SIGReg, a regularizer that forces the latent distribution to match an isotropic Gaussian: the representation is stabilized by prescribing what it must look like, independently of the environment it models. We argue that the anti-collapse pressure can instead come from the transition data itself. Action-Contrastive Masked Transition Modeling (AC-MTM) keeps LeWM's forward latent-prediction objective and adds a training-only inverse-dynamics head trained with Action-NCE: each latent transition must identify the action that produced it among the other actions in the batch, a discrimination task that a collapsed encoder provably fails. The inverse branch is discarded after training, leaving test-time encoding, forward prediction, planning, and compute identical to LeWM. On four standard pixel-control tasks under a matched planning protocol, AC-MTM trains stably from scratch and matches SIGReg on average. On the harder multi-object OGBench Visual Scene task, results are consistent with the prescribed geometry becoming a bottleneck: AC-MTM reaches 80.0$\pm$2.0% success versus 58.0$\pm$2.0% for SIGReg, improving by 20-24 points in each training seed. A single 50-episode random-policy run gives a 52% baseline estimate. Contrastive inverse dynamics thus provides a distribution-free anti-collapse signal that requires no target network, stop-gradient, pretrained encoder, or reconstruction objective, and we characterize the action-space and observability assumptions under which it holds. We make our code available at https://github.com/jackboyla/action-contrastive-jepa

</details>


### [115] [Domain-Adapted Molecular Language Models for Efficient Search of Make-on-Demand Libraries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17567)
*Henrik Wille,Luis-Finley Schütz,Felix Strieth-Kalthoff*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Molecular language models need explicit domain adaptation to outperform traditional molecular fingerprints in virtual screening across different chemistry domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While pretrained molecular language models are increasingly used as molecular encoders, their practical suitability for molecular discovery across different domains remains unclear, prompting a systematic benchmarking study.

Method: Benchmarked four molecular language models across six virtual molecular libraries spanning drug discovery, organic materials, and catalysis, comparing native embeddings against molecular fingerprints and evaluating domain adaptation strategies.

Result: Native molecular language model embeddings show inconsistent performance across libraries, while molecular fingerprints provide a consistently strong baseline. Explicit domain adaptation through fine-tuning significantly improves representation quality and sample efficiency, with adapted encoders becoming top performers.

Conclusion: Molecular representation quality strongly depends on the target domain, and explicit domain adaptation can substantially improve the practical utility of molecular foundation models for virtual screening and self-driving laboratories.

Abstract: Pretrained molecular language models are increasingly used as molecular encoders for learning structure-property relationships. However, their practical suitability for molecular discovery within and beyond their pretraining domain remains unclear. Herein, we systematically benchmark four molecular language models across six virtual molecular libraries spanning drug discovery, organic materials, and catalysis. Native molecular language model embeddings show substantial variation in discovery performance across libraries, whereas molecular fingerprints provide a consistently strong and robust baseline. Consistent with a potential domain-representation mismatch, we show that explicit domain adaptation substantially improves representation performance. Fine-tuning molecular language model encoders on structures from the target virtual library consistently improves sample efficiency, with several adapted encoders emerging as the top-performing representations across the benchmark tasks. These results show that molecular representation quality depends strongly on the target domain and that explicit adaptation can improve the practical utility of molecular foundation models. More broadly, our findings establish domain-adapted molecular representations as a promising strategy for sample-efficient adaptive decision making in virtual screening and self-driving laboratories.

</details>


### [116] [OOD Detection for EEG-based Machine Learning in High-Risk Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17620)
*Philipp Bomatter,Henry Gouk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a benchmark for EEG OOD detection, evaluates various methods, and assesses their practical impact on clinical prediction tasks while disentangling OOD detection from model uncertainty estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models for EEG analysis are promising but vulnerable to distribution shifts, which can cause catastrophic failures when encountering OOD data. OOD detection methods can mitigate these risks but remain under-explored for EEG, and current evaluations typically ignore their practical impact on downstream applications.

Method: The authors introduce a benchmark for EEG OOD detection, evaluate a broad range of methods, and assess their value in two clinical downstream prediction tasks. They also disentangle OOD detection capabilities from model uncertainty estimation capabilities.

Result: The results provide insights about the current state of the art for EEG OOD detection and model uncertainty estimation, and demonstrate how complementary methods for both aspects can be combined to form a robust safety net for real-world deployment.

Conclusion: The paper bridges the gap between isolated OOD detection evaluations and practical application impact, showing that combining complementary methods for OOD detection and uncertainty estimation can enable safer deployment of EEG-based ML models in real-world, high-risk clinical applications.

Abstract: Machine learning models for electroencephalography (EEG) analysis show great promise across a wide range of applications, but their deployment in high-risk domains is hindered by their vulnerability to distribution shifts. Encountering out-of-distribution (OOD) data can lead to catastrophic, overconfident predictive failures. While OOD detection methods can mitigate these risks, they remain heavily under-explored for EEG. Moreover, evaluations in the broader literature typically evaluate OOD detection performance in isolation, ignoring their practical impact on downstream applications. To bridge this gap, we introduce a benchmark for EEG OOD detection, evaluate a broad range of methods, and furthermore evaluate their value in two clinical downstream prediction task. Our results disentangle OOD detection and model uncertainty estimation capabilities, which are frequently conflated in the literature, provide actionable insights about the current state of the art for EEG OOD detection and model uncertainty estimation, and demonstrate how complementary methods for both aspects can be combined to form a robust safety net for the deployment of EEG-based machine learning models in real-world applications.

</details>


### [117] [rl-triton: High-Performance Triton GPU Kernels for Reinforcement Learning Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17641)
*Lars Simon Zehnder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: rl-triton is an open-source GPU kernel library that unifies 7 RL credit assignment algorithms into a single associative scan framework with O(log T) parallel computation, achieving 1.6-5.7× speedup over vectorized torch.compile baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the need for high-performance GPU implementations of RL credit assignment algorithms. Existing implementations are often inefficient and scattered across different codebases, lacking unified optimization for massively parallel simulation regimes with thousands of environments and short rollouts.

Method: The authors develop a unified associative scan framework that recasts seven distinct RL estimation algorithms (GAE, V-Trace, Retrace(λ), TD(λ) returns, discounted returns, eligibility traces, and episodic prefix sums) as instances of a single first-order linear recurrence. They implement algorithm-specific fused Triton kernels that construct recurrence coefficients on-chip, verified algebraically with explicit handling of terminated/truncated episodes.

Result: The library achieves 1.6-5.70× speedup over vectorized torch.compile baseline in massively parallel simulation regimes. Speedups generally increase with longer sequence lengths as the baseline requires more HBM round-trips with log T growth. All algorithms share the same associative scan operator.

Conclusion: rl-triton provides a high-performance, unified framework for RL credit assignment that significantly outperforms existing implementations through efficient GPU parallelization using associative scans, with algebraic verification and explicit episode handling ensuring correctness.

Abstract: We present rl-triton, an open-source library of high-performance GPU kernels for reinforcement learning credit assignment, implemented in Triton. The core contribution is a unified associative scan framework that recasts seven distinct RL estimation algorithms - Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE), V-Trace, Retrace($λ$), TD($λ$) returns, discounted returns, eligibility traces, and episodic prefix sums - as instances of a single first-order linear recurrence solved in $O(\log T)$ parallel steps. All algorithms share the same associative scan operator, with algorithm-specific fused Triton kernels constructing their recurrence coefficients on-chip. We verify the associative operator algebraically and define the treatment of terminated and truncated episodes explicitly. Benchmarks show a 1.6-5.70$\times$ full-call speedup over a vectorized torch.compile baseline in the massively parallel simulation regime (thousands of environments, short rollouts). The reported range covers all seven algorithms on both GPUs, both with and without per-step truncation handling. For most algorithms, speedups increase at longer sequence lengths, as the baseline requires more scan stages as $\log T$ grows, each adding an intermediate HBM round-trip. The library is available at https://github.com/simonsays1980/rl-triton.

</details>


### [118] [Elimination Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17646)
*Mian Huang,Xueqin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EG is a framework for analyzing when locally optimal solutions can be realized by shared deployment rules, focusing on understanding what distinctions get lost in elimination/compression and whether resulting defects are visible to the intended task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when locally optimal objects can be realized by shared deployment rules, and to analyze what distinctions are lost during elimination/compression processes, whether those losses affect task performance, and whether changes can repair defects.

Method: Develops Elimination Geometry (EG) as a typed, native-loss, audit-oriented framework that synthesizes tools from geometry, optimization, information theory, statistics, and machine learning. Separates local solvability, global realizability, and finite-sample certifiability.

Result: Provides formal results addressing regular, coordination, singular, compositional, and resource-limited mechanisms. Applications include sparse model selection, distribution-free prediction, treatment policies, routed expert systems, and learned score fields.

Conclusion: EG enables structural diagnosis linked to finite-data authorization, mechanism-matched intervention, and independent validation. Certificates can guide architecture repair while documenting failures. Framework requires fixed deployment contracts and validation rules before attributing performance limits to architecture.

Abstract: This monograph develops elimination geometry (EG), a typed, native-loss, audit-oriented framework for studying when locally optimal objects can be realized by a shared deployment rule. Elimination and compression may erase distinctions required by prediction, inference, control, or representation. EG asks which distinctions are lost, whether the induced defect is visible to the declared task, and whether changing information, architecture, action space, or deployment domain can repair it. EG separates local solvability, global realizability, and finite-sample certifiability. It derives native defects from the original objective and distinguishes architecture obstruction from model approximation, generalization, and implementation error. The monograph synthesizes tools from geometry, optimization, information theory, statistics, and machine learning into interfaces for integrability, representation admissibility, resource constraints, observational overlap, and common deployment. Formal results address regular, coordination, singular, compositional, and resource-limited mechanisms with explicit antecedents and claim boundaries. Applications include sparse model selection, distribution-free prediction, observational treatment policies, routed expert and retrieval systems, and learned score fields. Obstruction-Aware Learning and Inference links structural diagnosis to finite-data authorization, mechanism-matched intervention, and independent validation. Reproducible synthetic and real-data studies illustrate how certificates can guide architecture repair while recording failed gates and unresolved cases. The framework requires the deployment contract, native endpoint, competing explanations, information and compute budgets, and validation rule to be fixed before a persistent performance floor is attributed to architecture.

</details>


### [119] [Picard Proximal Monte Carlo for Parallel Bayesian Imaging with Score-Based Generative Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17666)
*Deliang Wei,Evan Bell,Wenhan Guo,Yifan Chen,Yu Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PiX-MC is a time-parallel posterior sampling framework for Bayesian imaging inverse problems that uses proximal Langevin dynamics and Picard iteration to enable parallelization across discretization nodes and multi-GPU support, achieving significant speedups over standard sequential samplers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bayesian imaging inverse problems require sampling from high-dimensional posteriors, but current score-based and diffusion models have sequential sampling procedures that are computationally expensive for large-scale imaging applications, creating a need for more efficient parallel sampling methods.

Method: PiX-MC combines proximal Langevin dynamics with Picard iteration. The proximal-likelihood formulation leverages efficient, problem-specific proximal operators for imaging likelihoods, while Picard refinement exposes parallelism across discretization nodes. The framework includes multi-block and annealed variants for improved scalability and performance.

Result: PiX-MC substantially reduces wall-clock time while preserving reconstruction quality. On a 512×512×80 sparse-view CT problem, the annealed multi-block PiX-MC achieves up to 50× runtime speedup over standard Langevin samplers using eight GPUs.

Conclusion: PiX-MC provides an effective parallel posterior sampling framework for Bayesian imaging that addresses the computational bottleneck of sequential samplers, with proven convergence guarantees and practical multi-GPU scalability, making it suitable for large-scale imaging applications.

Abstract: Bayesian imaging inverse problems often require sampling from high-dimensional posterior distributions. While recent score-based and diffusion models provide expressive Bayesian priors, their sampling procedures remain inherently sequential and computationally expensive for large-scale imaging applications. We propose PiX-MC, a time-parallel posterior sampling framework based on proximal Langevin dynamics and Picard iteration. The proximal-likelihood formulation exploits the fact that many imaging likelihoods admit efficient, problem-specific proximal operators, while Picard refinement exposes parallelism across discretization nodes and naturally supports multi-GPU implementation. To further improve practical scalability and sampling performance, we develop multi-block and annealed variants of the proposed framework. We establish convergence guarantees under transparent assumptions, accommodating non-log-concave posteriors, imperfect learned score models, multi-block implementations, and annealing schedules. Experiments on a diverse collection of imaging inverse problems demonstrate that PiX-MC substantially reduces wall-clock time while preserving reconstruction quality. On a $512\times512\times80$ sparse-view computed tomography (CT) problem, annealed multi-block PiX-MC achieves up to a $50\times$ runtime speedup over the standard Langevin sampler using eight GPUs.

</details>


### [120] [Conformal Prediction for Molecular Properties under Label Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17678)
*Hyeonsu Lee,Juyeon Kim,Erkhembayar Jadamba,Seungjin Choi,Hyunjin Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A conformal prediction framework for drug discovery that handles distribution shifts by weighting conformal scores with label probability ratios, producing statistically rigorous prediction intervals without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Drug discovery is costly and failure-prone, with AI reliability undermined by distribution shift when experimental conditions diverge from training data. Conventional point predictions offer limited guidance for high-stakes experimental design.

Method: A conformal prediction framework tailored to label shift that weights conformal scores using marginal label probability ratios to produce statistically rigorous prediction intervals without requiring retraining.

Result: The method enables robust uncertainty quantification even when property distributions drift, directly addressing pervasive obstacles in applying AI to real-world drug development and providing actionable confidence measures.

Conclusion: The approach enhances AI trustworthiness in drug discovery, aligns with regulatory demands for transparency and uncertainty reporting, and supports more reliable decision-making in development pipelines.

Abstract: Drug discovery and development underpins healthcare but remains costly and failure-prone. A critical bottleneck lies in predicting molecular properties such as solubility, potency, and toxicity, which directly determine whether a candidate can advance from preclinical to clinical trials. Artificial Intelligence (AI) has accelerated this process, yet its reliability is often undermined by distribution shift, as experimental conditions frequently diverge from training data. In addition, conventional point predictions provide only single-value estimates, offering limited guidance for high-stakes experimental design. We address these challenges with a conformal prediction framework tailored to label shift. By weighting conformal scores using marginal label probability ratios, our method produces statistically rigorous prediction intervals without retraining. This enables robust uncertainty quantification even when property distributions drift, directly tackling one of the most pervasive obstacles to applying AI in real-world drug development. By moving beyond accuracy alone to provide actionable confidence measures, our approach enhances the trustworthiness of AI-driven predictions. This further aligns predictive modeling with regulatory demands for transparency and uncertainty reporting and ultimately supports more reliable decision-making in billion-dollar development pipelines.

</details>


### [121] [Cross-View Correspondence Is a Measurement Intervention: Two-Sided Validation for Agent Evaluation and Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17713)
*Zhen Zhang,Ahmad Hafez,Amr Alanwar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cross-view correspondence in agent evaluation is a measurement intervention that must be validated, as improper handling can create false sensitivity/invariance and leave mechanism labels/learning credit unidentified.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent evaluation practices treat cross-view correspondence (transforming outputs for comparison) as neutral preprocessing, but this is problematic because: 1) omitting correspondence can manufacture false sensitivity, 2) over-aggressive mapping can manufacture false invariance, and 3) multiple optimal correspondences can leave mechanism labels and signed learning credit unidentified.

Method: Developed a validity theory with three components: two-sided validation (nuisance removal + response preservation), all-optima identification of downstream conclusions, and uncertainty propagation after validity is established. Characterized linear feasibility boundary for response-preserving nuisance removal, computed sharp ranges over exact-optimum correspondence sets, and created a distribution-free certificate that retains credit coordinates only when all exact optima agree on nonzero sign.

Result: Experimental findings: 1) Two deterministic optimal tracebacks disagreed on temporal localization for 55.9% of 1,586 nonzero trajectory pairs; 2) Two frozen 800-rollout tool-use audits showed exact-optimum reversals of intended turn-level credit; 3) Map calibrated only on benign examples erased every retained harmful response, while two-sided validation selected response-preserving alternatives.

Conclusion: Cross-view correspondence must be declared, validated, and propagated into uncertainty before agent evaluation or credit assignment can support any point conclusion. The correspondence is fundamentally a measurement intervention, not neutral preprocessing.

Abstract: Agent evaluations and trace-based learning often compare outputs across transformed views through a post-response correspondence treated as neutral preprocessing. We show that this correspondence is a measurement intervention: omitting it can manufacture sensitivity, an over-aggressive map can manufacture invariance, and multiple optimal correspondences can leave mechanism labels and signed learning credit unidentified. We develop a validity theory and audit with three components: two-sided validation of nuisance removal and response preservation, all-optima identification of downstream conclusions, and uncertainty propagation after validity is established. We characterize the linear feasibility boundary for response-preserving nuisance removal, compute sharp ranges over exact-optimum correspondence sets, and give a distribution-free certificate that retains a credit coordinate only when all exact optima agree on its nonzero sign. Across public code and SQL pipelines, two deterministic optimal tracebacks disagree on temporal localization for 55.9% of 1,586 nonzero trajectory pairs; two frozen 800-rollout tool-use audits, including a task-and-seed-disjoint replication, expose exact-optimum reversals of intended turn-level credit, although a clean public quick-start subset shows none. A pre-registered transport gate failed on natural responses; frozen corrected and held-out controls then show that a map calibrated only on benign examples erases every retained harmful response, while two-sided validation selects response-preserving alternatives. Cross-view correspondence must therefore be declared, validated, and propagated into uncertainty before agent evaluation or credit assignment supports a point conclusion.

</details>


### [122] [MAGPIE-Net: Predicting short-duration heavy-rainfall events in station neighborhoods from multitemporal FY-4A AGRI observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17753)
*Xiang Lin,Yunying Li,Chengzhi Ye,Zitong Chen,Jing Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MAGPIE-Net is a deep learning model that directly converts geostationary satellite observations into local heavy-rainfall warnings by embedding a geographically adaptive grid-to-station mapping, outperforming traditional gridded-prediction approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional deep-learning nowcasting methods convert satellite signals into local warnings through post-processing of gridded precipitation predictions, which prevents station-neighborhood event targets from directly supervising the satellite-to-station learning pathway. This indirect approach limits the effectiveness of using satellite observations for local heavy-rainfall warnings.

Method: Propose MAGPIE-Net which embeds a geographically adaptive, differentiable grid-to-station mapping in a pathway combining: 1) convection-initiation features from multitemporal infrared and water-vapor observations, 2) multiscale encoding, and 3) auxiliary gridded precipitation diagnosis. Station-neighborhood event losses directly constrain the satellite representation and its mapping to irregular station locations for 0-3 hour event prediction.

Result: In independent 2023 warm-season tests over central and eastern China: CSI values were 0.371, 0.304, and 0.238 at 0-1, 1-2, and 2-3 hours for 40km/20mm h-1 definition. Detection rate of 65.1% with mean lead time of 64.6 minutes, compared to 23.6% and 18.3 minutes for best baseline. During early-warning stage (antecedent rainfall <1mm), detected 51.9% of episodes with 38.5 min lead time. Superior performance maintained for smaller neighborhoods and 50 mm h-1 threshold.

Conclusion: Event-oriented satellite-to-station modeling directly converts multitemporal geostationary cloud and moisture observations into local heavy-rainfall warnings more effectively than gridded-precipitation modeling approaches.

Abstract: Short-duration heavy-rainfall warning determines whether 1 h rainfall will exceed a threshold within a target-station neighborhood over the next few hours. Multitemporal infrared and water-vapor observations from the Fengyun-4A Advanced Geostationary Radiation Imager (FY-4A AGRI) capture cloud-top cooling, moisture evolution, and cloud expansion before substantial surface rainfall develops. However, most deep-learning nowcasting methods convert these signals into local warnings by post-processing gridded precipitation predictions, preventing station-neighborhood event targets from directly supervising the satellite-to-station learning pathway. We propose MAGPIE-Net, which embeds a geographically adaptive, differentiable grid-to-station mapping in a pathway combining convection-initiation features, multiscale encoding, and auxiliary gridded precipitation diagnosis. Station-neighborhood event losses thereby constrain the satellite representation and its mapping to irregular station locations for 0-3 h event prediction. In independent 2023 warm-season tests over central and eastern China, critical success index (CSI) values under the primary 40 km/20 mm h-1 definition were 0.371, 0.304, and 0.238 at 0-1, 1-2, and 2-3 h. Across episodes, MAGPIE-Net achieved a detection rate of 65.1% and a mean lead time of 64.6 min, compared with 23.6% and 18.3 min for the best gridded-output baseline, and remained superior for smaller neighborhoods and the 50 mm h-1 threshold. During the critical early-warning stage, when antecedent 1 h rainfall within 40 km remained below 1 mm, MAGPIE-Net detected 51.9% of episodes with a mean lead time of 38.5 min. These results show that event-oriented satellite-to-station modeling converts multitemporal geostationary cloud and moisture observations into local heavy-rainfall warnings more effectively than gridded-precipitation modeling.

</details>


### [123] [Training-Free Human-in-the-Loop Anomaly Detection via Memory Bank Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17775)
*Ayusha Abbas,Saram Abbas,Kabita Adhikari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Training-free human-in-the-loop framework where domain experts correct PatchCore anomaly detectors by direct memory bank editing without retraining, significantly improving detection performance from just 10 golden samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Anomaly detectors are hardest to deploy where training data is scarcest, such as newly commissioned production lines with only a handful of verified "golden" samples and no machine-learning engineer on site. There's a need for deployment-time correction without requiring retraining, gradients, or original training data.

Method: A human-in-the-loop framework where domain experts correct a PatchCore detector by editing the memory bank directly. False-positive corrections insert normal patches through a self-calibrating novelty gate that admits only patches beyond the median pool-normal nearest-neighbor distance. The system works with a memory bank built on only ten golden samples and doesn't require retraining.

Result: From a bank built on ten golden samples, operator corrections close a median 66% of the gap to an uncorrected fully trained bank (mean 80%). Significantly improves 12 of 15 MVTec AD categories and harms none. Ten samples plus corrections outperform hundreds of samples without them. Headroom is smaller for already-trained banks but still beneficial in certain categories like toothbrush (+0.10 AUROC), metal nut (+0.09), zipper (+0.05), screw (+0.05).

Conclusion: The framework enables effective deployment-time correction of anomaly detectors in low-data scenarios without requiring retraining, making ML systems accessible to domain experts without ML expertise. The method is particularly valuable for newly commissioned systems with scarce training data, though live expert trials with real-world mislabeling costs remain for future work.

Abstract: Anomaly detectors are hardest to deploy exactly where training data is scarcest: a newly commissioned production line has a handful of verified "golden" samples and no machine-learning engineer on the factory floor. We present a training-free human-in-the-loop framework in which a domain expert corrects a PatchCore detector by direct memory bank editing: no retraining, no gradients, no original training data. A false-positive correction inserts the reviewed image's normal patches through a self-calibrating novelty gate admitting only those beyond the median pool-normal nearest-neighbour distance. From a bank built on only ten golden samples, operator corrections close a median 66% of the gap to an uncorrected fully trained bank (mean 80%, raised by three categories that overshoot parity), significantly improving 12 of 15 MVTec AD categories and harming none: ten samples plus corrections outperform hundreds of samples without them. On already-trained banks the headroom is smaller and concentrated where the bank undersamples normal appearance (gated: toothbrush +0.10, metal nut +0.09, zipper +0.05, screw +0.05), and no category except grid is significantly harmed. Evaluation uses a held-out protocol (20 splits per category, Holm-corrected Wilcoxon), because corrected images entering the bank inflate naive evaluation toward AUROC 1.0 by memorisation. Passive and active querying are statistically indistinguishable; a matched-label-budget control attributes gains to deployment-time label production at 43% of exhaustive-review cost; a defect-memory extension fails decisively. Feedback is simulated from ground truth; live expert trials, where mislabelling is costliest on small banks, remain future work.

</details>


### [124] [Debate Training Reduces Reward Hacking in RLAIF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17776)
*Zachary Kenton,Lili Janzer,Rory Greig,Tian Huey Teh,Kirill Tyshchuk,Jonah Brown-Cohen,Harri Edwards,Senthooran Rajamanoharan,Noah Y. Siegel,Natasha Jaques,Rohin Shah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL finetuning LLMs using debate (two-player adversarial game) reduces reward hacking compared to RLAIF baseline, especially when the AI judge is weaker than the policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing reward hacking in RLAIF where policies learn to exploit systematic errors in AI judges, particularly problematic when judges are weaker than policies - a setting relevant for overseeing increasingly capable AI systems.

Method: Use debate: a two-player adversarial game between generator and critic adjudicated by a weaker LLM judge. Compare against single-player RLAIF baseline on mathematics tasks where final-answer correctness is verifiable. Train Gemini 2.5 Flash-class policy with frozen Gemini 2.5 Flash Lite judge, and use critique word limits to balance the game.

Result: Debate maintains judge performance throughout training while baseline quickly hacks the judge, leading to 45% performance gap recovery. Debate overrides prompted misalignment, has smaller train/validation reward gap than RL from verifiable rewards, and critique word limits (up to 150 words) successfully balance the game.

Conclusion: Debate shows promise for reducing reward hacking in RLAIF, but balancing multi-agent training is critical. Critique word limits help avoid judge hacking but trade off expressive clarity. Results are a positive update on debate feasibility for AI oversight.

Abstract: We demonstrate that RL finetuning an LLM using debate, a two-player adversarial game between a generator and a critic adjudicated by a weaker LLM judge, reduces reward hacking compared to a reinforcement learning from AI feedback (RLAIF) baseline. Reward hacking is a central obstacle in RLAIF: as training progresses, the policy learns to exploit systematic errors in its AI judge, degrading task performance, a problem that worsens precisely when the judge is weaker than the policy, the setting most relevant to overseeing increasingly capable AI systems. We study mathematics tasks, where final-answer correctness is verifiable, allowing us to measure reward hacking dynamics. We train a Gemini~2.5 Flash-class policy with a frozen, weaker Gemini~2.5 Flash Lite judge, comparing a single-player RLAIF baseline against debate. While the baseline quickly hacks the judge, debate maintains judge performance throughout training, leading to a higher peak validation accuracy (45\% performance gap recovered) that persists through many RL steps. Additional experiments show that: 1) further weakening the judge leads to faster hacking, but this can be compensated by adding an additional debate round; 2) debate incentives override prompted misalignment; 3) RL using an LLM judge has a smaller train/validation reward gap than RL from verifiable rewards; 4) learning to critique to convince the judge using ground truth labels is possible but slow. Taken together, our results are a positive update on the feasibility of debate, while highlighting that balancing multi-agent training is critical: without player constraints, adversarial training risks defaulting to critic judge-hacking. We show that critique word limits (effective up to 150 words) successfully balance the game and avoid judge hacking, though this introduces a trade-off by restricting critic expressive clarity.

</details>


### [125] [Fourth-Moment Geometry of Rademacher Sums](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17802)
*Peigan Gao,Jian Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proves sharp moment bounds for Rademacher sums, establishing optimal constants for Khintchine-type inequalities and settling several longstanding conjectures about Gaussian stability and quadratic stability estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand how higher moments of normalized Rademacher sums depend on fourth-order structure, and to settle conjectures by Jakimiuk and Barański et al. regarding optimal constants in Khintchine-type inequalities and stability estimates.

Method: The method combines: (1) sharp fixed-q moment envelope analysis, (2) separate arguments below the convexity threshold, (3) a fourth-order framework to analyze how moments depend on fourth-order mass, and (4) substantial computational assistance from ChatGPT 5.6 Sol for discovering proofs.

Result: Main results: (1) Gaussian stability inequality for the full range p≥4 with linear-in-q bound, (2) Sharp finite-dimensional L_p/L_4 Khintchine constant for p≥5 with flat coefficient vector as extremizer, (3) Jakimiuk's conjectured quadratic stability estimate at p=3, (4) Coefficient-sensitive tail bounds via Laplace-transform forms.

Conclusion: The paper successfully settles major conjectures in Rademacher sum theory, providing optimal constants and stability estimates that retain information about sparsity and effective dimension, with applications to random projections and error analysis.

Abstract: Let $\varepsilon_1,\ldots,\varepsilon_n$ be independent Rademacher signs and let $a=(a_1,\ldots,a_n)\in\R^n$ satisfy the normalization below. For the normalized Rademacher sum, we determine how its higher moments depend on the fourth-order mass. Combining a sharp fixed-q moment envelope with a separate argument below the convexity threshold gives the Gaussian stability inequality for the full range $p\geq4$ of this linear-in-q bound. The same fourth-order framework determines the sharp finite dimensional $L_p/L_4$ Khintchine constant for $p\geq5$, with the flat coefficient vector as the extremizer. These results settle the conjectures of Jakimiuk and of Barański, Murawski, Nayar, and Oleszkiewicz stated below. We also prove Jakimiuk's conjectured quadratic stability estimate at $p=3$. The resulting bounds retain information about sparsity and effective dimension, with applications to Rademacher random projections and randomly signed errors; those applications are not developed further here. Their Laplace-transform form also gives coefficient-sensitive tail bounds. The proofs are discovered with substantial assistance from ChatGPT 5.6 Sol.

</details>


### [126] [An Empirical Study of Reward Specification and Benchmark Reliability in GRPO-based LLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17804)
*Rubén Balbastre,Juan Manuel Orduña,Mariano Pérez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies a specification problem in LLM unlearning for generative QA: when target-adjacent prompts admit broader answers without target-specific leakage, models should answer at that level rather than leak, evade, or refuse. The research examines four reward designs in a LoRA-GRPO RWKU setting and finds that optimization success doesn't guarantee behavioral unlearning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current practical LLM unlearning evaluation only focuses on suppressing target-specific knowledge and preserving non-target utility. This leaves a third behavior underspecified: how models should handle target-adjacent prompts that admit broader answers without target-specific leakage. The research aims to address this specification gap in generative QA unlearning scenarios.

Method: The study uses a controlled LoRA-GRPO RWKU (Reward-based Knowledge Unlearning) setting to compare four reward designs: lexical suppression, anti-refusal shaping, rubric-based broad answering, and explicit refusal contrast, both with and without SFT warm-up. Evaluation includes RWKU forget scores, held-out completion audits, terminal training-rollout audits, and training dynamics analysis.

Result: Experiments show that optimization success is not equivalent to behavioral unlearning. Different evaluation methods (forget scores, completion audits, training-rollout audits, training dynamics) can lead to different conclusions about unlearning success. The researchers trace these disagreements to reward-hacking endpoints, policy-support limits in GRPO, benchmark probes that miss endpoint changes, and rewards that select broad-topic answering with low semantic leakage during optimization.

Conclusion: The paper highlights a critical specification problem in LLM unlearning evaluation and demonstrates that current optimization metrics don't reliably predict desired behavioral outcomes. The findings suggest that unlearning evaluations need more sophisticated specifications that account for how models should handle target-adjacent prompts, and that different reward designs lead to different behavioral endpoints that may not align with intended unlearning objectives.

Abstract: Practical LLM unlearning is usually evaluated through two objectives: suppress target-specific knowledge and preserve non-target utility. In generative QA, this leaves a third behavior underspecified: when a target-adjacent prompt admits a broader answer without target-specific leakage, the model should answer at that level rather than leak, evade, or refuse. We study this specification problem in a controlled LoRA-GRPO RWKU setting, comparing four reward designs that span lexical suppression, anti-refusal shaping, rubric-based broad answering, and an explicit refusal contrast, with and without SFT warm-up. The experiments show that optimization success is not equivalent to behavioral unlearning: RWKU forget scores, held-out completion audits, terminal training-rollout audits, and training dynamics can point to different conclusions. We trace these disagreements to reward-hacking endpoints, policy-support limits in GRPO, benchmark probes that miss endpoint changes, and rewards that can select broad-topic answering with low semantic leakage during optimization.

</details>


### [127] [MotoSafety: Edge-AI with Learned Temporal Importance for Two-Wheeler Collision Risk Assessment Under Time Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17823)
*Sumit S. Shevtekar,Chandresh K. Maurya,Gourab Sil,Subasish Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MotoSafety: A lightweight edge-AI architecture for powered two-wheeler collision risk assessment using cognitive stressor data, achieving high accuracy with minimal parameters for real-time deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Powered two-wheeler riders in low- and middle-income countries face serious safety challenges, but there's limited research on how cognitive stressors like Time Pressure (TP) influence collision risk. Existing studies lack large-scale datasets and real-time assessment systems.

Method: Created a large-scale dataset of 129,000+ labeled multivariate time-series sequences from 153 simulator rides by 51 participants under No, Low, and High Time Pressure conditions. Proposed MotoSafety architecture based on Learned Temporal Importance principle, using ground truth TP as inductive bias. Evaluated with 64 features across vehicle dynamics, control inputs, proximity, and behavioral violations.

Result: MotoSafety achieved 94.97% accuracy and 99.33% ROC AUC for classification, outperforming 10 baselines including TimesNet and LLM4TS. For forecasting: 0.039 MSE and 0.094 MAE (4.4x lower error than Time-LLM and iTransformer). With only 1.15M parameters and 0.135 ms latency, suitable for edge deployment. Using only 21 IMU+GPS features achieved 93.91% accuracy. Architecture transferred well to human activity (97.66%) and clinical domains (99.65%).

Conclusion: MotoSafety provides an effective, lightweight framework for real-time powered two-wheeler collision risk assessment that can be deployed on low-cost edge devices. The use of cognitive stressors as inductive bias improves performance, and the architecture shows strong transferability to other domains, advancing the Safe System Approach for Intelligent Transportation Systems.

Abstract: Powered two-wheeler riders face critical safety challenges in low- and middle-income countries, yet limited studies exist on how cognitive stressors such as Time Pressure influence collision risk.
