To address this gap, we introduce a large-scale dataset of over 129,000 labeled multivariate time-series sequences from 153 simulator rides by 51 participants under No, Low, and High TP, capturing 64 features across vehicle dynamics, control inputs, proximity, and behavioral violations. Building on this dataset, we propose MotoSafety, a novel edge-AI architecture grounded in the Learned Temporal Importance principle. MotoSafety achieves 94.97% accuracy and 99.33% ROC AUC, outperforming ten baselines, including TimesNet and LLM4TS, and achieves 0.039 MSE and 0.094 MAE for forecasting (4.4x lower error than Time-LLM and iTransformer). With only 1.15M parameters and 0.135 ms latency, it is suitable for edge deployment on low-cost CPU hardware. Using ground truth TP as an inductive bias improves accuracy from 94.09% to 94.97%, while predicted TP achieves 94.82%. Using only 21 IMU+GPS features, it achieves 93.91% accuracy, indicating practical deployment. Beyond PTW safety, the architecture shows better transferability to human activity (97.66%) and clinical (99.65%) domains. This lightweight framework advances PTW collision risk assessment, supporting the Safe System Approach for Intelligent Transportation Systems.

</details>


### [128] [Leveraging Association Context Retrieval in Knowledge Edit- ing to Build White-Box Attacks on LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17836)
*Roman Maksimov,Vladimir Aletov,Vladimir Solodkin,Dmitry Bylinkin,Daniil Medyakov,Aleksandr Beznosikov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A white-box attack method using knowledge editing techniques to induce unsafe behavior in LLMs by removing constraints across entire thematic categories rather than just predefined prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs gain increasing autonomy, it's essential to investigate methods to induce unsafe behavior for safety evaluation. Existing locate-then-edit approaches from Knowledge Editing show promise because models edited this way tend to assign unusually high probabilities to edit targets, which could be leveraged for attacks.

Method: Modifies knowledge editing frameworks by incorporating associative knowledge retrieved from the model itself, extending constraint removal to cover entire thematic categories rather than being limited to prompts from a predefined dataset. This creates a white-box attack inspired by locate-then-edit approaches.

Result: Experiments with various model architectures demonstrate improved attack effectiveness over competing methods while not dealing critical damage to general model performance.

Conclusion: The proposed white-box attack method successfully induces unsafe behavior in LLMs more effectively than existing methods, leveraging knowledge editing techniques to remove safety constraints across broad thematic categories, which is important for evaluating model safety as autonomy increases.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) are granted increasing autonomy, it is essential to investigate methods that can induce unsafe behavior. We propose a novel white-box attack inspired by locate-then-edit approaches from the field of Knowledge Editing. Our choice is motivated by the observation that models edited with such schemes tend to assign unusually high prediction probabilities to the edit target, a property that is particularly advantageous when designing attacks. We modify the editing framework by incorporating as- sociative knowledge retrieved from the model, thereby extending constraint removal to an entire thematic category rather than being limited to prompts from a predefined dataset. Experiments with various archi- tectures demonstrate improved attack effectiveness over competing methods without dealing critical damage to general model performance.

</details>


### [129] [MoRAX: Mobility-based Representation Augmentation for Geospatial Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17848)
*Ya Wen,Jixuan Cai,Yulun Zhou,Alec Kirkley*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MoRAX is a lightweight framework that augments geospatial foundation models with human mobility data to capture functional urban structure, enabling zero-shot deployment in unseen cities with improved socioeconomic and environmental prediction performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geospatial Foundation Models (GFMs) rely on Earth-observation data but lack information about human activity. Human mobility data reveals functional relationships between regions but is often city-specific and not transferable for urban representation learning.

Method: MoRAX is a lightweight framework that augments geospatial embeddings with functional structure derived from human mobility. It uses a teacher-student approach where the teacher observes mobility data, and the student learns from the teacher's representations without direct mobility input, enabling zero-shot deployment in unseen cities.

Result: Across four cities in two countries, the MoRAX teacher model consistently outperforms GFMs and baselines in eight socioeconomic and environmental prediction tasks. The student model approaches teacher performance on most tasks. Cross-country transfer shows mobility conditioning provides a general mechanism for grounding geospatial foundations in human dimensions.

Conclusion: MoRAX successfully bridges the gap between geospatial visual representations and human functional structure, creating more comprehensive urban representations that transfer effectively across cities and countries while maintaining GFM coverage and consistency.

Abstract: Geospatial Foundation Models (GFMs) are emerging as a powerful paradigm for learning semantically rich and geographically consistent visual and physical representations. However, their reliance on Earth-observation (EO) data leaves information about human activity largely underrepresented. Human mobility data reveals the functional and relational structure between regions that is missing from EO data, but is often limited only to the city where it is observed, making it challenging to use for transferable urban representation learning. We introduce MoRAX, a lightweight framework for augmenting geospatial embeddings with functional structure derived from human mobility. MoRAX preserves the coverage and consistency of a GFM while providing information about the functional connectivity among urban regions, permitting zero-shot deployment in unseen cities with or without available mobility data. Across four target cities spanning two countries, the MoRAX teacher model, which observes mobility, consistently outperforms GFMs and strong urban representation baselines in eight socioeconomic and environmental prediction tasks. Meanwhile, the student model, which never takes mobility data as input, approaches the teacher in performance on most tasks. Transfer results across countries further demonstrate that modulation conditioned on mobility flows provides a general mechanism for grounding geospatial foundations in the human dimension of cities.

</details>


### [130] [Efficient Resource Optimization for Split Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17849)
*Wei Wei,Xianhao Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents an efficient optimization framework for split federated learning (SFL) that jointly optimizes model splitting and resource allocation to minimize training costs, with polynomial-time algorithms and approximation guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing optimization schemes for SFL are either heuristic or computationally inefficient, making them unsuitable for handling large-scale user populations. There's a need for efficient solutions to optimize SFL under resource-constrained networks.

Method: 1) Develop a polynomial-time algorithm for the model splitting problem that achieves global optimum. 2) Formulate joint model splitting and resource allocation as a two-dimensional master problem. 3) Develop an efficient approximation method with (1+ε)-approximation guarantee for the joint problem.

Result: The proposed approach provides efficient solutions that strike the optimal energy-latency tradeoff. The method achieves global optimum for model splitting and has theoretical approximation guarantees for the joint optimization problem.

Conclusion: The paper establishes an efficient optimization framework for SFL that overcomes the limitations of previous approaches by providing computationally efficient, theoretically-grounded solutions for joint model splitting and resource allocation in resource-constrained networks.

Abstract: Split federated learning (SFL) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for model training at the edge. However, SFL inherently involves discrete decision variables for model splitting and resource allocation, resulting in a challenging mixed-integer problem. Consequently, prior optimization schemes for SFL are either \textit{heuristic} or \textit{computationally inefficient}, which cannot handle large-scale user populations. To address this limitation, this work establishes an efficient optimization framework for SFL under resource-constrained networks. Our framework jointly optimizes model splitting and resource allocation to minimize training cost, which is defined as the weighted sum of latency and energy costs. We first study the model splitting problem and develop a polynomial-time algorithm that achieves the global optimum. Then, we extend the approach to the joint model splitting and resource allocation problem. In this case, we formulate it as a two-dimensional master problem and develop an efficient approximation method with a $(1+ε)$-approximation guarantee. Extensive experiments show that the proposed approach provides efficient solutions to strike the optimal energy--latency tradeoff.

</details>


### [131] [Dynamic Compression in Recurrent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17896)
*Jyothish Pari,Ryan Bahlous-Boldi,Pulkit Agrawal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recurrent models with dynamic compression can selectively revisit past tokens to revise their fixed-size state, improving efficiency for long-context tasks by trading computation for memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current recurrent models compress history in a single causal pass, forcing limited state to compromise across future demands since each input must be compressed before knowing its future use.

Method: Introduces dynamic compression allowing recurrent models to selectively revisit past tokens and revise fixed-size state through additional recurrent updates, enabling selective refinement of relevant information when needed.

Result: Dynamic compression substantially reduces recurrent state required for accurate function reuse and scales more favorably as number of stored functions grows, demonstrating computation-memory tradeoff.

Conclusion: Recurrent models can spend more computation revisiting history to make more effective use of fixed-size state, offering an improved approach for long-context processing.

Abstract: Recurrent models process long contexts efficiently by compressing their history into a fixed-size state, but modern architectures typically do so in a single causal pass over the sequence. Each input must therefore be compressed before the model knows how it will later be used, forcing a limited state to compromise across possible future demands. We introduce dynamic compression, which allows a recurrent model to selectively revisit past tokens and revise its fixed-size state through additional recurrent updates. The model need not preserve every part of the history at uniformly high fidelity in its recurrent state, because lower-fidelity information can be revisited from the retained raw sequence when it becomes relevant. We study this in a controlled setting where the model first learns multiple functions in-context and, later in the same sequence, encounters a series of few-shot tasks that each require it to identify and reuse one of those functions. A single-pass model must preserve every function at sufficient fidelity for any future task, whereas selective re-scanning allows the model to revisit and refine only the function currently needed. We find that dynamic compression substantially reduces the recurrent state required for accurate reuse and scales more favorably as the number of stored functions grows. These results demonstrate a computation--memory tradeoff in which recurrent models can spend more computation revisiting their history to make more effective use of a fixed-size state.

</details>


### [132] [Hybrid ML for Lightweight Pre-Route Delay Estimation in Open-Source IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17914)
*Marvin Castro Castro,Erick Carvajal Barboza*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight hybrid ML model combining decision trees and linear regression improves pre-routing delay estimation accuracy in chip design by 80% compared to OpenLane, while being 300× smaller and faster.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static Timing Analysis (STA) for digital integrated circuits faces challenges in obtaining accurate delay estimations when limited physical design information is available during pre-routing stages. Traditional approaches and complex ML models may be insufficient or computationally expensive for early design phases.

Method: A hybrid and lightweight machine learning approach combining decision trees with linear regression to improve pre-routing delay estimations. The model works with the open-source RTL-to-GDSII tool OpenLane but doesn't require OpenLane-specific parameters to be effective.

Result: Achieves 80% error reduction compared to OpenLane's original delay estimates. Even without using OpenLane-specific parameters, the model shows 71% improvement. The solution is over 300 times smaller, 2 times faster, and offers higher explainability compared to alternatives.

Conclusion: This hybrid ML approach provides an accurate, efficient, and explainable alternative to traditional delay propagation techniques and more complex machine learning models for pre-routing STA in digital IC design.

Abstract: Static Timing Analysis (STA) is a critical step in the design flow of digital integrated circuits, however, obtaining accurate delay estimations can represent a challenge when limited information regarding physical design is available. In response, this work presents a hybrid and light-weight machine learning (ML) based approach that combines a decision tree with linear regression to improve pre-routing delay estimations generated by the open-source RTL-to-GDSII tool OpenLane. The proposed model achieves an 80\% reduction in error compared to OpenLane's estimates, demonstrates a 71\% improvement even without utilizing OpenLane-specific parameters. Overall, this method offers an alternative to traditional delay propagation techniques and more complex machine learning models that is not only accurate, but is also over 300 times smaller, 2 times faster and offers a higher explainability.

</details>


### [133] [Efficient RLVR Scheduling via Graph-Structured Online Difficulty Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17941)
*Zhizhao Liu,Zhiliang Tian,Xi Wang,Zhihua Wen,Yihang Xiong,Zhiquan Lai,Dongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a graph-based online difficulty estimator for RLVR that shares rollout feedback across related samples to enable efficient difficulty-adaptive exploration without dedicated probing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RLVR improves LLM reasoning but requires costly rollout exploration. Current adaptive schedulers face challenges with reliable online difficulty estimation - dedicated probing adds overhead, while history-based estimators suffer from cold start, stale feedback, and ignore sample relationships.

Method: Constructs difficulty-aware sample graph based on semantic and reasoning similarities. Introduces latent difficulty states with Potts prior for neighboring similarity. Uses state-level Beta-Binomial model to aggregate rollout outcomes. Implements online mean-field variational algorithm to continuously update latent-state assignments and state-level difficulty.

Result: Experiments across multiple base models, RL schedulers, and benchmarks demonstrate that the framework achieves better performance.

Conclusion: Proposed framework enables efficient difficulty-adaptive exploration for RLVR without dedicated probing by leveraging graph-based difficulty estimation that shares feedback across related samples.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models but relies on costly rollout exploration. Assigning the same exploration budget to samples with different difficulty levels is inefficient: easy samples may receive redundant rollouts, whereas difficult but learnable samples may receive too little exploration. Existing adaptive schedulers address this mismatch through curriculum-based sample selection or non-uniform rollout allocation based on estimated sample difficulty. However, obtaining reliable online difficulty estimates remains challenging: dedicated probing adds substantial generation overhead, whereas history-based estimators face a cold start with no initial observations and stale feedback, and typically ignore relations among samples. To address these limitations, we propose a plug-and-play graph-based online difficulty estimator that shares rollout feedback across related samples and continuously updates their difficulty estimates, mitigating cold start and staleness without dedicated probing. Specifically, we first construct a difficulty-aware sample graph based on semantic and reasoning similarities. Based on this graph, we introduce latent difficulty states and use a Potts prior to encourage neighboring samples to share the same state. We then employ a state-level Beta-Binomial model to aggregate the rollout outcomes associated with each state. Finally, we use an online mean-field variational algorithm to continuously update the latent-state assignments and state-level difficulty as new feedback arrives. Our framework can be integrated into sample-selection and rollout-allocation schedulers, enabling difficulty-adaptive exploration without dedicated probing. Experiments across multiple base models, RL schedulers, and benchmarks demonstrate that our framework achieves better performance.

</details>


### [134] [SIGMA: SHAP-Guided Implicit-Trajectory Generation for Metadata-Free LLM-Based AutoFE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17948)
*Xuan Zheng,Kento Uchida,Shinichi Shirakawa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SIGMA is a scalable AutoFE framework that uses SHAP values for task-aware feature generation instead of semantic metadata, and employs implicit trajectory representation to maintain constant context length while improving generation stability and reducing duplicates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based AutoFE approaches face two key challenges: 1) semantic metadata is often unavailable in practical settings, limiting applicability, and 2) trajectory-based prompting causes context window overflow issues, while alternatives lead to unstable generation with local optima traps and high feature duplication rates.

Method: SIGMA uses SHAP values to provide task-aware guidance for group feature generation, replacing the need for semantic metadata. It implements EXIT (EXposed-feature Implicit Trajectory) where exposed features in the prompt implicitly represent the optimization trajectory, maintaining constant prompt length.

Result: SIGMA achieves comparable performance to SOTA LLM baselines with nearly constant prompt length. EXIT reduces duplicate feature ratio from 37.2% to 6.8%. SIGMA matches traditional SOTA performance using only 5.4 features on average, showing substantial efficiency gains.

Conclusion: SIGMA provides a scalable solution for metadata-free AutoFE that addresses context window limitations and generation instability issues, achieving efficiency and effectiveness comparable to or better than existing approaches while operating with minimal feature requirements.

Abstract: Recent research has leveraged Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance Automated Feature Engineering (AutoFE) through semantic descriptions and trajectory-based prompting. However, there exist two challenges that limit their applicability and scalability in long-horizon optimization: (1) semantic metadata is unavailable in many practical settings, and (2) trajectory accumulation increases the risk of exceeding the context window, while without it, the generation process can become unstable, leading to becoming stuck in the local optima and a high duplicate rate of generated features. To this end, we propose a SHAP-enhanced Implicit-trajectory Generation for Metadata-free AutoFE (SIGMA), a scalable constant-context optimization framework. SIGMA leverages SHAP values to provide task-aware signals for guiding group feature generation instead of semantic information. In addition, we adopt an EXposed-feature Implicit Trajectory (EXIT) approach, where the exposed features in the prompt implicitly represent the trajectory. Empirical results demonstrate that SIGMA achieves performance comparable to the state-of-the-art (SOTA) LLM baselines with a nearly constant prompt length. Notably, EXIT significantly reduces the duplicate ratio of generated features from 37.2% to 6.8%. At the same time, SIGMA matches traditional SOTA performance with only 5.4 features on average, demonstrating substantial efficiency gains in feature utilization.

</details>


### [135] [An Omitted Mode Is a Rare Rule: The Sampling-Verification Danger Law in Continuous Code World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17956)
*Javier Aguilar Martín*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Code World Model paradigm uses LLM-synthesized executable models verified by reproducing sampled transitions; analysis shows acceptance only certifies sample consistency, not safety in continuous control, with demonstrated planner exploitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what the acceptance criterion in the Code World Model paradigm actually certifies for safety in continuous control domains, particularly when dealing with hybrid instruments and discontinuous reset modes.

Method: Mathematical analysis of pipeline danger as expected risk, proving formulas for critical event detection probability and localization budgets. Empirical evaluation using GPT-5.x LLM synthesis on 1D and 2D hybrid instruments, with targeted interventions and positive controls. Statistical validation across 1034 artifacts with independent samples.

Result: GPT-5.x repairs omitted 1D clamps in 105/111 cases but fails completely on 2D regions (0/156). Acceptance only certifies sample consistency, not safety: proven harmless entry rules exist that match all samples yet are dangerous. On hybrid instruments, mode-blind accepted models are exploited with nearly total return loss.

Conclusion: Acceptance in Code World Models certifies only sample consistency, not safety or correctness; identifiability of safety rules is class-relative and depends on instrument properties. The pipeline remains vulnerable to critical events missed by sampling, necessitating stronger verification methods for continuous control safety.

Abstract: In the Code World Model paradigm an LLM synthesizes an executable world model that a classical planner searches, and the model is accepted when it reproduces sampled transitions. We ask what that acceptance certifies in continuous control. We define the pipeline's danger as an expected risk and isolate its exact factor: the probability that N i.i.d. gate rollouts all miss a critical event of probability r is exactly (1-r)^N; an independent acceptance sample adds its budget to the exponent. On three hybrid instruments the accepted mode-blind model is exploited: the planner is pinned at the mode boundary at a regret of nearly the whole attainable return. We prove a localization budget, valid at boundary points: models with Lipschitz constant at most L differing by eta at a point disagree above tolerance eps on a region of volume at least kappa((eta-eps)/L)^(d+m); the discontinuous reset modes studied pay no such budget. With real LLM synthesis, GPT-5.x repairs an omitted 1D clamp in 105 of 111 mode-containing draws -- every attempt exact on 50 of 56 instrument-stream blocks (95% CI [0.781, 0.960]). On 2D regions no artifact recovers the rule (0/156); eight targeted interventions leave the failure in place, and positive controls locate it: a located rule is not induced, while given form and location the constants follow exactly. A version-space certificate proves identification is class-relative: at the widest dose the declared fit succeeds in 20/20 blocks and every sample-consistent circle is within tolerance in 18/20. We prove a class of entry rules exactly consistent with every sample yet harmless at play, so identifiability is a measurable property of the instrument. Re-scoring all 1034 artifacts on independent samples confirms acceptance certifies sample consistency and no more: where the gate is provably informative it covers about two percent of the exploited planner's queries.

</details>


### [136] [Understanding the Surprising Generalization Properties of Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17957)
*Nour Shaheen,Junwei Ma,Alex Labach,Frank Hutter,Valentin Thomas,Anthony L. Caterini*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Single-table self-supervised pre-training enables surprisingly strong transfer for tabular foundation models, with feature count being more important than instance count for usefulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tabular foundation models rely on massive synthetic corpora or large real datasets, but the authors want to understand if strong transfer can emerge from simpler, single-table pre-training.

Method: Self-supervised pre-training on just a single real table, analyzing what makes tables broadly useful or poor across downstream tasks, and testing column-level pre-processing vs dataset-level filtering.

Result: Surprisingly strong transfer emerges from single-table pre-training; usefulness correlates more with number of features than instances; column-level pre-processing improves performance while dataset-level filtering doesn't.

Conclusion: Tabular in-context generalization is largely retrieval-based, offering a new task-centric, retrieval-based framework for future model and corpus design in tabular foundation models.

Abstract: Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) increasingly rely on in-context learning, where a model receives labelled examples at inference time and predicts labels for new inputs without updating its weights. Existing TFMs are typically trained on either massive synthetic corpora or very large collections of real datasets. In contrast, we show that surprisingly strong transfer can emerge from self-supervised pre-training on just a single real table. In this setting, we also find that tables tend to be either broadly useful or broadly poor regardless of downstream prediction task, and that the strongest predictor of usefulness is the number of features rather than the number of instances. This leads to a task-centric interpretation of tabular pre-training: the number and the quality of tasks are essential for the pre-training of TFMs.
  We show that the same task-centric perspective can help corpus design at scale: fine-grained column-level pre-processing consistently improves downstream performance, while no improvements are observed when we filter or deduplicate at the dataset level.
  Finally, we offer a new perspective for how TFMs generalize: we believe that tabular in-context generalization is largely retrieval-based, and good models are those that learn to identify relevant examples in the provided context and aggregate them well. The mechanics of TFMs have been relatively understudied; our task-centric, retrieval-based perspective offers a new framework to guide future model and corpus design.

</details>


### [137] [Too Sure to Be Safe: Model Calibration for Reliable Log Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17965)
*Bin Li,Dongdong Wang,Siyang Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoRD is a post-hoc calibration framework that improves confidence reliability in log anomaly detectors by learning prediction-route-specific reliability models from latent representations and selectively recalibrating high-risk predictions to suppress overconfident errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language model-based log anomaly detectors achieve strong detection performance but have poorly calibrated confidence estimates. They frequently assign excessive confidence to incorrect predictions, especially for anomalous logs under severe class imbalance, creating a critical reliability gap for operational monitoring systems even when conventional calibration metrics appear good.

Method: LoRD (Log Reconstruction and Distance) is a lightweight post-hoc calibration framework that learns prediction-route-specific reliability models from latent representations of correctly classified validation samples. It estimates prediction reliability through route-wise reconstruction distances and selectively recalibrates high-risk predictions based on the estimated reliability, suppressing overconfident errors while preserving reliable predictions.

Result: Extensive experiments on four large-scale log benchmark datasets with multiple language model-based detectors show that LoRD consistently improves confidence reliability and substantially reduces overconfident anomaly-related errors without sacrificing anomaly detection performance.

Conclusion: LoRD effectively addresses the calibration gap in log anomaly detection by providing reliable confidence estimates through prediction-route-specific reliability modeling and selective recalibration, making it suitable for operational monitoring systems where reliable confidence estimation is critical.

Abstract: Online log anomaly detection is critical for maintaining the reliability of large-scale computing systems. Although recent language model-based log anomaly detectors achieve strong detection performance, their confidence estimates remain poorly calibrated. We show that these detectors frequently assign excessive confidence to incorrect predictions, particularly for anomalous logs under severe class imbalance. Moreover, confidence on erroneous predictions remains persistently high even when conventional calibration metrics indicate good calibration, creating a critical reliability gap for operational monitoring systems. To address this issue, we propose Log Reconstruction and Distance (LoRD), a lightweight post-hoc calibration framework for reliable log anomaly detection. LoRD learns prediction-route-specific reliability models from latent representations of correctly classified validation samples and estimates prediction reliability through route-wise reconstruction distances. Based on the estimated reliability, LoRD selectively recalibrates high-risk predictions to suppress overconfident errors while preserving reliable predictions. Extensive experiments on four large-scale log benchmark datasets and multiple language model-based detectors demonstrate that LoRD consistently improves confidence reliability and substantially reduces overconfident anomaly-related errors without sacrificing anomaly detection performance.

</details>


### [138] [Evaluating and improving crop-yield forecasting methods during extreme drought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17971)
*Shrey Gupta,Yi Ming,George Mohler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep learning models outperform traditional ML for extreme drought year corn yield forecasting despite feature distribution dissimilarity, though ML models benefit more from sample weighting and feature selection modifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Climate variability impacts food production, necessitating reliable forecasting models for crop yield prediction. Extreme drought events like the 2012 Midwestern US drought test model limits and challenge prediction under conditions outside historical observations.

Method: Used 16 meteorological drivers to compare ML (non-deep learning) and deep learning (VITA) models for county-level corn yield prediction in 2012 drought year. Applied sample weighting and feature selection to address training-test feature distribution dissimilarity and spatial-temporal sparsity issues.

Result: Deep learning model VITA outperformed ML models regardless of modifications. Sample weighting and feature selection improved ML models but showed little to no improvement for VITA. The study revealed challenges of dissimilar feature distributions between training and test data for extreme events.

Conclusion: While deep learning models excel at extreme event forecasting, modifications like sample weighting and feature selection are more effective for traditional ML models. The research highlights the importance of addressing feature distribution dissimilarity in agricultural forecasting for climate extremes.

Abstract: The impact of climate variability on food production has led to the creation of various forecasting models that uses machine learning (ML), numerical weather predictors (NWP) or a hybrid of ML-NWP models to identify structural and physical relationships between meteorological drivers and crop growth, in order to predict crop yield. Droughts, for example the 2012 Midwestern US (Corn Belt) drought, are extreme events that affect crop production and test the limits of these forecasting models. Using 16 meteorological drivers as predictors, we compare ML (non-deep learning) and deep learning forecasting models to predict the county-level corn yield for the extreme drought year, 2012. This forecasting problem is characterized by a dissimilarity between the feature distributions of the training and test data, where the meteorological conditions of the extreme drought year fall outside the range of historically observed values. Additionally, the dataset consists of spatial and temporal irregularities where counties with missing yields introduce spatial sparsity and the use of only a subset of daily values per year introduce temporal sparsity. To overcome this, we use sample weighting and feature selection as modifications to improve our forecasting models. These modifications lead to an improvement for ML models; however, the deep learning model VITA shows little to no improvement. While VITA outperforms the ML models with or without modifications, our current study sheds light on the effect of dissimilarity between train and test feature distributions on forecasting models, compares deep learning versus non-deep learning models, and introduces modifications that are effective for non-deep learning models.

</details>


### [139] [Recirculation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17981)
*Michael C. Mozer,Shoaib Ahmed Siddiqui,Danny Sawyer,Sunny Sanyal,Rosanne Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recirculation is an inference-time architectural enhancement for foundation models that adds recurrence to track belief states, reducing perplexity and boosting accuracy without extra latency or model weight changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fundamental limitation that state updates in feedforward transformers are bounded by model depth motivates the need for a technique that allows models to track belief states dynamically without the drawbacks of chain-of-thought computation or expensive recurrent transformer training.

Method: Recirculation introduces a specific form of recurrence that enables the model to act as a dynamical system for belief state tracking. An adaptive variant requires only light hyperparameter tuning while freezing original model weights, using serial processing in the prefill phase with essentially no additional generation latency.

Result: Adaptive recirculation achieves 23% reduction in perplexity on a suite of datasets, 21% increase in accuracy on GSM8k, and reliable improvements in accuracy on other downstream tasks with the Gemma3 family, all without changing model weights.

Conclusion: This training-free approach demonstrates that leveraging the model itself to inform architectural modifications offers a promising route to architectural evolution guided by trained network properties rather than arbitrary design choices.

Abstract: We describe an inference-time architectural enhancement for off-the-shelf foundation models that markedly reduces perplexity and boosts accuracy across generation and reasoning tasks. Our approach incurs essentially no additional latency during generation, though it requires serial processing in the prefill phase. Motivated by the fundamental limitation that state updates in feedforward transformers are bounded by model depth, our technique, recirculation, introduces a specific form of recurrence that allows the model to act as a dynamical system and track belief states. We distinguish this technique from chain-of-thought computation---which is better reserved for complex inferences rather than basic state tracking---as well as from popular depth-recurrence techniques (looping) and the costly training of recurrent transformers. We also propose and evaluate an adaptive variant of recirculation which requires only light tuning of hyperparameters while freezing the original model weights. Relative to the off-the-shelf baseline, adaptive recirculation achieves remarkable gains on the Gemma3 family, including a 23% reduction in perplexity on a suite of datasets, a 21% increase in accuracy on GSM8k, and reliable improvements in accuracy on other downstream tasks. Our training-free approach succeeds by leveraging the model itself to inform architectural modifications, suggesting a route to architectural evolution guided by a trained network's properties rather than forced, arbitrary design choices.

</details>


### [140] [Composing Flow-Matching Energies with Known Physics: Generation, OOD Detection, and Inversion on PDE Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18004)
*Yixuan Sun,Anirban Samaddar,Sandeep Madireddy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Flow matching models with potential-induced velocity provide explicit energy functions for physical field modeling, enabling improved sampling, OOD detection, and inverse problem solving without variational approximations or MCMC training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Probabilistic modeling of physical fields needs both data-driven priors and known physical structure (governing equations). Energy-based models (EBMs) are natural for this due to additive energy composition, but have been difficult to train and sample from due to intractable partition functions.

Method: Use flow matching models with potential-induced velocity that yield explicit scalar energy functions at all transport times. The energy gradient equals the learned score and recovers marginal negative log-density at optimum. Time-dependent energies are obtained from matching regression objective on independent linear Gaussian interpolation without variational approximations or additional MCMC training steps.

Result: The explicit energy enables: 1) Energy-corrected data generation, 2) Energy as OOD detection scoring function, 3) Energy compositional posterior sampling for inverse problems. Showed explicit energy permits general MCMC samplers in predictor-corrector framework, reducing PDE residual and spectral distance compared to flow ODE baseline. Demonstrated combining data energy and physics-based energy improves OOD detection accuracy.

Conclusion: Flow matching with potential-induced velocity provides tractable energy functions that bridge data-driven priors and physical constraints, overcoming traditional EBM limitations while enabling improved sampling, OOD detection, and inverse problem solving through energy composition.

Abstract: Probabilistic modeling of physical fields benefits from both a data-driven prior and known physical structure such as the governing equations. Energy-based models (EBMs) are a natural fit since energies compose additively, which enables augmenting physics information during inference. However, EBMs have been difficult to train and sample from due to the intractable partition function. We show in this work that flow matching models with a potential-induced velocity yield an explicit scalar energy at all transport times, whose gradient is exactly the converted learned score and which recovers the marginal negative log-density at the population optimum. The time-dependent energy functions are obtained purely from the matching regression objective on an independent linear Gaussian interpolation, without a variational form or additional MCMC steps, and the sampling retains the flow ODE. Access to the energy function from a trained model serves three roles: energy-corrected data generation, energy as a scoring function for out-of-distribution (OOD) detection, and energy compositional posterior sampling for inverse problems. In particular, we show the explicit energy permits general MCMC samplers in the predictor-corrector sampling framework, reducing PDE residual and spectral distance compared to the flow ODE baseline. Furthermore, we demonstrate utilizing the data energy and physics-based energy (e.g., PDE residuals) as complementary mechanisms to improve detection accuracy for OOD tasks. In addition, we explore the connection to MCMC-based inference for inverse problems by composing the energy with a quadratic observational likelihood that yields a posterior energy, used as an explicitly chosen family of inference-time targets.

</details>


### [141] [Policy-Invariant Reward Shaping from LLM Feedback: A Framework for Hybrid RL Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18008)
*Christophe D. Hounwanou,John Emeka Eze,Yaé U. Gaba*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM-derived reward signals can be formalized as bounded potential functions in Goal-Augmented MDPs, preserving optimal policies even with inaccurate LLM scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the lack of theoretical grounding for LLM-derived reward signals in hybrid LLM-RL systems. While combining large language models with reinforcement learning is increasingly common, the theoretical status of these LLM-generated rewards is often unclear or left implicit.

Method: Formalizes the hybrid LLM-planner and RL-controller architecture as a Goal-Augmented Markov Decision Process (GA-MDP). Shows that when LLM per-state progress scores are used as bounded potential functions, the resulting shaping term preserves the optimal policy set. Verifies results numerically on a small MDP under four potential configurations, including adversarial cases scaled to twenty times the base reward magnitude.

Result: The approach provides stronger theoretical guarantees than general LLM-as-reward methods. When LLM scores are bounded and used as potential functions, optimal policies are preserved even when LLM scores are inaccurate. Numerical verification confirms this theoretical result works even under adversarial conditions.

Conclusion: The theoretical framework shows that LLM-derived potential functions can safely guide RL agents while preserving optimality guarantees, providing a more robust foundation for hybrid LLM-RL architectures than naive reward-shaping approaches.

Abstract: Combining large language models with reinforcement learning is increasingly explored, yet the theoretical status of LLM-derived reward signals is often left implicit. We formalize the hybrid LLM-planner and RL-controller architecture as a Goal-Augmented Markov Decision Process and show that when the LLM per-state progress score is used as a bounded potential function, the resulting shaping term preserves the optimal policy set even when the LLM scores are inaccurate. This guarantee is stronger than what general LLM-as-reward approaches provide. We verify the result numerically on a small MDP under four potential configurations, including an adversarial one scaled to twenty times the base reward magnitude.

</details>


### [142] [Revisiting WEASEL 2.0: Reproduction, Sensitivity, and an Adaptive Ensemble-Size Rule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18021)
*Cian Higgins,Gerard Carrigan,Pinar Sungu Isiacik,Georgiana Ifrim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WEASEL 2.0 time series classifier's hyperparameter thresholds are empirically tested; an adaptive ensemble size rule is proposed that reduces memory/time usage without significant accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original WEASEL 2.0 paper used simple thresholding rules for maximum ensemble size and maximum window size without empirical justification, raising questions about whether these thresholds are optimal or necessary.

Method: Reproduced WEASEL 2.0 on 114 UCR datasets to validate published results, then systematically tested sensitivity of four design choices: downstream classifier, feature weighting absence, maximum window-size rule, and maximum ensemble-size rule. Proposed adaptive rule that sets maximum ensemble size based on series length and number of classes.

Result: Successfully reproduced WEASEL 2.0 results (mean accuracy 0.865, median 0.928, p=0.655). First three design choices robust to perturbation, but maximum ensemble-size rule over-provisions for long-series datasets. Adaptive rule reduces peak fit memory (median 37 MB, mean 395 MB) and fit time (median 0.4s, mean 4s) with minimal accuracy impact (median 0%, mean -0.11%).

Conclusion: The adaptive ensemble size rule is empirically justified and provides significant computational savings, especially for long-series datasets, without compromising classification accuracy, making WEASEL 2.0 more practical for real-world applications.

Abstract: WEASEL 2.0 is a dictionary-based time series classifier that combines dilated sliding windows with a randomised hyperparameter ensemble and a fixed-size dense feature representation. Two of its hyperparameter choices, the maximum ensemble size and the maximum window size, are specified by simple thresholding rules whose chosen thresholds are not empirically justified in the original paper. In this work we reproduce WEASEL 2.0 on 114 UCR datasets, achieving a mean accuracy of 0.865 and median of 0.928, closely matching the published values (Wilcoxon signed-rank, p = 0.655). We then test the sensitivity of four design choices: the downstream classifier, the absence of feature weighting, the maximum window-size rule, and the maximum ensemble-size rule. The first three are robust to perturbation. The fourth is over-provisioned for long-series datasets, motivating an adaptive rule that sets the maximum ensemble size from series length and number of classes. Evaluated on fixed-length datasets, the adaptive rule reduces peak fit memory by a median of 37 MB (mean 395 MB) and fit time by a median of 0.4 s (mean 4 s), with a median accuracy change of 0% (mean -0.11%). Memory and time savings concentrate on long-series datasets where the original rule allocates the largest ensemble size.

</details>


### [143] [Why GPT-Style Models Do Not Directly Transfer to Symbolic Music: Compression in the Wrong Coordinate System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18025)
*Yi Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GPT-style tokenization works by creating coordinate systems where musical regularities become predictively compressible, not just by finding larger reusable combinations. Effective tokenization balances predictive effectiveness and relational losslessness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current symbolic music tokenizations focus on finding reusable musical units (chords, motifs, phrases) similar to linguistic tokens, but this approach overlooks the fundamental importance of predictive compressibility - finding coordinate systems where musical facts become predictively regular.

Method: The paper introduces the Effectiveness-Losslessness Framework with two principles: 1) Predictive Effectiveness Principle defines Fact-Token boundaries through decoupling and denesting, 2) Relational Losslessness Principle defines Token-State boundaries that preserve contextual freedom. The framework is validated through controlled symbolic-music experiments.

Result: Experiments show that effective coordinate construction improves predictive compressibility, while fixed relational projections constrain contextual modeling. Sequence compaction alone doesn't guarantee predictive compression, and preserving contextual freedom enables higher-order musical organization to emerge without explicit structural labels.

Conclusion: GPT-style model architectures transfer across modalities, but tokenization interfaces don't. Effective tokenization must discover representations that expose predictive regularities while preserving the relational freedom from which contextual structure can emerge naturally.

Abstract: GPT-style models achieve strong performance by representing language with finite vocabularies of reusable discrete tokens. This success has motivated symbolic music tokenizations to treat recurring musical structures, such as chords, motifs, and phrases, as reusable units analogous to linguistic tokens. However, tokenization derives its advantage not from reusable combinations alone, but from compression: effective compression requires coordinates in which recurring regularities form stable and predictable conditional distributions. The key problem is therefore not to find larger musical combinations, but to discover the coordinate system in which musical facts become predictively compressible. We formulate the Effectiveness--Losslessness Framework and define tokenization as the construction of a predictively effective and relationally lossless coordinate system. The Predictive Effectiveness Principle defines the Fact--Token Boundary: decoupling and denesting construct coordinate interfaces that expose predictive regularities. The Relational Losslessness Principle defines the Token--State Boundary: tokenization stops before context-dependent relations are fixed, leaving their computation to model states. Controlled symbolic-music experiments validate these boundaries. Effective coordinate construction improves predictive compressibility, while fixed relational projections constrain contextual modeling. Sequence compaction alone does not guarantee predictive compression, while preserving contextual freedom allows higher-order musical organization to emerge without explicit structural labels. These results reveal why GPT-style models do not transfer directly across modalities: architectures transfer, but tokenization interfaces do not. Tokenization must discover effective representations while preserving the relational freedom from which contextual structure can emerge.

</details>


### [144] [TabNSM: Neural Sparse Mixer for Tabular Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18026)
*Ali Eslamian,Qiang Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TabNSM is a scalable deep regression framework for tabular data that combines sparse interaction modeling, structured regression objectives, and difficulty-aware sampling to achieve strong performance on high-dimensional datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for large-scale tabular regression have limitations: tree-based models lack end-to-end representation learning, while deep models have high complexity and sensitivity to noisy/redundant features. There's a need for a scalable deep learning approach that can handle high-dimensional tabular data efficiently.

Method: TabNSM extends sparse-attention and mixer architectures with three key components: 1) Adaptive Sparse Interaction Module (ASIM) for feature discovery and sparse local interaction encoding, 2) Multi-Stage Regression Head for progressive refinement, 3) GridLoss (ordinal-aware soft-binning objective), and 4) RISE (difficulty-aware sampling based on loss quantiles).

Result: On nine real-world regression benchmarks, TabNSM delivers strong predictive performance and practical scalability, with particularly consistent gains on high-dimensional and heterogeneous datasets.

Conclusion: Selective interaction modeling, structured regression supervision, and difficulty-aware sampling provide an effective and scalable approach to deep tabular regression, addressing the limitations of both tree-based and traditional deep models.

Abstract: Large-scale, high-dimensional tabular regression remains challenging: tree-based models are robust but lack end-to-end representation learning, while deep models enable flexible feature learning but often incur costly interaction modeling and sensitivity to noisy or redundant features. We propose TabNSM, a scalable regression framework that extends our earlier sparse-attention and mixer architectures. At its core, the Adaptive Sparse Interaction Module (ASIM) integrates foreground feature discovery, sparse local interaction encoding, and Feature-Token Mixing, providing near-linear complexity under fixed sparse configurations. For regression, TabNSM introduces three complementary components: a Multi-Stage Regression Head for progressive prediction refinement; GridLoss, an ordinal-aware soft-binning objective that incorporates target structure into representation learning; and RISE (Reweighted Instance Sampling by Error), a difficulty-aware sampling strategy based on loss-quantile bins. Across nine real-world regression benchmarks, TabNSM delivers strong predictive performance and practical scalability, with particularly consistent gains on high-dimensional and heterogeneous datasets. These results demonstrate that selective interaction modeling, structured regression supervision, and difficulty-aware sampling provide an effective and scalable approach to deep tabular regression.

</details>


### [145] [Optimize Your Sampling: Tuned Diffusion Sampling with Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18040)
*Travis Zhang,Christian Belardi,Justin Lovelace,Jin Peng Zhou,Saebyeol Shin,Carla P. Gomes,Kilian Q. Weinberger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OYS is a black-box Bayesian optimization method for selecting diffusion model sampling timesteps that directly optimizes quality metrics, achieving better performance than default schedules and other optimization methods while reducing computational costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling from diffusion models is computationally expensive due to many forward passes through large neural networks. While efficient solvers and samplers have been studied, timestep selection has received little attention, and previous optimization approaches use theoretical surrogates rather than direct quality metrics.

Method: OYS treats timestep selection as a black-box optimization problem using Bayesian optimization to directly optimize the target quality metric (rather than theoretical surrogates). The method requires no additional training and is applicable even to distilled models.

Result: OYS outperforms default schedules and Align Your Steps on text-to-image generation, improves over default schedules on inpainting and other image tasks, works with both simple (Euler) and sophisticated (DPM-Solver++) samplers. A 5-step OYS schedule retains 89%-94% of the quality of a 50-step schedule while reducing inference cost by 10x.

Conclusion: OYS provides an effective black-box optimization approach for timestep selection in diffusion models that directly optimizes quality metrics, significantly reducing computational costs while maintaining high sample quality across various tasks and samplers.

Abstract: Sampling from a diffusion model typically requires many forward passes through a large neural network, making generation computationally expensive. While much work has focused on efficient solvers and samplers, comparatively little attention has been paid to selecting the sampling timesteps themselves. A recent line of work optimizes theoretically derived surrogates for sample quality rather than the quality metric itself. We propose Optimizing Your Sampling (OYS), which instead treats timestep selection as a black-box optimization problem, optimizing the target metric directly with Bayesian optimization. OYS outperforms both the default schedules and those of Align Your Steps on text-to-image generation, and improves over the default schedules on inpainting and other image tasks, in both quantitative and human evaluations. OYS requires no additional training, is applicable even to distilled models, and improves both simple and sophisticated samplers such as Euler and DPM-Solver++. A 5-step OYS schedule retains 89%-94% of the quality of a 50-step schedule while reducing inference cost by 10x.

</details>


### [146] [The concentration game: Bayesian updating, regret, and information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18061)
*Akshay Balsubramani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a unified information-theoretic framework for analyzing regret in repeated games, showing that Bayesian updating, exponential weights algorithms, and concentration phenomena all emerge from a single two-player game formulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a unified understanding of various learning algorithms (Bayesian updating, exponential weights, bandits, boosting) and concentration phenomena through a single information-theoretic game-theoretic framework.

Method: A two-player zero-sum repeated game between a learner and nature, where the terminal payoff measures the comparator's gain at fixed relative entropy from the prior, and nature operates under an information budget constraint given the learner's mixed action.

Result: Shows that Gibbs/Bayes weights emerge uniquely as the Bellman equalizer strategy, with an exact three-part regret decomposition: per-round information loss, additive retempering drift, and comparator's information relative to prior.

Conclusion: Provides a unified information-theoretic framework that explains diverse learning algorithms and concentration bounds through a single regret decomposition, replacing traditional quadratic-variation surrogates with an exact information-theoretic ledger.

Abstract: We give a two-player zero-sum repeated game between a learner and nature whose value identity generates Bayesian updating and an exact accounting of exponential-weights regret at once, and supplies the comparator-class variational form that a wide class of concentration phenomena share. The terminal payoff is the most a comparator can gain at fixed relative entropy from the prior, and the one-step constraint is an information budget on nature's move under the learner's mixed action. With the learner's move otherwise unrestricted, Gibbs/Bayes weights emerge as its unique Bellman equalizer -- the mixed action that makes the per-round loss independent of which direction nature moves -- with log-partition functions playing the role of value functions. The regret decomposes exactly into three parts: a per-round information loss reflecting the variation in observed outcomes, an additive retempering drift that accounts exactly for any change of measurement scale between rounds, and the information the comparator carries relative to the prior. The variance and bounded-range proxies that drive standard regret bounds are looser relaxations of this decomposition, which holds generally and governs them all. Both players' strategies are read off from the decomposition term by term, and repeated play yields an information-theoretic ledger of self-play in place of the usual quadratic-variation surrogate. The same comparator-class geometry accounts for the classical large-deviation bounds, and methods across bandits, posterior sampling, aggregation, and boosting are specializations of the one regret decomposition.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [147] [WONDER: A Radio World Model-based Negotiation Framework for Multi-Agent UAV Coverage Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16955)
*Jiahao Huang,Rongpeng Li,Zhifeng Zhao,Guoru Ding,Honggang Zhang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: WONDER framework uses radio world models and multi-round negotiation for distributed UAV swarm coverage restoration after disasters, outperforming existing methods while maintaining full UAV connectivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-disaster damage to terrestrial infrastructure disrupts wireless coverage, and UAV swarms offer a promising solution for rapid restoration. However, there's a significant gap between locally visible movement choices and swarm-level coverage outcomes due to limitations in local geometry observations, hidden radio impact, and inter-UAV communication.

Method: WONDER uses a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA)-based radio world model to predict incremental radio effects of candidate trajectories from deployment-available information. It employs multi-round negotiation coordinating ranked proposals by committing one trajectory at a time and re-evaluating remaining proposals under updated context. The framework also adopts Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)-style Actor and alternates between updating the world model and the actor.

Result: Experiments in RadioDynamics simulation environment (62 metropolitan scenes) show WONDER achieves highest balanced score among seven evaluated methods, reaching 0.870 with a 0.162 coverage advantage over STACCA while maintaining 100% connectivity between UAVs.

Conclusion: The WONDER framework effectively bridges the gap between local observations and swarm-level coverage outcomes through radio world modeling and distributed negotiation, providing a promising solution for UAV swarm-based wireless coverage restoration in post-disaster scenarios.

Abstract: Post-disaster damage to terrestrial infrastructure can disrupt wireless coverage,while Uncrewed Aerial Vehicle (UAV) swarms provide a promising solution for rapid restoration.However, due to the limitations in local geometry observations hidden radio impact,and inter-UAV communication,there exists a significant gap between locally visible movement choices and swarm-level coverage outcomes.To combat this gap,we propose a raido World-model-based Optimized Negotiation framework for Distributed UAV covERage (WONDER).Particularly, to tackle the unavailability of the future radio field from onboard observations, WONDER uses a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture (JEPA)-based radio world model to learn and predict the incremental radio effect of each candidate trajectory from deployment-available information.Multi-round negotiation in WONDER then coordinates ranked proposals by committing one trajectory at a time and re-evaluating the remaining proposals under the updated context. Our theoretical analyses further validate the effectiveness of such a world model-based framework. WONDER also adopts a Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)-style Actor and alternates between updating the world model and the actor. Furthermore,we build RadioDynamics,a comprehensive simulation environment that integrates UAV mobility,radio propagation, inter-UAV communication modeling,and digital-twin geometry with ray-traced fields in $62$ metropolitan scenes.Experiments on $11$ testing scenes in RadioDynamics show that WONDER achieves the highest balanced score among seven evaluated methods,reaching $0.870$ with a $0.162$ coverage advantage over STACCA, while maintaining $100\%$ connectivity between UAVs.

</details>


### [148] [Adaptive Incentive Design in Dynamic Principal-Agent Problem via Kernelized Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17614)
*Arghya Mallick,Anuj S. Vora,Sergio Grammatico,Peyman Mohajerin Esfahani*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Introduces a stochastic utility model for dynamic principal-agent problems to restore continuity, enabling efficient contract design via a heteroscedastic bandit algorithm with O(√T(log T)^{m+1}) regret, applied to V2G incentive design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dynamic principal-agent literature assumes deterministic agent utility, causing discontinuity in principal's expected utility and forcing computationally intractable discretizations of contract space. Realistic subsystems have inherent physical and behavioral variations that aren't captured by deterministic models.

Method: Introduces stochastic counterpart into agent's utility model to capture realistic variations, proving this restores continuity. Formulates interaction as structured multi-armed bandit with heteroscedastic noise. Proposes Heteroscedastic GP-UCB algorithm with Neural Network (Arcsin) kernel to capture non-stationary, sigmoidal utility geometry.

Result: Establishes high-probability cumulative regret bound of O(√T(log T)^{m+1}) for m-dimensional compact contract space. Demonstrates practical efficacy by formulating Vehicle-to-Grid (V2G) incentive design problem, proving its equivalence to dynamic principal-agent problem, and showing superior economic performance for grid aggregators.

Conclusion: The stochastic utility model successfully overcomes the fundamental bottleneck of discontinuity in principal-agent problems, enabling computationally efficient contract design with theoretical guarantees and practical applications in real-world problems like V2G incentive systems.

Abstract: We consider the dynamic principal-agent problem under asymmetric information, wherein a principal sequentially designs contracts to incentivize an agent with unknown preferences and hidden actions. A fundamental bottleneck in the existing literature is the assumption of deterministic agent utility, which renders the principal's expected utility discontinuous and forces computationally intractable discretizations of the contract space. In this paper, we address this limitation by introducing a stochastic counterpart into the agent's utility model, capturing the inherent physical and behavioral variations in realistic subsystems. We formally prove that this stochastic formulation restores the continuity of the principal's expected utility. Leveraging this continuous geometric structure, we formulate the interaction as a structured multi-armed bandit problem subject to heteroscedastic noise. We propose a \texttt{Heteroscedastic GP-UCB} algorithm that utilizes a Neural Network (Arcsin) kernel, chosen to capture the non-stationary, sigmoidal geometry of the utility landscape. For an $m$-dimensional compact contract space, we establish a high-probability cumulative regret bound of $O\left(\sqrt{T}(\log T)^{m+1}\right)$. Finally, we demonstrate the practical efficacy of our theoretical framework by formulating the Vehicle-to-Grid (V2G) incentive design problem, proving its equivalence to a dynamic principal-agent problem, and showing superior economic performance for grid aggregators.

</details>


### [149] [Offline Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning with a Physics-Informed World Model for Cooperative Mixed Traffic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17739)
*Lu Liu,Chi Xie,Xi Xiong*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A physics-informed world model-based offline multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for CAV cooperative control at highway bottlenecks using local observations only, with physics-based supervision and uncertainty handling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable cooperative control of connected and automated vehicles at partially observable highway bottlenecks in mixed traffic without requiring complete global traffic states or online trial-and-error, which are often impractical or unsafe in real-world scenarios.

Method: Physics-informed world model-based offline MARL framework that reconstructs global traffic state from local CAV observation-action histories, uses coupled macroscopic-microscopic traffic dynamics for physics supervision, probabilistic ensemble world model for transitions/rewards, and multi-step imagined rollouts with pessimistic rewards and uncertainty-driven truncation for offline policy learning.

Result: Experiments in SUMO-based on-ramp bottleneck using ~1 million offline transitions show physics supervision improves state reconstruction and world-model prediction accuracy compared to approaches without physics guidance.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully enables effective CAV cooperative control in partially observable mixed traffic scenarios without needing complete global information or online exploration, with physics-informed learning enhancing state reconstruction and model accuracy.

Abstract: This study investigates cooperative control of connected and automated vehicles (CAVs) at partially observable highway bottlenecks in mixed traffic, aiming to mitigate congestion without relying on complete global traffic states or online trial-and-error. We propose a physics-informed world model-based offline multi-agent reinforcement learning framework that reconstructs a physically interpretable global traffic state from local CAV observation-action histories, with coupled macroscopic-microscopic traffic dynamics providing physics-based supervision. A probabilistic ensemble world model learns traffic-state transitions and system rewards, while model disagreement quantifies epistemic uncertainty. Multi-step imagined rollouts with pessimistic rewards and uncertainty-driven truncation are then used for offline policy learning. Experiments in a SUMO-based on-ramp bottleneck using approximately $1\times10^6$ offline transitions show that physics supervision improves state reconstruction and world-model prediction accuracy.

</details>


### [150] [A Theoretical Framework for Parallel Lifelong MAPF Using Group Decentralized Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17928)
*Alex DeWeese,Jiaoyang Li,Guannan Qu*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper introduces Group Decentralized RHCR (GD-RHCR), an extended framework that partitions agents for parallel planning to reduce computational costs in Lifelong Multi-Agent Path Finding while maintaining near-optimal performance guarantees similar to RHCR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While RHCR (Rolling-Horizon Collision Resolution) is widely regarded as a high-performing solution for L-MAPF (Lifelong Multi-Agent Path Finding), its computational cost limits applicability to modest agent counts. There's a need for more scalable solutions that maintain solution quality.

Method: First theoretically proves near-optimality of RHCR using Locally Interdependent Multi-Agent MDP literature. Then extends this to propose GD-RHCR (Group Decentralized RHCR), which incorporates a group decentralized structure that partitions agents based on transitive communication schemes and plans for each partition in parallel.

Result: Shows that both RHCR and GD-RHCR achieve similar exponentially close to optimal guarantees, establishing theoretical duality between time-based restrictions (vanilla RHCR) and space-based partitioning (GD-RHCR). Demonstrates that GD-RHCR attains high throughput that scales to higher agent counts while maintaining significantly lower per-plan computational cost across varying maps.

Conclusion: GD-RHCR provides a scalable extension to RHCR that maintains theoretical guarantees while dramatically reducing computational costs, making lifelong multi-agent path finding more practical for larger numbers of agents through a clever combination of temporal and spatial decomposition approaches.

Abstract: In the Lifelong Multi-Agent Path Finding (L-MAPF) problem, agents must repeatedly move from one destination to another while avoiding obstacles and inter-agent collisions. Widely regarded as one of the highest-performing solutions to this problem is the Rolling-Horizon Collision Resolution (RHCR) framework. However, commensurate with its quality solutions, it incurs a computational cost that limits its applicability to even modest agent counts. In this paper, leveraging theoretical methods from the Locally Interdependent Multi-Agent MDP literature, we first theoretically prove the near-optimality of RHCR in a discounted MDP formulation of the L-MAPF problem. Then, we leverage these results to naturally motivate an extended framework called Group Decentralized RHCR (GD-RHCR) which incorporates a group decentralized structure that partitions agents based on a transitive communication scheme and plans for each partition of agents in parallel. We show that both RHCR and GD-RHCR achieve similar exponentially close to optimal guarantees, establishing a theoretical duality between the time based restrictions performed by vanilla RHCR and the additional space based partitioning performed by GD-RHCR. Lastly, we show that across varying maps, GD-RHCR is able to attain high throughput that scales into higher agent counts while maintaining a significantly lower per plan cost.

</details>


### [151] [Collective Counterfactual Planning: Coordination, Consent, and Verification under Representational Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17932)
*Chainarong Amornbunchornvej*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A formal model (CCP) where agents have limited representational subspaces in a common task space, analyzing team solvability through four gates: implementation coalitions, conception, consent, and verification qualification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Groups complete complex projects that no individual can handle alone, but existing models focus on capability/knowledge limitations rather than representational geometry limitations where each agent perceives/conceives through agent-specific subspaces.

Method: Formal model of Collective Counterfactual Planning (CCP) with agents having projection onto agent-specific subspaces; defines four gates for team solvability, analyzes Collective Counterfactual Solvability (CCS) problem separating geometric feasibility, executable attainment, and validated completion.

Result: Iterated cross-agent relay can unlock solutions not in one-shot pooling; subspace-dark goals are unverifiable even if accidentally attained; memoryless vs audited consent constrain different objects; four-step horizon-bounded scheme is sound and complete under exact representation.

Conclusion: The CCP model provides a geometry for sequential mutual enabling, executing invisible-purpose steps, forced sub-teaming at expertise boundaries, and completion that cannot be validly declared, exposing fundamental limits of collective planning.

Abstract: Groups routinely complete projects that no single member can plan, execute, or verify alone. We propose a formal model of this phenomenon, Collective Counterfactual Planning (CCP), in which the binding limitation on each agent is neither capability, knowledge, nor observability, but representational geometry: each agent perceives the state, conceives moves, consents to actions, and certifies goal requirements only through a projection onto an agent-specific subspace of a common task space. Four gates jointly determine whether a team can reach a conjunctive goal and legitimately recognize that it has done so: the exogenous implementation coalitions required to perform each action, together with three representational gates -- conception, consent, and task-relative verification qualification. We define the Collective Counterfactual Solvability (CCS) problem, separating geometric feasibility, executable attainment, and validated completion. The results expose a positive-negative duality. Iterated cross-agent relay can unlock a solution that no one-shot pooling of individual plans contains, but any goal requirement depending essentially on the subspace dark to the entire team is unverifiable and therefore not validly completable, even when the trajectory accidentally attains it. Memoryless and audited consent further constrain different objects -- action directions versus cumulative trajectory states -- and neither dominates the other. A four-step exhaustive horizon-bounded solvability scheme is sound and complete under exact representation of the relay closure; restricted implementations remain sound on returned plans but need not be complete. The model gives one geometry for sequential mutual enabling, competent execution of steps whose purpose is invisible to the executor, forced sub-teaming at expertise boundaries, and completion that cannot be validly declared.

</details>
